Boltzmann–Grad limit for the inelastic Lorentz gas: Part I. Existence, uniqueness, and rigorous derivation via weak convergence
Théophile Dolmaire, Alessia Nota

Abstract
In this paper we provide a rigorous derivation of the inelastic linear Boltzmann equation, in the Boltzmann-Grad limit, from a dissipative, random, Lorentz gas in arbitrary dimensions d ≥ 2 d\geq 2 . Specifically, we consider a microscopic particle system where scatterers are randomly distributed according to a Poisson process, and a tagged light particle undergoes inelastic collisions with the scatterers following a reflection law characterized by a fixed restitution coefficient. We establish the existence and uniqueness of weak solutions to the inelastic linear Boltzmann equation within the class of non-negative Radon measures, assuming that the initial data has a finite exponential moment. We first show that the forward dynamics of the dissipative particle system is globally defined almost surely and then prove the weak- ∗ \ast convergence of the microscopic solution towards the weak solutions of the inelastic linear Boltzmann equation, providing an explicit rate of convergence. Furthermore, under the same initial data assumptions, we prove the existence of strong solutions to the inelastic linear Boltzmann equation, constructed via a series representation of the solutions.



Keywords:
Boltzmann–Grad Limit; Kinetic Equations;
Rigorous Derivation;
Linear Boltzmann Equation;
Lorentz Gas;
Dissipative Particle Systems;
Inelastic Collisions;
Granular Media

1
Introduction

Kinetic equations describe in terms of distribution functions the evolution of large particle systems such as gas, plasmas, or even animal or human populations in which spread of diseases or evolution of opinions can be studied. The paradigmatic example is the Boltzmann equation, where the quantity
f(t,x,v)
represents the probability to find particles of a dilute gas lying at time
t
at position
x
and moving with velocity
v
. The evolution of the distribution function is obtained from the microscopic dynamics of such a particle system, and the rigorous justification of the validity of the equation, deduced directly from the microscopic dynamics, is a task of central importance to establish solid foundations of statistical mechanics. Such a task is often referred to as the
derivation
of the kinetic equations. The rigorous derivation of kinetic equations ensures that the equations can be used in a reliable manner to study the behaviour of large particle systems, and in some cases the procedure can be refined to provide a quantitative estimation.

In the present article, we will establish the rigorous derivation of the linear inelastic Boltzmann equation in dimension
d
, with
d\geq 2
arbitrary. The equation describes the inelastic Lorentz gas, that is, the evolution of non-interacting, light particles that evolve among a background of infinitely heavy, randomly distributed scatterers, and that collide inelastically with the scatterers. Such a system describes for instance the transport of electrons in a metal. We will start from the deterministic evolution of the particles, assumed to be point-particles, among the scatterers of radius
\varepsilon>0
. The scatterers will be distributed randomly in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
according to a Poisson process of intensity
\mu_{\varepsilon}>0
. We will show that in the Boltzmann-Grad limit (that is, when
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
in such a way that
\mu_{\varepsilon}\varepsilon^{d-1}=1
, so that the mean free path remains constant, while the volume fraction, proportional to
\mu_{\varepsilon}\varepsilon^{d}
, vanishes), the distribution of a tagged particle of the microscopic system can be approximated by the associated solution to the linear inelastic Boltzmann equation, that writes:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}f(t,x,v)+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f(t,x,v)=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\frac{|v\cdot\omega|}{r^{2}}f(\hskip 0.7113pt^{\prime}\hskip-1.42262ptv)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega-C_{d}|v|f(v)

(1.1)

where
{}^{\prime}\hskip-0.7113ptv=v-\big(1+1/r\big)\big(v\cdot\omega\big)\omega
is the pre-collisional velocity of a particle that collides with a scatterer at a point such that the normal of the scatterer is
\omega\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
and such that the velocity after such a collision is
v
,
r\in[0,1]
is the
restitution coefficient
measuring the inelasticity of the collision and
C_{d}=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\big|\frac{v}{|v|}\cdot\omega\big|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega
is a positive constant that depends only on the dimension. In the case
r=1
, we recover the elastic case.

To the best of our knowledge, even if in a linear framework, our result constitutes the first rigorous derivation of a dissipative collisional equation where energy is lost over time, and, specifically, of an inelastic version of the Boltzmann equation from a microscopic particle system that evolves according to deterministic dynamics. More precisely, we prove that the solution of the microscopic inelastic Lorentz model converges in a suitable weak sense towards the solution of (
1.1
). We also emphasize that the derivation we provide is quantitative, in the sense that the error made by approximating the solution of the linear inelastic Boltzmann equation by the evolution of the light particle is explicitly given, as a power of
\varepsilon
, where
\varepsilon
is the size of the scatterers of the Lorentz gas.

State of the art on the Lorentz Gas Model.

The Lorentz gas, introduced by H. A. Lorentz in 1905 to model the motion of electrons in metals, stands as a simple but highly non-trivial model in this context. It is a rare source of exact results in kinetic theory, providing a concrete example where microscopic reversibility can be reconciled with macroscopic irreversibility. Indeed, for this system, one can prove, under suitable scaling limits, a rigorous validation of linear kinetic equations and, from this, of diffusion equations.

The Lorentz gas consists of a particle moving through infinitely heavy, randomly distributed
scatterers. The interaction between the Lorentz particle and the scatterers is specified by a central potential of finite range. Hence, the motion of the Lorentz particle is defined through the solution of Newton’s equations of motion.
In the elastic case, the original system is Hamiltonian, the only stochasticity being that of the positions of the
scatterers. This randomness is essential to obtain the correct kinetic description.

The first scaling one could consider is the Boltzmann-Grad limit (or low-density limit),
namely, when the number of collisions is small, and thus
the mean free path of the particle is macroscopic.
The initial breakthrough in this direction was achieved by Gallavotti, who derived the linear Boltzmann equation for a particle moving through a random distribution of fixed, hard scatterers
[
36
]
,
[
37
]
. This work was subsequently generalized in terms of convergence of path
measures and extended to more general scatterer distributions by Spohn
[
59
]
, see also
[
60
]
. Boldrighini, Bunimovich and Sinai
proved instead that the limiting Boltzmann equation holds for almost every scatterer configuration
drawn from a Poisson distribution
[
14
]
. We remark that these results have
been provided in cases of compactly supported potentials and provide a qualitative validation of the
linear Boltzmann equation, with no explicit control of the error in the kinetic limit. For a quantitative estimate of the error for a Lorentz Gas of hard-spheres, we
refer to
[
5
]
. For first contributions towards the open problem of the validation in the case of long-range potentials we refer to
[
23
,
3
]
and
[
55
]
.

In the nonlinear framework, the derivation of the nonlinear Boltzmann equation from a Newtonian system of hard spheres has been rigorously proven first by Lanford in
[
42
]
, in the low-density regime and for short times. Lanford’s result was later extended, among many others, in
[
35
]
(quantitative derivation),
[
57
]
(short range potential interactions),
[
25
]
,
[
44
]
(domains with boundary),
[
12
]
(study of the the fluctuations), and finally
[
22
]
(extension of Lanford’s theorem to arbitrary time intervals). For more details on the derivation of the nonlinear Boltzmann equation, the reader may refer to
[
13
]
, and the references therein.

As previously emphasized, the randomness in the distribution of scatterers plays a crucial role in deriving the linear Boltzmann equation. Indeed, in the case of a periodic setting, where heavy particles are positioned at the vertices of a lattice in Euclidean space, we face the maximum amount of correlation between the heavy particles. This significantly alters the structure of the resulting kinetic equation. The linear Boltzmann equation fails to be the correct mesoscopic description of this model (see
[
17
]
). The first complete proof of the Boltzmann-Grad limit for the periodic Lorentz gas, valid for all lattices and space dimensions, has been obtained by Marklof and Strömbergsson
[
47
]
(see also
[
48
]
). The properties of the resulting generalized linear Boltzmann equation are discussed by Caglioti and Golse in
[
18
]
.

In the weak-coupling regime, when there are very many but weak collisions, interpreted as a central limit effect, a
linear Landau equation appears. The first result in this direction was obtained by Kesten and Papanicolaou for a particle in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
subject to a weak mean zero random force field
[
40
]
. Later, Dürr, Goldstein, and Lebowitz showed that in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
the velocity process converges in distribution to the Brownian motion on a surface of constant speed for sufficiently smooth interaction potentials
[
32
]
. The linear Landau equation appears also in an intermediate scale between the low density and
the weak-coupling regime, see
[
24
]
.

The rigorous derivation of hydrodynamic equations, specifically the heat
equation, from the mechanical system given by the random Lorentz gas relies on the kinetic approximation of the microscopic dynamics, i.e. uses the kinetic equation a bridge. This approach has been used to obtain the heat equation in different contexts, see
[
4
,
5
,
11
]
, and also
[
33
]
. We additionally refer to
[
45
]
for a different approach in this direction.

Most of the mathematical results on the linear Boltzmann equation assume
that there are no additional transport terms arising from external fields. However, the presence of such fields significantly impact both the derivation of the equation in the low-density regime and the properties of its solutions. Specifically, the motion of a Lorentz particle in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
under a uniform, constant magnetic field formally leads to a generalized Boltzmann equation with memory effects (see
[
9
,
10
]
and also
[
41
]
where the model has been studied numerically). A rigorous derivation of this equation has recently been achieved in
[
56
]
. We refer also to
[
46
]
for the rigorous derivation of linear kinetic equations that include magnetic transport effects.

The linear inelastic Lorentz model and the inelastic linear Boltzmann equation.

In the case of light particles interacting inelastically with the scatterers, the question of the rigorous derivation remained open. The derivation is also lacking for the nonlinear inelastic Boltzmann equation. This is because of the singularities developed by systems of inelastic particles, the so-called
granular materials
, and by the kinetic equations describing these objects (see for instance
[
62
]
). The difficulties come on the one hand from the decay of the temperature, preventing the existence of steady states and implying the convergence towards self-similar solutions
[
53
]
. On the other hand, the particles tend to create stable clusters (hence the name of
granular
media), inducing an explosion of the gradient in the position variable or convergence towards Dirac masses at the level of the kinetic equation (
[
6
]
,
[
61
]
). In particular, the creation of clusters violates the separation of the micro- and mesoscopic scales, such a separation being a crucial ingredient to obtain the validity of kinetic equations from particle systems. At the level of the particle system, the dynamics among the clusters might degenerate so that infinitely many collisions can take place in finite time. This is the phenomenon of
inelastic collapse
(see
[
51
]
,
[
21
]
,
[
63
]
,
[
20
]
,
[
7
]
,
[
52
]
,
[
27
]
,
[
28
]
,
[
29
]
,
[
30
]
), which represents in particular a serious obstruction to perform a rigorous derivation of the nonlinear inelastic Boltzmann equation. We will address carefully this issue in the linear setting.

In the case of the linear model, studied numerically in
[
39
]
(see also
[
49
]
, in the case when an external force re-injects energy in the system, and
[
50
]
,
[
31
]
in the specific case of the Maxwell collision kernel), the decay of the temperature can be estimated from below, so that it is possible to prove that the gas does not cool down in finite time. Besides, the question of the regularity of the solutions of the linear equation remained open to the best of our knowledge. We will discuss this question, providing a natural condition to ensure the convergence of the series representation of the solutions to the linear inelastic Boltzmann equation.

For a general introduction to granular gases and the inelastic Boltzmann equation, we refer to the classical references
[
15
]
,
[
62
]
and
[
19
]
.

Formal derivation of the inelastic linear Boltzmann equation.

For the sake of completeness, in this section, we will present the usual formal arguments that enable to recover the inelastic linear Boltzmann equation, describing at the kinetic scale the evolution of a tagged particle in a background of inelastic scatterers. The Boltzmann equation is obtained formally by considering the number
f(t,x,v)
of particles at time
t
, position
x
and moving with velocity
v
, and by evaluating the change rate of
f
due to the different microscopic mechanisms that affect the dynamics of the particles. In the present case, the particles described by
f
do not interact one with another, and can collide only with the background of fixed scatterers. The variation per unit of time of the number of particles, at time
t
and at the position
x
, with a velocity
v\in A
for
A
measurable, is given by:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}f(t,x,v)\mathds{1}_{A}(v)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}v\cdot\nabla_{x}f(t,x,v)\mathds{1}_{A}(v)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\omega}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}b(|v|,\left|\frac{v}{|v|}\cdot\omega\right|)f(v)\Big[\mathds{1}_{A}(v^{\prime})-\mathds{1}_{A}(v)\Big]\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega

(1.2)

where
v^{\prime}
is the post-collisonal velocity of a particle colliding with a scatterer with impact parameter
\omega\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
and pre-collisional velocity
v
. Here,
v^{\prime}
is given by the scattering relation
v^{\prime}=\kappa_{\omega}(v)
, already defined in (
2.7
), as:

\displaystyle v^{\prime}=\kappa_{\omega}(v)=v-(1+r)\big(v\cdot\omega\big)\omega.

(1.3)

If
v^{\prime}\in A
, a collision
v\rightarrow v^{\prime}
increases the number of particles with velocity in
A
, while if
v\in A
a collision
v\rightarrow v^{\prime}
decreases the number of particles in
A
(assuming that
v
and
v^{\prime}
do not belong together to
A
, which is very likely is
A
has a small measure). All the possible collisions are considered thanks to the integration over all the possible pre-collisional velocities
v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and angular parameters
\omega\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
.

Finally, the collision kernel
b
describes the rate at which the collisions with particles with pre-collisional velocity
v
and angular parameter
\omega
take place. By Galilean invariance, such a collision kernel depends only on
|v|
and
\big|\big(v/|v|\big)\cdot\omega\big|
.

Considering an approximation of any measurable function
\varphi
by scale functions, we obtain the following weak form for the linear Boltzmann equation, in the case of a general collision kernel
b
:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}f(t,x,v)\varphi(v)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}v\cdot\nabla_{x}f(t,x,v)\varphi(v)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\omega}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}b(|v|,\left|\frac{v}{|v|}\cdot\omega\right|)f(v)\Big[\varphi(v^{\prime})-\varphi(v)\Big]\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega.

(1.4)

Denoting by
J\kappa_{\omega}
the Jacobian determinant of the scattering
\kappa_{\omega}:v\mapsto v^{\prime}
(for
\omega
fixed), we have
\big|J\kappa_{\omega}\big|=r
. Denoting by
\hskip 0.7113pt{}^{\prime}\hskip-0.7113ptv
the velocity such that
\kappa_{\omega}(\hskip 0.7113pt^{\prime}\hskip-0.7113ptv)=v
or equivalently
\hskip 0.7113pt{}^{\prime}\hskip-0.7113ptv=\kappa^{-1}_{\omega}(v)=v-(1+1/r)(v\cdot\omega)\omega
, we deduce the following strong form for the linear Boltzmann equation, for a general collision kernel
b
:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}f(t,x,v)+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f(t,x,v)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\omega}}\frac{1}{|J\kappa_{\omega}(^{\prime}v)|}b\left(|\hskip 0.7113pt^{\prime}\hskip-1.42262ptv|,\left|\frac{\hskip 0.7113pt{}^{\prime}\hskip-0.7113ptv}{|\hskip 0.7113pt^{\prime}\hskip-0.7113ptv|}\cdot\omega\right|\right)f(\hskip 0.7113pt^{\prime}\hskip-0.7113ptv)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega-f(v)\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\omega}}b\left(|v|,\left|\frac{v}{|v|}\cdot\omega\right|\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\omega}}\frac{1}{r}b\left(|\hskip 0.7113pt^{\prime}\hskip-0.7113ptv|,\left|\frac{\hskip 0.7113pt{}^{\prime}\hskip-0.7113ptv}{|\hskip 0.7113pt^{\prime}\hskip-0.7113ptv|}\cdot\omega\right|\right)f(^{\prime}\hskip-0.7113ptv)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega-f(v)\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\omega}}b\left(|v|,\left|\frac{v}{|v|}\cdot\omega\right|\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega.

(1.5)

In the specific case of
hard sphere
collisions, that we will consider in this paper, the explicit expression of the collision kernel is
b(|V|,\left|\frac{V}{|V|}\cdot\omega\right|)=|V\cdot\omega|
. Therefore, using that
|\hskip 0.7113pt^{\prime}\hskip-1.42262ptv\cdot\omega|=\frac{1}{r}|v\cdot\omega|
, the strong form of the linear Boltzmann equation for a Lorentz gas, in the case of inelastic collisions with a fixed restitution coefficient, and for the hard sphere collision kernel, is:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}f(t,x,v)+v\cdot\nabla_{x}f(t,x,v)=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\omega}}\frac{|v\cdot\omega|}{r^{2}}f(^{\prime}v)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega-f(v)\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\omega}}|v\cdot\omega|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega.

(1.6)

It is possible to simplify the expression of the loss term of the collision operator, since we have in general:

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\omega}}|\frac{v}{|v|}\cdot\omega|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\omega}}|R(e_{1})\cdot R(\sigma)|\cdot|R^{\prime}(\sigma)|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\sigma=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\omega}}|e_{1}\cdot\sigma|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\sigma,

(1.7)

where
R
is any vectorial rotation that sends the first vector of the canonical basis
e_{1}
on
v/|v|
(which is a fixed vector, since the integration variable is
\omega
). We will denote by
C_{d}
the previous integral, which depends only on the dimension
d
:

\displaystyle C_{d}=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\omega}}|e_{1}\cdot\sigma|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\sigma.

(1.8)

This allows to obtain, for a general dimension
d\geq 2
, the final expression (
1.1
) for the strong form of the inelastic linear Boltzmann equation. Notice that in dimension
2
, we have
C_{2}=\int_{0}^{2\pi}|\cos\theta|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta=4,

whereas in dimension
3
, choosing the parametrization
\sigma=(\cos\varphi\cos\theta,\sin\varphi\cos\theta,\sin\theta)
of the sphere, with
\theta\in[-\pi/2,\pi/2]
and
\varphi\in[0,2\pi]
, we find

C_{3}=\int_{0}^{2\pi}\int_{-\pi/2}^{\pi/2}|\cos\varphi\cos\theta|\cos\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\varphi=2\pi.

The difficulties concerning the derivation of the inelastic linear Boltzmann equation.

The rigorous derivation of the linear Boltzmann equation, in the elastic case, as performed originally by Gallavotti in the seminal article
[
37
]
, consists in considering the quantity:

\displaystyle f_{\varepsilon}(t,x,v)=\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\varepsilon}}\big[f_{0}\left(T^{-t}_{c,\varepsilon}(x,v)\right)\big],

(1.9)

and showing that
f_{\varepsilon}
converges, in some strong sense (pointwise, in the
L^{1}
norm, or more generally in any
L^{p}
norm), in the Boltzmann-Grad limit
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
,
\mu_{\varepsilon}\varepsilon^{d-1}=1
towards the solution of the linear Boltzmann equation with initial datum
f_{0}
.
Observe that
f_{\varepsilon}(t,x,v)
is defined by (
1.9
) as the mean value of
f_{0}\left(T^{-t}_{c,\varepsilon}(x,v)\right)
, averaged over all the distributions
c
of the scatterers, distributed according to a Poisson process in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with intensity
\mu_{\varepsilon}
.
T^{-t}_{c,\varepsilon}(x,v)
is the preimage of the point
(x,v)
of the phase space, by the dynamical flow of the tagged particle. In other words, (
1.9
) describes the probability to find the tagged particle in the configuration
(x,v)
of the phase space at time
t
, which is equivalent to consider the probability to find the tagged particle at the initial time
t=0
in the configuration
T^{-t}_{c,\varepsilon}(x,v)
.

In the case of a tagged particle colliding inelastically with the scatterers, one has to take into account the fact that the dynamical flow of the tagged particle, at
c
fixed, is contracting the measure in the phase space. Therefore, in the present case, we have to consider:

\displaystyle f_{\varepsilon}(t,x,v)=\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\varepsilon}}\big[|J\big(T^{-t}_{c,\varepsilon}(x,v)\big)|\cdot f_{0}\big(T^{-t}_{c,\varepsilon}(x,v)\big)\big],

(1.10)

where
J\big(T^{-t}_{c,\varepsilon}(x,v)\big)
denotes the Jacobian determinant of
T^{-t}_{c,\varepsilon}
, with respect to the
(x,v)
variables. This Jacobian determinant can be computed explicitly, for example relying on the Transport-Collision-Transport formula introduced in
[
26
]
.

We further observe that, in the inelastic case, we are facing an additional major difficulty. More precisely, if the intensity
\mu_{\varepsilon}
of the Poisson process satisfies the Boltzmann-Grad limit, that is, is scaled as
\mu_{\varepsilon}\varepsilon^{d-1}=1
, the mean free path of the tagged particle is a constant independent of
\varepsilon
. But in the case of inelastic collisions, the velocity of the tagged particle is increasing at each collision when the dynamics is considered backward in time. More precisely, the norm of the velocity will grow geometrically with a positive probability, leading to a sequence of infinite collisions in finite time, which prevents us from considering directly the inverse
T_{c,\varepsilon}^{-t}
of the dynamical flow.

To overcome this difficulty, we will rely on an approach based on the weak formulation of the linear inelastic Boltzmann equation. This approach is the analog of the one used in
[
54
]
. We aim to discuss the direct derivation from the expression (
1.10
), that is, the derivation of (
1.1
) in the strong sense, in a future work.

The proof of the derivation relies on the series representation of the solutions of the linear Boltzmann equation, following the pioneering work of Gallavotti
[
37
]
. It is worth to remark that in the case considered in this paper, due to the inelastic nature of the collisions, we face the following serious difficulty. Considering the series expansion of the solution to the inelastic linear Boltzmann equation, in the integrand of the
k
-th term appears the product
\prod_{l=1}^{k}|v^{-(l-1)}\cdot\omega_{l}|/r^{2}
, where
\omega_{l}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
is the angular parameter of the
l
-th collision, and
v^{-(l)}
is the
l
-th pre-collisonal velocity (defined as the
l
-th iteration of the inverse of the scattering
\kappa_{\omega_{l}}
(
1.3
)). Due to the dissipative nature of the collisions,
|v^{-(l)}|
grows exponentially fast in
l
, so that the
k
-th term of the series representation grows as
(1/r)^{2k+k^{2}}
, yielding severe issues to prove the convergence of the series.

We address this issue by considering solutions with a bounded exponential moment. To the best of our knowledge, the series representation of the solutions of the inelastic linear Boltzmann equation, and the proof of its convergence, is a novelty.

Plan of the paper.

The content is organized as follows. In Section 2, we introduce the particle system we are considering at the microscopic level, as well as the Boltzmann-Grad scaling that allows to reach the mesoscopic description. We then present the main results, and the strategy we adopt for the derivation. Section 3 is devoted to the rigorous results we will need concerning the inelastic linear Boltzmann equation (
1.1
). We discuss in particular the series representation of the solutions of (
1.1
). In Section 4 we perform the rigorous derivation of (
1.1
) from the inelastic Lorentz gas. Finally, in Section 5 we prove that the dynamics of the light particle inelastically colliding with the scatterers, is well-posed, globally in time, for a set of distributions of scatterers of probability
1
.

Notations

We will denote by
\mathcal{P}\big(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\big)
the set of the probability measures in
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\mathcal{M}\big(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\big)
will denote the set of finite signed Radon measures on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and
\mathcal{M}_{+}\big(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\big)
its non-negative cone, that is, the subset of non-negative measures in
\mathcal{M}\big(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\big)
(see
[
34
]
, Section 7.1). In addition, we will denote by
\mathcal{M}_{+,1}\big(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\big)
the subset of
\mathcal{M}\big(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\big)
of non-negative Radon measures with a finite order moment, that is, such that:

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{+,1}\big(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\big)=\big\{f\in\mathcal{M}_{+}\big(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\big)\ /\ \int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\hskip-14.22636pt|v|\,f(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v)<+\infty\big\}.

(1.11)

\mathcal{M}\big(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\big)
,
\mathcal{M}_{+}\big(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\big)
and
\mathcal{M}_{+,1}\big(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\big)
will be endowed with the total variation norm.

We will denote by
\mathcal{C}_{0}\big(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\big)
the set of continuous functions on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
that are vanishing at infinity, and by
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{c}([0,+\infty[\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
the set of infinitely differentiable functions, compactly supported in
[0,+\infty[\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

We recall that since
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
is locally compact, the dual of
\mathcal{C}_{0}\big(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\big)
is
\mathcal{M}\big(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\big)
(see for instance
[
58
]
).

When
I
is a finite set, we will denote by
\#I
its cardinal. For a subset
B
of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
which is Lebesgue-measurable, we will denote by
|B|
its Lebesgue measure.

For two subsets
A
,
B
of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we also introduce their
sum
, denoted by
A+B
, and defined as:

\displaystyle A+B=\{x+y\ /\ x\in A,\,y\in B\}.

(1.12)

We will often consider the sum of segments with balls.

Finally, for any subset
A
of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we will denote by
\overline{A}
its closure, that is,
\overline{A}
is the smallest closed subset of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
that contains
A
.

2
Model, main results and strategy

2.1
The model

2.1.1
The microscopic dynamics

Distributions of scatterers.

We call
distribution of scatterers
(or
distribution
in short), denoted by
c
, a finite or countable set of points
c_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, which is locally finite. In other words,
c=\big(c_{i}\big)_{i\in I}\in\big(\mathbb{R}^{d}\big)^{I}
, with
\#I\in\mathbb{N}
or
I=\mathbb{N}
, and
\#(c\cap K)\in\mathbb{N}
for any compact set
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
. The whole collection of distributions of scatterers will be denoted by
C
. In order to define the Poisson process of intensity
\mu>0
, we equip
C
with the measure
\mathbb{P}_{\mu}
defined as follows. The probability of finding exactly
N
points
c_{i_{1}},\dots,c_{i_{N}}
of
c
in a given Lebesgue-measurable subset
B\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
, of finite measure, is equal to:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mu,N}(B)=e^{-\mu|B|}\frac{\mu^{N}}{N!}|B|^{N},

(2.1)

where
|B|
is the Lebesgue measure of
B
. In other words, the probability of finding
N
points
c_{i_{1}},\dots,c_{i_{N}}
of
c
lying respectively in the infinitesimal volumes
dc_{1},\dots,dc_{N}
centered at
x_{1},\dots,x_{N}
is

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mu,N}(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{1}\dots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{N}=e^{-\mu|B|}\frac{\mu^{N}}{N!}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{1}\dots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{N},

(2.2)

which is the Janossy measure of order N of the Poisson point process restricted to the Borel subset
B\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
. The reader may refer to Definition 4.6, Example 4.8 and formula (4.21) in
[
43
]
, and also to the reference
[
38
]
).

For
\varepsilon>0
and given a distribution
c
, we will also consider the family of balls
\big(B(c_{i},\varepsilon)\big)_{i\in I}
centered on the points
c_{i}
of
c
, and of radius
\varepsilon>0
. We will call this family of balls the
distribution of scatterers
c
of radius
\varepsilon
.

The dynamics of the tagged particle.

We define now the dynamics of the tagged particle among the scatterers, in terms of a singular differential equation.

Definition 1

(Forward flow of the tagged particle)
.

Let
r\in\ ]0,1]
,
\varepsilon>0
,
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{*}
, and let
c\in C
be a distribution of scatterers in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Let
(x,v)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
. We define the
forward flow of the tagged particle
, as the piecewise affine mapping:

\displaystyle T_{c,\varepsilon}:\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccc}[0,t]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}&\rightarrow&\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d},\\
(s,x,v)&\mapsto&T_{c,\varepsilon}^{s}(x,v)=\big(x_{c,\varepsilon}(s),v_{c,\varepsilon}(s)\big)\end{array}\right.

that is right continuous in
t
at
(x,v)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
fixed, with a limit from the left at all points, and that satisfies, using the notation
\big(x_{c,\varepsilon}(s),v_{c,\varepsilon}(s)\big)=\big(x(s),v(s)\big)
:

\displaystyle T_{c,\varepsilon}^{0}(x,v)=(x,v),

(2.5)

\displaystyle\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s}T_{c,\varepsilon}^{s}(x,v)=\big(v(s),0\big)\hskip 14.22636pt\text{if}\hskip 14.22636ptd\big(x(s),c\big)>\varepsilon,

(2.6)

and

\displaystyle v(s)=v(s^{-})-(1+r)\big(v(s^{-})\cdot\omega(s)\big)\omega(s)\hskip 14.22636pt\text{if}\hskip 14.22636ptd\big(x(s),c\big)=\varepsilon\hskip 5.69054pt\text{and}\hskip 8.53581pt\#\big(c\cap\big(x(s)+\overline{B(0,\varepsilon)}\big)\big)=1,

(2.7)

where:

\displaystyle v(s^{-})=\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tau\rightarrow s\\
\tau<s\end{subarray}}v(\tau),

(2.8)

and

\displaystyle\omega(s)=\lim_{\begin{subarray}{c}\tau\rightarrow s\\
\tau<s\end{subarray}}\frac{x(\tau)-c(s)}{\big|x(\tau)-c(s)\big|}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{with}\hskip 14.22636ptc(s)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\hskip 8.53581pt\text{being the center of the collided scatterer at time }s,

(2.9)

\displaystyle\text{ that is, such that}\hskip 2.84526pt\{c(s)\}=c\cap\big(x(s)+\overline{B(0,\varepsilon)}\big).

Figure 1
:
Representation of a collision obtained by the scattering mapping:
v^{\prime}=\kappa_{\omega}(v)
. In comparison, an elastic collision (
r=1
) is represented in light grey.

Depending on the configuration of scatterers
c
, the forward flow of the tagged particle introduced in Definition
1
might not be globally defined. We introduce therefore a notation that will be useful to state the main result of the present article.

Definition 2

(Generalized forward flow)
.

For any positive time
t>0
and any configuration
c
of scatterers, we define the
generalized forward hard sphere flow
as the mapping:

\displaystyle\widetilde{T}_{c,\varepsilon}:\left\{\begin{array}[]{cccc}[0,t]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}&\hskip-5.69054pt\rightarrow&\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d},&\\
(s,x,v)&\hskip-5.69054pt\mapsto&\widetilde{T}_{c,\varepsilon}^{s}(x,v)&\hskip-5.69054pt=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lc}\,T_{c,\varepsilon}^{s}(x,v)&\hskip-2.84526pt\text{if the flow }s\mapsto T_{c,\varepsilon}^{s}(x,v)\text{ is globally defined}\\
&\hskip-14.22636pt\text{on }[0,t]\text{ for the distribution of scatterers }c\\
&\text{and the initial configuration }(x,v),\\
\\
\,(x,v)&\text{ otherwise.}\end{array}\right.\end{array}\right.

Remark 1
.

In Definitions
1
and
2
, we introduced only the flow
forward in time
. We will see that it will be possible to establish a well-posedness result for such a flow (see Proposition
8
). Nevertheless, it will be conceptually important to consider a
backward in time
version of such a flow, that is, to consider the past of a trajectory which lies at
(x,v)
in the phase space at time
0
. Such a backward flow will be denoted by
T_{c,\varepsilon}^{t}(x,v)
, with
t<0
.
However, and contrary to the elastic case, the backward flow turns out to be more singular than the forward flow. Indeed, it is easy to consider scatterer configurations that lead to an infinite number of collisions in finite time when considering the backward in time dynamics of a tagged particle colliding with inelastic scatterers, as the sequence of consecutive time intervals of mean free flight is a geometric series of ratio
>1
. This phenomenon is an instance of the
inelastic collapse
, well documented in the case of interacting inelastic hard spheres (see for instance
[
15
]
,
[
51
]
,
[
52
]
,
[
27
]
,
[
30
]
, and the references therein).

2.1.2
The Boltzmann-Grad limit

In order to perform the derivation of the linear inelastic Boltzmann equation from the dynamics of the tagged particle, we will consider the so-called Boltzmann-Grad scaling, also known as the low density limit.

Definition 3

(Boltzmann-Grad limit)
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
and
\mu_{\varepsilon}>0
. We consider a distribution of scatterers
\big(B(c_{i},\varepsilon)\big)_{i\in I}
of radius
\varepsilon>0
, distributed according to the Poisson process of intensity
\mu_{\varepsilon}>0
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. We say that the distribution of scatterers satisfies the Boltzmann-Grad scaling condition if:

\displaystyle\mu_{\varepsilon}\varepsilon^{d-1}=1.

(2.16)

By definition, the
Boltzmann-Grad limit
is characterized by
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
while (
2.16
) holds.

By assumption, in the Boltzmann-Grad limit the tagged particle suffers, in average, a fixed number of collisions per unit of time with the scatterers
B(c_{i},\varepsilon)
. We remark that the Boltzmann-Grad limit is also referred to as the low density limit, because the volume fraction tends to zero. Indeed, in any fixed ball
B(0,R)
, the average volume occupied by the scatterers of volume
C(d)\varepsilon^{d}
is given by:

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{+\infty}k\,e^{-\mu_{\varepsilon}|B(0,R)|}\frac{\mu_{\varepsilon}^{k}}{k!}\big|B(0,R)\big|^{k}C(d)\varepsilon^{d}=\mu_{\varepsilon}\big|B(0,R)\big|C(d)\varepsilon^{d}=C(d)\big|B(0,R)\big|\varepsilon\rightarrow 0.

(2.17)

Discussion on the inelastic collapse in the Boltzmann-Grad limit.

Taking into account the dissipation of the kinetic energy due to the scattering (
1
), we have that the norm of the velocity of the tagged particle is, in average, contracted by a constant at each collision. Since the mean free path is constant in the Boltzmann-Grad scaling, the series of the consecutive time intervals of mean free flight is diverging.

As a consequence, we can expect that the gas of tagged particles does not cool down in finite time when (
2.16
) holds. In other words, it is expected that the inelastic collapse (see Remark
1
) does not take place within the inelastic Lorentz gas, at least for almost every distribution of scatterers. This comment applies only to the case of the forward in time dynamics. Concerning the backwards dynamics, the effect is the opposite: the norm of the velocities grows exponentially fast with the number of collisions, and therefore in the low density limit the series of the consecutive time intervals of mean free flight times is converging. This means that we expect the inelastic collapse to take place for the backwards dynamics, and it is actually not hard to imagine configurations of scatterers leading to the collapse.

2.2
Main result

We first provide the notion of solution of the microscopic inelastic Lorentz model as follows.

Definition 4

(Microscopic solution of the inelastic Lorentz model)
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
and
\mu_{\varepsilon}>0
be two real numbers satisfying the condition (
2.16
). Let
f_{0}\in\mathcal{P}\big(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\big)
, for any Borel set
A
of
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
we define the

microscopic solution of the inelastic Lorentz model
, that we denote by
f_{\varepsilon}\in L^{\infty}\big([0,T);\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})\big)
, as the measure-valued function defined by the following duality relation:

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathds{1}_{A}(x,v)f_{\varepsilon}(t,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mathbb{P}_{\mu_{\varepsilon}}(\{c\in C\ /\ \widetilde{T}_{c,\varepsilon}^{t}(x_{0},v_{0})\in A\})f_{0}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x_{0},\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v_{0}),

(2.18)

where
C
is the Poisson process on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
of intensity
\mu_{\varepsilon}
,
\mathbb{P}_{\mu_{\varepsilon}}
the associated measure, and
\widetilde{T}_{c,\varepsilon}^{t}(x,v)
is the generalized forward flow of the tagged particle introduced in Definition
2
.

Our goal is to prove that in some weak sense
f_{\varepsilon}(x,v,t)\to f(x,v,t)
as
\varepsilon\to 0
in the Boltzmann-Grad limit
\mu_{\varepsilon}\cdot\varepsilon^{d-1}=1
, where
f
is a solution of the kinetic equation (
1.6
)
with initial datum
f_{0}
. We now introduce the notion of solutions to (
1.6
) in the sense of measures.

Definition 5

(Weak solution of the linear inelastic Boltzmann equation (
1.6
))
.

Let
f_{0}\in\mathcal{P}\big(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\big)
be a probability measure on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
f\in\mathcal{C}\big([0,+\infty[,\mathcal{M}_{+,1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})\big)
be a continuous function taking values in the set of measure
\mathcal{M}_{+,1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
, defined in (
1.11
).

We say that
f
is a
weak solution of the linear inelastic Boltzmann equation (
1.6
) with initial datum
f_{0}
if
f(0,\cdot,\cdot)=f_{0}
and if

\displaystyle\hskip-5.69054pt-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}\hskip-4.2679pt\widetilde{\varphi}(0,x,v)f_{0}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v)-\int_{0}^{+\infty}\hskip-7.11317pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}\hskip-4.2679pt\partial_{t}\widetilde{\varphi}(t,x,v)f(t,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t-\int_{0}^{+\infty}\hskip-7.11317pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}\hskip-4.2679ptv\cdot\nabla_{x}\widetilde{\varphi}(t,x,v)f(t,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt=\int_{0}^{+\infty}\hskip-7.11317pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\omega}}|v\cdot\omega|\big[\widetilde{\varphi}(t,x,v^{\prime})-\widetilde{\varphi}(t,x,v)\big]\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega f(t,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t

(2.19)

for any test function
\widetilde{\varphi}\in\mathcal{C}_{c}^{\infty}([0,+\infty[\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
, with
v^{\prime}=v-(1+r)(v\cdot\omega)\omega
defined in (
1.3
).

We are now in position to state the main result of the present article.

Theorem 1

(Derivation of the weak form of the linear inelastic Boltzmann equation)
.

Let
f_{0}\in\mathcal{P}\big(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\big)\cap\mathcal{M}_{+,1}\big(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\big)
. Let us assume in addition that there exists a constant
p>1
such that:

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}e^{|v|^{p}}f_{0}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v)<+\infty,

(2.20)

and let
f\in\mathcal{C}\big([0,+\infty[,\mathcal{M}_{+}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})\big)
be the unique weak solution of the linear inelastic Boltzmann equation (
1.6
) with initial datum
f_{0}
. Then,
f_{\varepsilon}(t,\cdot,\cdot)
converges weakly
-*
towards
f(t,\cdot,\cdot)
in the Boltzmann-Grad limit
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
with (
2.16
) holding true, that is, for any
t\geq 0
and for any test function
\varphi\in\mathcal{C}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we have:

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}\varphi(x,v)f_{\varepsilon}(t,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v)\underset{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}{\longrightarrow}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}\varphi(x,v)f(t,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v),

(2.21)

where
f_{\varepsilon}
is the microscopic solution of the inelastic Lorentz model starting from
f_{0}
defined in (
2.18
), Definition
4
.

The rate of convergence in (
2.21
) is explicit. More precisely, there exists a universal constant
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that, for any
0<\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}
, and for any
t_{0}>0
,
\varphi\in\mathcal{C}_{0}\big(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we have:

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}\varphi(x,v)f_{\varepsilon}(t,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v)-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}\varphi(x,v)f(t,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v)\Big|\leq C_{\text{final}}\,\varepsilon^{1/4},

(2.22)

where
C_{\text{final}}=C_{\text{final}}(d,r,p,t_{0},f_{0},\varphi)
is a constant that depends only on the dimension
d
, the restitution coefficient
r
, the exponential weight
p
, the initial datum
f_{0}
and the test function
\varphi
. The constant
C_{\text{final}}
depends on
f_{0}
and
\varphi
only via the exponential moment (
2.20
) of
f_{0}
, and the supremum norm
{\left|\kern-1.29167pt\left|\varphi\right|\kern-1.29167pt\right|}_{\infty}
of
\varphi
.

The existence and uniqueness of the weak solution to the inelastic linear Boltzmann equation (
1.1
) will be established in Section
3.4
, respectively in Proposition
2
and
3
.

Remark 2
.

Observe that the weak
-*
convergence stated in Theorem
1
is equivalent to:

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}\mathds{1}_{A}(x,v)f_{\varepsilon}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v)\underset{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}{\longrightarrow}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}\mathds{1}_{A}(x,v)f(t,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v)

(2.23)

for any measurable set
A
of
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
, which is also equivalent to state that the sequence of measures
\big(f_{\varepsilon}\big)_{\varepsilon}
converges towards the measure
f
, in the weak sense in terms of probability theory (see for instance
[
8
]
).

2.3
Strategy of the proof

We summarize here the main steps needed to prove the main result of this paper, i.e. Theorem
1
. In a first part, we motivate the formal definition of the objects we will consider, and in a second part, we will describe the detailed plan of the proof of Theorem
1
, decomposed into several intermediate results.

2.3.1
The main ideas and objects behind the proof

The microscopic distribution function.

The arguments in the present Section
2.3.1
have to be understood at a formal level. We will make all the steps rigorous in the sequel.
Our aim is to perform the rigorous derivation of the inelastic linear Boltzmann equation (
1.6
) from the microscopic inelastic Lorentz model. More precisely, we will prove that a solution
f
of (
1.6
) can be approximated by the distribution function
f_{\varepsilon}
of a microscopic tagged particle, that collides inelastically with a set of scatterers of size
\varepsilon
, when
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
, assuming that the distribution of the scatterers obeys a Poisson process of intensity
\mu_{\varepsilon}
with
\mu_{\varepsilon}\varepsilon^{d-1}=1
.

The natural object to be considered as distribution function
f_{\varepsilon}
is:

\displaystyle f_{\varepsilon}(x,v)=\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\varepsilon}}\Big[\big|J\big(T_{c,\varepsilon}^{-t}(x,v)\big)\big|\cdot f_{0}\big(T_{c,\varepsilon}^{-t}(x,v)\big)\Big],

(2.24)

where
f_{0}
is the distribution function of the tagged particle in the phase space at the initial time, and
T_{c,\varepsilon}^{-t}(x,v)
is the flow of the tagged particle, introduced in Definition
1
. As already mentioned, (
2.24
) is the generalization of the classical distribution function introduced by Gallavotti in
[
37
]
to the inelastic case. Observe, nevertheless, that (
2.24
) is defined relying on the backward flow, which is not clearly defined a priori (see Remark
1
).

The quantity
f_{\varepsilon}
defined in (
2.24
) corresponds to the density, with respect to the Lebesgue measure, of the measure describing the probability to find at time
t
the tagged particle in the elementary volume
x+\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x
, with a velocity lying in
v+\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v
. The Jacobian of the dynamical flow has to be considered, because the measure in the phase space is not conserved along the dynamics of the tagged particle, due to the dissipative nature of the collisions.

The approach to perform the derivation of the weak form.

In this paper, we focus on the derivation of the weak solutions
f
of (
1.6
). That is, for any time
t\geq 0
and any test function
\varphi=\varphi(x,v)
that is regular enough, we consider the quantity
I_{\varepsilon}(\varphi,t)
defined as:

\displaystyle\overline{I}_{\varepsilon}(\varphi,t)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}f_{\varepsilon}(t,x,v)\varphi(x,v)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\varepsilon}}\bigg[|J\big(T^{-t}_{c,\varepsilon}(x,v)\big)|\cdot f_{0}\big(T^{-t}_{c,\varepsilon}(x,v)\big)\bigg]\varphi(x,v)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x,

(2.25)

assuming that we can indeed consider the quantity (
2.24
).

Using Fubini’s theorem, and the change of variables
(x,v)\rightarrow T^{t}_{c,\varepsilon}(x,v)
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}
for distributions of scatterers
c
fixed, we find:

\displaystyle\overline{I}_{\varepsilon}(\varphi,t)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\varepsilon}}\Bigg[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}|J\big(T^{-t}_{c,\varepsilon}(x,v)\big)|\cdot f_{0}\big(T^{-t}_{c,\varepsilon}(x,v)\big)\varphi(x,v)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\Bigg]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\varepsilon}}\Bigg[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}f_{0}(x,v)\varphi\big(T^{t}_{c,\varepsilon}(x,v)\big)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\Bigg]=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}f_{0}(x,v)\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\varepsilon}}\big[\varphi\big(T^{t}_{c,\varepsilon}(x,v)\big)\big]\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x.

(2.26)

Therefore, the object we will consider at the level of the microscopic particle system to prove the weak convergence will be:

\displaystyle\varphi_{\varepsilon}(t,x,v)=\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\varepsilon}}\big[\varphi\big(T^{t}_{c,\varepsilon}(x,v)\big)\big],

(2.27)

so that the weak convergence of
f_{\varepsilon}
towards
f
, by testing against any test function
\varphi
, is obtained by comparing the two integrals:

\displaystyle I_{0}(\varphi,t)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}f(t,x,v)\varphi(x,v)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\hskip 14.22636pt\text{and}\hskip 14.22636ptI_{\varepsilon}(\varphi,t)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}f_{0}(x,v)\varphi_{\varepsilon}(t,x,v)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v,

(2.28)

with
\varphi_{\varepsilon}
defined by (
2.27
). Let us also observe that, in the particular case when the test function
\varphi
is chosen to be the indicator function
\mathds{1}_{A}
of a measurable set
A
of
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we have the elementary identity concerning
\varphi_{\varepsilon}
:

\displaystyle\varphi_{\varepsilon}(t,x,v)=\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\varepsilon}}\big[\mathds{1}_{A}\big(T^{t}_{c,\varepsilon}(x,v)\big)\big]=\int_{C}\mathds{1}_{A}\big(T^{t}_{c,\varepsilon}(x,v)\big)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{\mu_{\varepsilon}}(c)=\mathbb{P}_{\mu_{\varepsilon}}\big(\{c\in C\ /\ T^{t}_{c,\varepsilon}(x,v)\in A\}\big).

(2.29)

In turn, observe that introducing the expressions (
2.27
), (
2.29
) of
\varphi_{\varepsilon}
, together with
I_{\varepsilon}
, allows to define a time-dependent family of measures
f_{\varepsilon}(t)
via its action on the test functions
\varphi
relying on the duality relation:

\displaystyle\langle f_{\varepsilon}(t),\varphi\rangle=I_{\varepsilon}(\varphi,t).

(2.30)

Considering the quantities (
2.27
) or (
2.29
) has the great advantage to rely on the forward flow of the tagged particle, allowing therefore to consider objects that are more regular than (
2.24
). This discussion motivates the introduction of the quantity
f_{\varepsilon}
by the action described in (
2.18
), Definition
4
.

Relying on the adjoint equation of (
1.6
).

In the same way as we rewrote the integral
\overline{I}_{\varepsilon}(\varphi,t)
in a more regular object by transferring the dynamical flow to the test function
\varphi
, we can also take advantage of the definition of the weak solutions of (
1.6
). In particular, we observe that if
f
is a weak solution of the linear inelastic Boltzmann equation (
1.6
) in the sense of Definition
5
, and if the function
\widetilde{\varphi}
solves the
adjoint equation
of (
1.6
):

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\widetilde{\varphi}-v\cdot\nabla_{x}\widetilde{\varphi}=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\omega}}|v\cdot\omega|\big[\widetilde{\varphi}(v^{\prime})-\widetilde{\varphi}(v)\big]\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega

(2.31)

with initial datum
\widetilde{\varphi}(0,\cdot,\cdot)=\varphi
(where
v^{\prime}=\kappa_{\omega}(v)
is defined in (
1.3
)), then

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}\varphi(x,v)f(t,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}\widetilde{\varphi}(t,x,v)f_{0}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v).

(2.32)

In the end, we will prove:

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}_{x}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(t,x,v)f_{0}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v)\underset{\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}{\longrightarrow}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}\widetilde{\varphi}(t,x,v)f_{0}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v),

(2.33)

with
\varphi_{\varepsilon}
as in (
2.27
) and
\widetilde{\varphi}
solving (
2.31
), relying on explicit expressions of the dual quantities
\varphi_{\varepsilon}
and
\widetilde{\varphi}
.

A brief comparison with the Kolmogorov equations.

The quantities (
2.24
) and (
2.27
) have strong links with the theory of the backward and forward Kolmogorov equations. Indeed, for a continuous Markov process
X(\tau)
on a certain probability space
\mathcal{S}
, with
0\leq\tau\leq t
, if we define
u
and
f
, respectively, by

\displaystyle u(\tau,s)=\mathbb{P}(X(\tau)=s)\hskip 14.22636pt\text{and}\hskip 14.22636ptf(\tau,s)=\mathbb{E}[g(X(t))|X(\tau)=s]

(2.34)

where
g
is an arbitrary function, it is well-known that
u
and
f
solve respectively the forward and the backward Kolmogorov equations, and that this two quantities are linked by the duality relation:

\displaystyle\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}}u(\tau,s)f(\tau,s)=\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}}u(0,s)f(0,s)\hskip 8.53581pt\forall\,\tau\in[0,t].

(2.35)

In our case, the continuous process is given by the flow
T^{\tau}_{c,\varepsilon}
, and the probability space is replaced by the phase space
\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}
. Considering as a test function
\varphi
the indicator function
\mathds{1}_{A}
of a subset
A
of the phase space, we have:

\displaystyle\varphi_{\varepsilon}(\tau,x,v)=\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\varepsilon}}\big[\mathds{1}_{A}\big(T^{\tau}_{c,\varepsilon}(x,v)\big)\big]=\mathbb{P}_{\mu_{\varepsilon}}\big(T^{\tau}_{c,\varepsilon}(x,v)\in A\big),

(2.36)

which corresponds clearly to the quantity
u
, solving the forward Kolmogorov equation. In the kinetic limit,
u
corresponds to the solution
\varphi
of the adjoint equation (
2.31
), and the relation (
2.32
) that the solution
\varphi
satisfies with a solution
f
of the linear inelastic Boltzmann equation can be interpreted as the duality relation between two solutions of a pair of backward and forward Kolmogorov equations. At the kinetic level, the limiting trajectories of the tagged particle are indeed Markov processes.

At the level of the particles, the process described by the dynamical flow cannot be Markovian anymore, because the scatterers have a positive size, allowing recollisions to take place. Nevertheless, considering anyway the duality bracket between
f_{\varepsilon}(\tau)
and
\varphi_{\varepsilon}(t-\tau,\cdot,\cdot)
, we have:

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}f_{\varepsilon}(\tau,x,v)\varphi_{\varepsilon}(t-\tau,x,v)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\varepsilon}}\bigg[|J\big(T^{-\tau}_{c,\varepsilon}(x,v)\big)|\cdot f_{0}\big(T^{-\tau}_{c,\varepsilon}(x,v)\big)\bigg]

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt\cdot\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\varepsilon}}\big[\mathds{1}_{A}\big(T^{t-\tau}_{c,\varepsilon}(x,v)\big)\big]\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x.

(2.37)

First, we observe that if
\tau=0
or
\tau=t
, one of the two expected values simplifies (because either
T^{-\tau}_{c,\varepsilon}
or
T^{t-\tau}_{c,\varepsilon}
becomes the identity, so it becomes independent from the distributions
c
of the scatterers), and the equality between the duality brackets at time
\tau=0
and at time
\tau=t
corresponds to the formal computation (
2.3.1
).

In the other cases, that is when
0<\tau<t
, we observe that if the covariance between the random variables
|J\big(T^{-\tau}_{c,\varepsilon}(x,v)\big)|\cdot f_{0}\big(T^{-\tau}_{c,\varepsilon}(x,v)\big)
and
\mathds{1}_{A}\big(T^{t-\tau}_{c,\varepsilon}(x,v)\big)
is zero, then we can write:

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}f_{\varepsilon}(t,x,v)\varphi_{\varepsilon}(t-\tau,x,v)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\varepsilon}}\bigg[|J\big(T^{-\tau}_{c,\varepsilon}(x,v)\big)|\cdot f_{0}\big(T^{-\tau}_{c,\varepsilon}(x,v)\big)\cdot\mathds{1}_{A}\big(T^{t-\tau}_{c,\varepsilon}(x,v)\big)\bigg]\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\varepsilon}}\Big[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}|J\big(T^{-\tau}_{c,\varepsilon}(x,v)\big)|\cdot f_{0}\big(T^{-\tau}_{c,\varepsilon}(x,v)\big)\cdot\mathds{1}_{A}\big(T^{t-\tau}_{c,\varepsilon}(x,v)\big)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\Big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\varepsilon}}\Big[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}f_{0}(x,v)\cdot\mathds{1}_{A}\big(T^{t}_{c,\varepsilon}(x,v)\big)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\Big],

(2.38)

which is a quantity independent from
\tau
.

However, there is no reason in general for the covariance between the two random variables
|J\big(T^{-\tau}_{c,\varepsilon}(x,v)\big)|\cdot f_{0}\big(T^{-\tau}_{c,\varepsilon}(x,v)\big)
and
\mathds{1}_{A}\big(T^{t-\tau}_{c,\varepsilon}(x,v)\big)
to be zero. Nevertheless, if we assume that we consider only distributions
c
of scatterers that give rise only to trajectories without recollisions, then
|J\big(T^{-\tau}_{c,\varepsilon}(x,v)\big)|\cdot f_{0}\big(T^{-\tau}_{c,\varepsilon}(x,v)\big)
and
\mathds{1}_{A}\big(T^{t-\tau}_{c,\varepsilon}(x,v)\big)
are indeed independent random variables, because the first one involves the backward flow, while the second involves the forward flow, both starting from the same point
(x,v)
of the phase space.

We recover therefore that in the absence of recollisions, the particle system behaves as a Markov process. We observe finally that the probability of recollisions vanishes in the Boltzmann-Grad limit, at least in the elastic case, which is consistent with the limit process that we recover in the kinetic limit.

2.3.2
The steps of the proof of Theorem
1

To establish the weak convergence stated in Theorem
1
, we will proceed as follows. We consider any test function
\varphi\in\mathcal{C}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
of the dual of the space of finite Radon measures
\mathcal{M}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and we define the quantities
I_{0}(\varphi,t)
and
I_{\varepsilon}(\varphi,t)
as in (
2.28
), (
2.27
). We recall that the main content of Theorem
1
is the convergence
I_{\varepsilon}(\varphi,t){\longrightarrow}I_{0}(\varphi,t)
, that is,
f_{\varepsilon}{\overset{*}{\rightharpoonup}}f
, as
\varepsilon\rightarrow 0
.

1.

We first prove that the
forward
dynamics of the particle system is well-posed, globally in time, a.e.. This step is necessary to give sense to
\varphi_{\varepsilon}
as defined in (
2.27
), and used in (
2.28
) to define
I_{\varepsilon}(\varphi,t)
. This is the content of Section
5
and, specifically, Proposition
8
.

2.

We rewrite
\varphi_{\varepsilon}
in a series form, where the index of each term corresponds to the number of obstacles that are collided by the tagged particle during the time interval
[0,t]
when evolving according to the dynamics of
T_{c,\varepsilon}^{t}(x,v)
, and where the integration variables are the positions
c_{k}
of the different obstacles that are collided. A small remainder will appear, corresponding on the one hand to a pathological distribution of the scatterers (pathological in the sense that the dynamics is not well-defined), and corresponding on the other hand to distributions of scatterers such that the initial position of the tagged particle at time
t=0
lies inside one of the obstacles. This is the content of Proposition
5
.

3.

In order to compare with the series expression of
\varphi_{\varepsilon}
, we write the solution
\widetilde{\varphi}
of the adjoint equation (
2.31
) as a series as well. This is Proposition
1
.

The series representation of
\widetilde{\varphi}
involves only the initial datum
\varphi=\widetilde{\varphi}(0,\cdot,\cdot)
, and makes sense provided that this initial datum is continuous. Considering then the integral
I_{0}(\varphi,s)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}\widetilde{\varphi}(s,x,v)f_{0}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v)
for any
s\in[0,t]
, we introduce this way a time-dependent family of measures
g(s)\in\mathcal{M}_{+}\big(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\big)
(for any
s\in[0,s]
) defined by duality as
g(s):\varphi\mapsto I_{0}(\varphi,s)
.

4.

Restarting from the series representation of
\varphi_{\varepsilon}
, we isolate now the distributions of scatterers that lead to a recollision. This provides an additional remainder, defined and estimated in Proposition
6
. In addition, we rewrite the terms of the new series representation in
(t,\omega)
-coordinates, which almost completes the comparison with the series representation of the solution
\widetilde{\varphi}
of the adjoint equation.

It is in this step that is done the careful (geometric) estimate on the domains of integration removed to prevent recollisions.

5.

Finally, we carefully study the remaining differences between the expressions of the solution of the adjoint equation, and the last version of
\varphi_{\varepsilon}
written in
(t,\omega)
-coordinates. Specifically, here it remains to estimate on the one hand the difference between the two dynamical tubes that arise. On the other hand, we estimate the error caused by the cut-offs in the integration domains that were introduced in the previous step.

6.

We can then conclude the proof of Theorem
1
. Indeed, the previous steps provide that
f_{\varepsilon}(t)
converges weakly towards the measure
g(t)
defined in the third step. In addition, observing that
g
is also a weak solution to the linear inelastic Boltzmann equation (
1.6
) (Proposition
2
), and that such a weak solution is unique once the initial datum is given (Proposition
3
), the proof of Theorem
1
follows.

Remark 3
.

Our constructive approach involves expanding
f_{\varepsilon}
into a series and comparing it with the series representation of the solution to the linear Boltzmann equation, as provided by the iterated Duhamel formula. This methodology aligns with the one that has been originally proposed in the pioneering work of Gallavotti
[
37
]
. On the other hand, the idea of employing weak solutions, tested against solutions to the adjoint equation, has been explored and discussed in
[
54
]
.
In that context, the coalescing process prevents to determine uniquely the past events that lead to the formation of a cluster of particles. In the present case, the singularity comes from the possibility that the inelastic collapse can take place in the past of a trajectory of the tagged particle. Considering weak solutions enables the analysis of the forward dynamics of the particle system, which is well-defined under the conditions considered in
[
54
]
, as well as in our case.

3
Properties of the inelastic linear Boltzmann equation

In this section, we present the key results concerning the inelastic linear Boltzmann equation (
1.1
) that will be used throughout the rest of the article. We discuss in particular the existence and uniqueness of solutions (strong or weak), as well as the decay of kinetic energy.

3.1
Duhamel formula for the solutions of (
1.6
)

Let us now provide a representation of the solutions of the linear Boltzmann equation (
1.6
). In the present Section
3.1
, the computations are formal. We will investigate the convergence of the series representation in Section
3.2
.
First, we define the quantity:

\displaystyle g(t,x,v)=f(t,x+tv,v),

(3.1)

where
f
is assumed to be a solution of (
1.6
). We have:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}g(t,x,v)+C_{d}|v|g(t,x,v)=\int_{\omega}\frac{|v\cdot\omega|}{r^{2}}g(t,x+t(v-\hskip 0.7113pt^{\prime}\hskip-2.13394ptv),^{\prime}\hskip-2.84526ptv)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega,

(3.2)

with
C_{d}
defined by (
1.8
). Integrating in time (
3.2
), we find:

\displaystyle g(t,x,v)=e^{-C_{d}|v|t}g(0,x,v)+\int_{0}^{t}e^{-C_{1}|v|(t-s)}\int_{\omega}\frac{|v\cdot\omega|}{r^{2}}g(s,x+s(v-\hskip 0.7113pt^{\prime}\hskip-2.13394ptv),\hskip 0.7113pt^{\prime}\hskip-1.42262ptv)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s.

(3.3)

We obtain after infinitely many iterations:

\displaystyle g(t,x,v)

\displaystyle=e^{-C_{d}|v|t}g(0,x,v)

\displaystyle\hskip 8.53581pt+e^{-C_{d}|v|t}\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}\int_{t_{1}=0}^{t}\int_{\omega_{1}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\dots\int_{t_{k}=0}^{t_{k-1}}\int_{\omega_{k}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}e^{C_{d}\sum_{j=1}^{k}t_{j}\left[|v^{-(j-1)}|-|v^{-j}|\right]}

\displaystyle\hskip 45.5244pt\times\left(\prod_{l=1}^{k}\frac{|v^{-(l-1)}\cdot\omega_{l}|}{r^{2}}\right)g(0,x+\sum_{m=1}^{k}\left[t_{m}(v^{-(m-1)}-v^{-m})\right],v^{-k})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{k}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{k}\dots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{1}.

(3.4)

Here, we denoted by
v^{-0}
the velocity
v
, and
\hskip 0.7113pt{}^{\prime}\hskip-0.7113ptv
is denoted by
v^{-1}
, i.e.
\kappa_{\omega_{1}}(v^{-1})=v
and, more generally,

\displaystyle\kappa_{\omega_{k}}(v^{-k})=v^{-(k-1)}.

(3.5)

Back to the solution
f
of (
1.6
), we obtain the following series representation:

\displaystyle f(t,x,v)

\displaystyle=e^{-C_{d}|v|t}f_{0}(x-tv,v)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+e^{-C_{d}|v|t}\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}\int_{t_{1}=0}^{t}\int_{\omega_{1}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}\dots\int_{t_{k}=0}^{t_{k-1}}\int_{\omega_{k}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}e^{C_{d}\sum_{j=1}^{k}t_{j}\left[|v^{-(j-1)}|-|v^{-j}|\right]}\left(\prod_{l=1}^{k}\frac{|v^{-(l-1)}\cdot\omega_{l}|}{r^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt\times f_{0}(x-tv+\sum_{m=1}^{k}\left[t_{m}(v^{-(m-1)}-v^{-m})\right],v^{-k})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{k}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{k}\dots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{1}.

(3.6)

3.2
On the convergence of the series representation

In the present section we will discuss the convergence of the series (
3.1
). One important difference between the inelastic and elastic cases is the presence of the product
\prod_{l=1}^{k}\frac{|v^{-(l-1)}\cdot\omega_{l}|}{r^{2}}
in the integrand, which prevents to obtain the convergence when assuming only
f_{0}\in L^{\infty}
. Note that in absence of
|v^{-(l-1)}\cdot\omega_{l}|
in the numerator, the term
1/r^{2k}=\left(1/r^{2}\right)^{k}
could be absorbed as in the elastic case, in an exponential. However, here, the norm of
v^{-(l-1)}
is causing an additional divergence, which grows fast. Indeed, depending on the angular parameters
\omega_{l}
, one can have:

\displaystyle|\hskip 0.7113pt^{\prime}\hskip-1.42262ptv|=\frac{1}{r}|v|,\hskip 8.53581pt|v^{-2}|=\frac{1}{r}|\hskip 0.7113pt^{\prime}\hskip-1.42262ptv|=\frac{1}{r^{2}}|v|,\dots

(3.7)

so that in the end, the product
\prod_{l=1}^{k}\frac{|v^{-(l-1)}\cdot\omega_{l}|}{r^{2}}
grows like
(1/r)^{k^{2}}
. On the other hand, for
a,b>0
, the series
\sum_{n\geq 0}\frac{a^{n}b^{n^{2}}}{n!}
is converging only if
b\leq 1
. In this case,
b=1/r^{2}
, so the series (
3.1
) may not converge without additional assumptions, except in the elastic case.

We will show that the series in (
3.1
) is converging provided that
f_{0}
decays sufficiently fast at infinity. Specifically, we will assume that
f_{0}
presents an exponential decay in velocity at infinity. Observe that a Maxwellian decay is not necessary. Actually, any super-polynomial decay in velocity would be enough to conclude.

Theorem 2

(Convergence of the series representation in the space of exponential weights in velocity)
.

Let
f_{0}:\mathbb{R}^{2d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a non-negative, measurable function. Let us assume that there exist
p
and
\alpha
two strictly positive real numbers such that:

\displaystyle\text{supess}_{(x,v)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\left|f_{0}(x,v)e^{\alpha|v|^{p}}\right|<+\infty.

(3.8)

Then, the series given by the formula (
3.1
) is converging.

Therefore, if
f_{0}\in\mathcal{C}^{1}\big(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\big)
and if (
3.8
) holds, there exists a strong solution to the linear inelastic Boltzmann equation (
1.6
) with initial datum
f_{0}
, given by the formula (
3.1
).

Proof.

We consider (
3.1
). We start with estimating precisely the difference between the norms of
v^{-(j-1)}
and
v^{-j}
. We have:

\displaystyle v^{-j}=v^{-(j-1)}-\left(1+\frac{1}{r}\right)v^{-(j-1)}\cdot\omega_{j}\omega_{j},

(3.9)

so that:

\displaystyle|v^{-j}|=|v^{-(j-1)}|\sqrt{1+\left(\frac{1}{r^{2}}-1\right)\frac{\left(v^{-(j-1)}\cdot\omega_{j}\right)^{2}}{|v^{-(j-1)}|^{2}}}\cdotp

(3.10)

The convergence of the series is based on the analysis of two different cases:
|\frac{v^{-(j-1)}}{|v^{-(j-1)}|}\cdot\omega_{j}|\leq\beta
or
|\frac{v^{-(j-1)}}{|v^{-(j-1)}|}\cdot\omega_{j}|>\beta
, where
0\leq\beta=\beta(k,l)\leq 1
will be chosen later. In the second case, one can take advantage of the exponential decay of
f_{0}
.

Let us consider now the
k
-th term (
k\geq 1
) of the series (
3.1
). Each of the
k
integrals in
\omega_{l}
(
1\leq l\leq k
) is decomposed between the domains
|\frac{v^{-(l-1)}}{|v^{-(l-1)}|}\cdot\omega_{l}|\leq\beta
and
|\frac{v^{-(l-1)}}{|v^{-(l-1)}|}\cdot\omega_{l}|>\beta
. The
k
-th term of the series is then decomposed into
2^{k}
sub-terms, encoded as follows. To each of such sub-terms, we associate the vector
(e_{1},\dots,e_{k})\in\{0,1\}^{k}
, where

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccc}e_{l}=0&\text{if the integral over }\omega_{l}\text{ runs over the domain:}&I(e_{l})=\big\{\omega_{l}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\ /\ |\frac{v^{-(l-1)}}{|v^{-(l-1)}|}\cdot\omega_{l}|\leq\beta\big\},\\
e_{l}=1&\text{if the integral over }\omega_{l}\text{ runs over the domain:}&I(e_{l})=\big\{\omega_{l}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\ /\ |\frac{v^{-(l-1)}}{|v^{-(l-1)}|}\cdot\omega_{l}|>\beta\big\}.\end{array}\right.

To each of the vectors
(e_{1},\dots,e_{k})
, we associate its
size

s
, defined as:

\displaystyle s\left(\left(e_{1},\dots,e_{k}\right)\right)=\sum_{l=1}^{k}e_{l}.

(3.13)

In other words, the size of
(e_{1},\dots,e_{k})
counts the number of entries that are equal to
1
. If
e_{l}=0
, we have:

\displaystyle|v^{-(l-1)}|\leq|v^{-l}|\leq\sqrt{1+\left(\frac{1}{r^{2}}-1\right)\beta^{2}}\cdot|v^{-(l-1)}|,

(3.14)

and if
e_{l}=1
, we have:

\displaystyle\sqrt{1+\left(\frac{1}{r^{2}}-1\right)\beta^{2}}\cdot|v^{-(l-1)}|<|v^{-l}|\leq\frac{1}{r}|v^{-(l-1)}|.

(3.15)

For
0\leq\beta\leq 1
, we define the following quantity:

\displaystyle q_{\beta}=\sqrt{1+\left(\frac{1}{r^{2}}-1\right)\beta^{2}}\geq 1,

(3.16)

which is equal to
1
if
\beta=0
, strictly larger than
1
if
\beta\neq 0
, and which is equal to
1/r
if
\beta=1
. Finally, let us observe that this quantity is an increasing function of
\beta\in[0,1]
.

For any vector
(e_{1},\dots,e_{k})\in\{0,1\}^{k}
, we have:

\displaystyle\Bigg|

\displaystyle\int_{t_{1}=0}^{t}\int_{\omega_{1}\in I(e_{1})}\dots\int_{t_{k}=0}^{t_{k-1}}\int_{\omega_{k}\in I(e_{k})}e^{C_{d}\sum_{j=1}^{k}t_{j}\left[|v^{-(j-1)}|-|v^{-j}|\right]}\left(\prod_{l=1}^{k}\frac{|v^{-(l-1)}\cdot\omega_{l}|}{r^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 170.71652pt\times f_{0}(x-tv+\sum_{m=1}^{k}\left[t_{m}(v^{-(m-1)}-v^{-m})\right],v^{-k})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{k}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{k}\dots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{1}\Bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\text{supess}_{(x,v)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\left|f_{0}(x,v)e^{\alpha|v|^{p}}\right|\int_{t_{1}=0}^{t}\int_{\omega_{1}\in I(e_{1})}\dots\int_{t_{k}=0}^{t_{k-1}}\int_{\omega_{k}\in I(e_{k})}\left(\prod_{l=1}^{k}|v^{-(l-1)}|\right)\left(\prod_{l=1}^{k}\frac{\left|\frac{v^{-(l-1)}}{|v^{-(l-1)}|}\cdot\omega_{l}\right|}{r^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 327.20668pt\times e^{-\alpha|v^{-k}|^{p}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{k}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{k}\dots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{1}.

(3.17)

Let us consider a vector
(e_{1},\dots,e_{k})\in\{0,1\}^{k}
of length
s
. In this case, since we have:

\displaystyle|v^{-(l-1)}|=\left[\prod_{m=1}^{l-1}\sqrt{1+\left(\frac{1}{r^{2}}-1\right)\left(\frac{v^{-(m-1)}}{|v^{-(m-1)}|}\cdot\omega_{m}\right)^{2}}\right]|v|.

(3.18)

In the product:

\displaystyle\prod_{l=1}^{k}|v^{-(l-1)}|

(3.19)

the scalar product
\frac{v}{|v|}\cdot\omega_{1}
appears
(k-1)
times, the scalar product
\frac{v^{-1}}{|v^{-1}|}\cdot\omega_{2}
appears
(k-2)
times, and more generally, the scalar product
\frac{v^{-(m-1)}}{|v^{-(m-1)}|}\cdot\omega_{m}
appears
(k-m)
times. Therefore, the product (
3.19
) is maximal when the terms that appear the most are the largest. In other words, the product (
3.19
) is maximal when
e_{1}=\dots=e_{s}=1
, and
e_{s+1}=\dots=e_{k}=0
.

As a consequence, we deduce on the one hand (using the convention
\prod_{m=1}^{0}u_{m}=1
):

\displaystyle\prod_{l=1}^{k}|v^{-(l-1)}|

\displaystyle=\prod_{l=1}^{k}\left[\prod_{m=1}^{l-1}\sqrt{1+\left(\frac{1}{r^{2}}-1\right)\left(\frac{v^{-(m-1)}}{|v^{-(m-1)}|}\cdot\omega_{m}\right)^{2}}\right]|v|

\displaystyle\leq\left(\prod_{l=1}^{s+1}\left[\prod_{m=1}^{l-1}\frac{1}{r}\right]|v|\right)\left(\prod_{l=s+2}^{k}\left[\left(\prod_{m=1}^{s}\frac{1}{r}\right)\left(\prod_{m=s+1}^{l-1}q_{\beta_{k}}\right)\right]|v|\right)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\prod_{l=1}^{s+1}\frac{1}{r^{l-1}}|v|\right)\left(\prod_{l=s+2}^{k}\frac{1}{r^{s}}q_{\beta_{k}}^{l-s-1}|v|\right)=\frac{1}{r^{\sum_{l=1}^{s+1}(l-1)+\sum_{l=s+2}^{k}s}}q_{\beta}^{\sum_{l=s+2}^{k}(l-s-1)}|v|^{k}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{r^{\left[\frac{s(s+1)}{2}+(k-s-1)s\right]}}q_{\beta}^{\frac{(k-s-1)(k-s)}{2}}|v|^{k}\leq\left(\frac{1}{r}\right)^{ks}q_{\beta}^{\frac{(k-s)^{2}}{2}}|v|^{k}.

(3.20)

On the other hand, from (
3.18
) we deduce also:

\displaystyle|v^{-k}|=\left[\prod_{m=1}^{k}\sqrt{1+\left(\frac{1}{r^{2}}-1\right)\left(\frac{v^{-(m-1)}}{|v^{-(m-1)}|}\cdot\omega_{m}\right)^{2}}\right]|v|\geq q_{\beta}^{s}|v|.

(3.21)

Finally, we have:

\displaystyle\prod_{l=1}^{k}\left|\frac{v^{-(l-1)}}{|v^{-(l-1)}|}\cdot\omega_{l}\right|\leq\beta^{k-s}.

(3.22)

Gathering (
3.2
), (
3.21
) and (
3.22
), we obtain the following upper bound on the elementary term (
3.2
), which is the part associated to the vector
(e_{1},\dots,e_{k})
(of length
s
) of the
k
-th term of the series representation (
3.1
):

\displaystyle\Bigg|

\displaystyle\int_{t_{1}=0}^{t}\int_{\omega_{1}\in I(e_{1})}\dots\int_{t_{k}=0}^{t_{k-1}}\int_{\omega_{k}\in I(e_{k})}e^{C_{d}\sum_{j=1}^{k}t_{j}\left[|v^{-(j-1)}|-|v^{-j}|\right]}\left(\prod_{l=1}^{k}\frac{|v^{-(l-1)}\cdot\omega_{l}|}{r^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 170.71652pt\times f_{0}(x-tv+\sum_{m=1}^{k}\left[t_{m}(v^{-(m-1)}-v^{-m})\right],v^{-k})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{k}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{k}\dots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{1}\Bigg|

\displaystyle\leq\left(\text{supess}_{(x,v)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\left|f_{0}(x,v)e^{\alpha|v|^{p}}\right|\right)\int_{t_{1}=0}^{t}\int_{\omega_{1}\in I(e_{1})}\dots\int_{t_{k}=0}^{t_{k-1}}\int_{\omega_{k}\in I(e_{k})}\left(\frac{1}{r}\right)^{ks}q_{\beta}^{\frac{(k-s)^{2}}{2}}|v|^{k}

\displaystyle\hskip 227.62204pt\times\left(\frac{1}{r}\right)^{2k}\beta^{k-s}e^{-\alpha(q_{\beta})^{sp}|v|^{p}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{k}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{k}\dots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\text{supess}_{(x,v)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\left|f_{0}(x,v)e^{\alpha|v|^{p}}\right|\right)\frac{t^{k}}{k!}\left(\frac{|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|\cdot|v|}{r^{2}}\right)^{k}\left(\frac{1}{r}\right)^{ks}q_{\beta}^{\frac{(k-s)^{2}}{2}}\beta^{k-s}e^{-\alpha(q_{\beta})^{sp}|v|^{p}}.

(3.23)

Now, each of the
k
-th terms of the series (
3.1
) is decomposed into
2^{k}
sub-terms, labelled by the vectors
(e_{1},\dots,e_{k})\in\{0,1\}^{k}
. In addition, for
k
fixed, and for a length
s
fixed, there are
{k\choose s}
such vectors. Therefore, the infinite series in (
3.1
) can be bounded from above by:

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}\sum_{s=0}^{k}{k\choose s}\left(\text{supess}_{(x,v)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\left|f_{0}(x,v)e^{\alpha|v|^{p}}\right|\right)\frac{t^{k}}{k!}\left(\frac{|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|\cdot|v|}{r^{2}}\right)^{k}\left(\frac{1}{r}\right)^{ks}q_{\beta}^{\frac{(k-s)^{2}}{2}}\beta^{k-s}e^{-\alpha(q_{\beta})^{sp}|v|^{p}}

\displaystyle=\left(\text{supess}_{(x,v)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\left|f_{0}(x,v)e^{\alpha|v|^{p}}\right|\right)\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}C^{k}\sum_{s=0}^{k}\frac{1}{s!(k-s)!}\left(\frac{1}{r}\right)^{ks}q_{\beta}^{\frac{(k-s)^{2}}{2}}\beta^{k-s}e^{-\alpha(q_{\beta})^{sp}|v|^{p}},

(3.24)

where

\displaystyle C=\frac{|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|\cdot|v|}{r^{2}}t.

(3.25)

We can now conclude the convergence of the series, relying on the study of the series:

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}=\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}C^{k}\sum_{s=0}^{k}\frac{1}{s!(k-s)!}\left(\frac{1}{r}\right)^{ks}q_{\beta}^{\frac{(k-s)^{2}}{2}}\beta^{k-s}e^{-\alpha(q_{\beta})^{sp}|v|^{p}}.

(3.26)

In order to do this, we need to choose in an appropriate manner the cut-off parameter
\beta
. Let us observe that the estimate (
3.2
) is obtained uniformly in terms of the vectors
(e_{1},\dots,e_{k})
, provided that
k
is fixed, as well as the length
s
of such vectors. Therefore,
\beta
cannot be chosen depending on the vectors
(e_{1},\dots,e_{k})
, nevertheless, we can choose
\beta
depending on both
k
and
s
. More precisely, we will consider:

\displaystyle\beta=\beta_{k,s}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\displaystyle{\frac{1}{(k-s)e^{\ln(1/r)s(s+1)}}}&\text{if }s\neq 0\text{ and }s\neq k,\\
\frac{1}{k}&\text{if }s=0,\\
\beta_{0}>0&\text{if }s=k.\end{array}\right.

To estimate the series
\mathcal{S}
, we start with separating the sum over
s
in three parts, treating separately the two extreme cases
s=0
and
s=k
. We set:

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}C^{k}\frac{1}{k!}q_{\beta}^{\frac{k^{2}}{2}}\beta^{k}e^{-\alpha|v|^{p}}}_{=\,\mathcal{S}_{1}}+\underbrace{\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}C^{k}\sum_{s=1}^{k-1}\frac{1}{s!(k-s)!}\left(\frac{1}{r}\right)^{ks}q_{\beta}^{\frac{(k-s)^{2}}{2}}\beta^{k-s}e^{-\alpha(q_{\beta})^{sp}|v|^{p}}}_{=\,\mathcal{S}_{2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\underbrace{\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}C^{k}\frac{1}{k!}\left(\frac{1}{r}\right)^{k^{2}}\hskip-8.53581pte^{-\alpha(q_{\beta})^{kp}|v|^{p}}}_{=\,\mathcal{S}_{3}}

(3.30)

An important point to keep in mind is that
q_{\beta}
depends on
\beta
, and that
q_{\beta}
converges to
1
when
\beta
converges to
0
, so that one cannot easily make use of the exponential weight in such a case.

We will rely on the two following formulas.

\displaystyle q_{\beta}^{\frac{k^{2}}{2}}\beta^{k}=e^{\frac{k^{2}}{4}\ln\left(1+\left(\frac{1}{r^{2}}-1\right)\beta^{2}\right)}e^{k\ln\beta}\leq e^{\frac{((1/r^{2})-1)}{4}k^{2}\beta^{2}+k\ln\beta}

(3.31)

and

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{r}\right)^{k^{2}}e^{-\alpha(q_{\beta})^{kp}|v|^{p}}=e^{k^{2}\ln(1/r)-\alpha(q_{\beta})^{kp}|v|^{p}}.

(3.32)

Concerning the first series
\mathcal{S}_{1}
, using (
3.31
), the choice
\beta_{k,0}=1/k
provides:

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}\frac{C^{k}}{k!}e^{\frac{((1/r^{2})-1)}{4}k^{2}\beta^{2}+k\ln\beta}\leq\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}\frac{C^{k}}{k!}e^{\frac{((1/r^{2})-1)}{4}-k\ln k}\leq e^{C+\frac{((1/r^{2})-1)}{4}}.

(3.33)

Concerning the third series
\mathcal{S}_{3}
, (
3.32
) provides, with the choice
\beta_{k,k}=\beta_{0}>0
(so that
q_{\beta_{0}}^{p}>1
is independent from
k
), that:

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{3}\leq\left(\sup_{k\geq 1}e^{k^{2}\ln(1/r)-\alpha(q_{\beta_{0}})^{kp}|v|^{p}}\right)\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}\frac{C^{k}}{k!}\leq e^{C}\left(\sup_{k\geq 1}e^{k^{2}\ln(1/r)-\alpha(q_{\beta_{0}})^{kp}|v|^{p}}\right).

(3.34)

We turn now to the central series
\mathcal{S}_{2}
. Exchanging the sums over
k
and
s
we find, and performing the change of variables
j=k-s
, we find:

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}C^{k}\sum_{s=1}^{k-1}\frac{1}{s!(k-s)!}\left(\frac{1}{r}\right)^{ks}q_{\beta}^{\frac{(k-s)^{2}}{2}}\beta^{k-s}e^{-\alpha(q_{\beta})^{sp}|v|^{p}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=1}^{+\infty}\sum_{j=1}^{+\infty}\frac{C^{j+s}}{s!j!}\left(\frac{1}{r}\right)^{(j+s)s}q_{\beta}^{\frac{j^{2}}{2}}\beta^{j}e^{-\alpha q_{\beta}^{sp}|v|^{p}}.

(3.35)

The choice (
3.2
) of
\beta_{k,s}
provides:

\displaystyle C^{j}\left(\frac{1}{r}\right)^{js}q_{\beta_{j+s,s}}^{\frac{j^{2}}{2}}\beta_{j+s,s}^{j}\leq\exp\left[j(\ln C-\ln j)+\frac{(1/r^{2}-1)}{4}-js^{2}\ln(1/r)\right].

(3.36)

Therefore, since
j(\ln C-\ln j)
tends to
-\infty
when
j\rightarrow+\infty
,
\exp\left(j(\ln C-\ln j)+\frac{(1/r^{2}-1)}{4}\right)
is bounded from above, uniformly in
s
, so that:

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{2}
