\displaystyle\leq\sum_{s=1}^{+\infty}\frac{C^{s}}{s!}\left(\frac{1}{r}\right)^{s^{2}}e^{-s^{2}\ln(1/r)}\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}\frac{\left[\sup_{l\geq 1}\exp\left(j(\ln C-\ln j)+\frac{(1/r^{2}-1)}{4}\right)\right]}{j!}

\displaystyle\leq\left[\sup_{l\geq 1}\exp\left(j(\ln C-\ln j)+\frac{(1/r^{2}-1)}{4}\right)\right]e^{C+1}.

(3.37)

Gathering (
3.33
), (
3.34
) and (
3.2
), we deduce that the series
\mathcal{S}
(given by the expression (
3.2
)) is converging.

Therefore, the series (
3.1
) is converging, and the proof of Theorem
2
is complete.
∎

3.3
Series representation of the solutions of the adjoint equation

We turn now the series representation of the solutions of the adjoint of the inelastic linear Boltzmann equation.

Proposition 1

(Series representation for the solutions of the adjoint equation (
2.31
))
.

Let
\varphi:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
function, such that
\varphi
and its gradient
\nabla_{x,v}\varphi
are vanishing at infinity.

We define the series:

\displaystyle\psi(t,x,v)

\displaystyle=e^{-C_{d}|v|t}\varphi(x+tv,v)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}\int_{t_{1}=0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\omega_{1}}}\hskip-8.53581pt\dots\int_{t_{k}=0}^{t_{k-1}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\omega_{k}}}e^{\big[\sum_{j=1}^{k}C_{d}|v^{(j-1)}|(t_{j}-t_{j-1})-C_{d}|v^{(k)}|t_{k}\big]}\prod_{l=1}^{k}\big|v^{(l-1)}\cdot\omega_{l}\big|

\displaystyle\hskip 108.12047pt\times\varphi(x+\sum_{m=1}^{k}\big(t_{m-1}-t_{m}\big)v^{(m-1)}+t_{k}v^{(k)},v^{(k)})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{k}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{k}\dots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{1},

(3.38)

with
C_{d}
defined in (
1.8
),
t^{(0)}=t
by convention and:

\displaystyle v^{(k)}=v^{(k-1)}-(1+r)\big(v^{(k-1)}\cdot\omega_{k}\big)\omega_{k}\hskip 5.69054pt\forall k\geq 1,\hskip 14.22636ptv^{(0)}=v.

(3.39)

Then the series
\psi(t,x,v)
is converging for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
,
x,v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\psi
is a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
function, and solves (in the strong sense) the adjoint equation (
2.31
) of the linear inelastic Boltzmann equation (
1.6
), with initial datum
\varphi
. In other words, we have:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{rclc}\partial_{t}\psi(t,x,v)-v\cdot\nabla_{x}\psi(t,x,v)&=&\displaystyle{\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\omega}}}|v\cdot\omega|\big[\psi(t,x,v^{\prime})-\psi(t,x,v)\big]\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega&\forall\,t\in\mathbb{R},\,x,v\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\\
\psi(0,x,v)&=&\varphi(x,v)&\forall\,x,v\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\end{array}\right.

with
v^{\prime}=v-(1+r)\big(v\cdot\omega)\omega
.

Conversely, any
\mathcal{C}^{1}
function
\widetilde{\varphi}:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
that is a strong solution to the adjoint equation (
2.31
) of the linear inelastic Boltzmann equation (
1.6
), with initial datum
\varphi
, is equal to the series
\psi
. In other words,
\widetilde{\varphi}=\psi
, the solution of the adjoint equation (
2.31
) is unique in the class of the
\mathcal{C}^{1}
functions, and is given by the expression (
1
) of
\psi
.

In addition, assuming only that
\varphi\in\mathcal{C}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
, for any
(t,x,v)\in[0,+\infty[\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the expression (
1
) defining the function
\psi
is still well-defined, it satisfies:

\displaystyle\big|\psi(t,x,v)\big|\leq{\left|\kern-1.29167pt\left|\varphi\right|\kern-1.29167pt\right|}_{\infty}e^{C_{d}|v|t}

(3.42)

and if the initial datum
\varphi=\psi(0,\cdot,\cdot)
is compactly supported, then the support
\text{supp}\,(\psi)
of
\psi
is such that
\text{supp}\,(\psi)\cap\big([0,t_{0}]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\big)
is a compact set of
\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
for any
t_{0}>0
.

Observe that the integration variables
t_{k}<t_{k-1}<\dots<t_{1}
(with
0\leq t_{k}
and
t_{1}\leq t
) have to be interpreted as the consecutive collision times of the tagged particle with the scatterers. We emphasize that
t_{k}
corresponds to the time of the first collision,
t_{k-1}
the second, and so on, so that the labeling of the collision times is inverted with respect to the labeling of the collisions.

The proof of Proposition
1
relies on classical arguments. We do not present the proof here, but it is postponed to Appendix
A
, for the sake of completeness.

Remark 4
.

It is important to observe here that the regularity of the initial datum
\varphi
does not propagate to the solution
\widetilde{\varphi}=\psi
of the adjoint equation (
2.31
), in the following sense. If we assume that
\varphi
is vanishing at infinity, it is not clear a priori that such a property holds true for
\psi
. This phenomenon is specific to the inelastic case, and might be interpreted as the consequence of the fact that, contrary to the elastic case for which
e^{\big[\sum_{j=1}^{k}C_{d}|v^{(j-1)}|(t_{j}-t_{j-1})-C_{d}|v^{(k)}|t_{k}\big]}=e^{-C_{d}|v|t}
, here the exponential term in (
1
) is more singular, and does not compensate entirely the growth coming from the product
\prod_{l=1}^{k}\big|v^{(l-1)}\cdot\omega_{l}\big|
.

3.4
Existence and uniqueness of the weak solution to the inelastic linear Boltzmann equation

We start with establishing the existence of weak solutions to the inelastic linear Boltzmann equation. In the same spirit as in
[
54
]
, we construct explicitly a weak solution, relying on solutions of the adjoint equation (
2.31
).

Proposition 2

(Existence of weak solutions to (
1.6
))
.

Let
f_{0}\in\mathcal{P}\big(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\big)
be a probability measure, which is also a non-negative Radon measure, and let
p>1
be constant such that:

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}e^{|v|^{p}}f_{0}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v)<+\infty.

(3.43)

Then, there exists a weak solution to (
1.6
) in the sense of Definition
5
, with initial datum
f_{0}
, that we denote by
g
, and that is defined by duality for all
t\geq 0
as:

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\varphi(x,v)g(t,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\psi(t,x,v)f_{0}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v)\hskip 14.22636pt\forall\varphi\in\mathcal{C}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}),

(3.44)

where
\psi
is the series given by the expression (
1
), with initial datum
\varphi
.

Proof.

First, according to the bound (
3.42
) for the solution
\psi
of the adjoint equation, we have that
(x,v)\mapsto\psi(t,x,v)e^{-|v|^{p}}
is a bounded function. This ensures, together with the assumption (
3.43
), that
g(t)
is a finite Radon measure.

To prove that
g
is a weak solution to (
1.6
), we consider a general test function
\widetilde{\eta}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}_{c}([0,+\infty[\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and we introduce the quantity:

\displaystyle\Delta(\widetilde{\eta})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}\hskip-4.2679pt\widetilde{\eta}(0,x,v)f_{0}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v)+\int_{0}^{+\infty}\hskip-7.11317pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}\hskip-4.2679pt\partial_{t}\widetilde{\eta}(t,x,v)g(t,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\int_{0}^{+\infty}\hskip-7.11317pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}\hskip-4.2679ptv\cdot\nabla_{x}\widetilde{\eta}(t,x,v)g(t,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t+\int_{0}^{+\infty}\hskip-7.11317pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}\mathcal{L}^{*}\big[\widetilde{\eta}\big](t,x,v)g(t,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t

(3.45)

with
\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}^{*}\big[\widetilde{\eta}\big](t,x,v)=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\omega}}|v\cdot\omega|\big[\widetilde{\eta}(t,x,v^{\prime})-\widetilde{\eta}(t,x,v)\big]\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega}
. We denote by
\xi
the quantity:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\widetilde{\eta}(t,x,v)+v\cdot\nabla_{x}\widetilde{\eta}(t,x,v)+\mathcal{L}^{*}\big[\widetilde{\eta}\big](t,x,v)=\xi(t,x,v).

(3.46)

We prove now an integral representation formula for
\widetilde{\eta}
in terms of solutions of the equation
\partial_{t}\phi+v\cdot\nabla_{x}\phi+\mathcal{L}^{*}\big[\phi\big]=0
. By assumption,
\widetilde{\eta}
is compactly supported, so let
t_{0}>0
be a real number such that
\text{supp}\,(\widetilde{\eta})\subset[0,t_{0}]\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
. We consider the function
\widetilde{\theta}
defined as
\widetilde{\theta}(t,x,v)=-\int_{t}^{t_{0}}\phi(s,t)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s
, where, for any
0\leq s\leq t_{0}
,
\phi(s,\cdot)
solves the Cauchy problem:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccc}\partial_{t}\phi(s,t)+v\cdot\nabla_{x}\phi(s,t)+\mathcal{L}^{*}\big[\phi(s,t)\big]&=&0,\\
\phi(s,s)&=&\xi(s).\end{array}\right.

By definition, we have
\widetilde{\theta}(t_{0},\cdot,\cdot)=0
for any
(x,v)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
. In addition, by construction we have also:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\widetilde{\theta}=\phi(t,t)-\int_{t}^{t_{0}}\partial_{t}\phi(s,t)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\xi(t)+\int_{t}^{t_{0}}v\cdot\nabla_{x}\phi(s,t)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s+\int_{t}^{t_{0}}\mathcal{L}^{*}\big[\phi(s,t)\big]\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\xi(t)-v\cdot\nabla_{x}\widetilde{\theta}(t)-\mathcal{L}^{*}\big[\widetilde{\theta}(t)\big],

(3.49)

so that
\widetilde{\theta}
solves also the equation (
3.46
). We deduce then that
\widetilde{\eta}=\widetilde{\theta}
. We can now rewrite
\Delta(\widetilde{\eta})
as follows:

\displaystyle\Delta(\widetilde{\eta})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}\hskip-4.2679pt\widetilde{\eta}(0,x,v)f_{0}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v)+\int_{0}^{+\infty}\hskip-7.11317pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}\hskip-4.2679pt\xi(t,x,v)g(t,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}\hskip-4.2679pt\widetilde{\eta}(0,x,v)f_{0}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v)+\int_{0}^{t_{0}}\hskip-7.11317pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}\hskip-4.2679pt\phi(t,t,x,v)g(t,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}\hskip-4.2679pt\widetilde{\eta}(0,x,v)f_{0}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v)+\int_{0}^{t_{0}}\hskip-7.11317pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}\hskip-4.2679pt\phi(t,0,x,v)f_{0}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t,

(3.50)

where in the last line we used that
\tau\mapsto\phi(s,t-\tau)
solves the adjoint equation (
2.31
) for any
s
, and we applied the definition of the function
g
. We find in the end:

\displaystyle\Delta(\widetilde{\eta})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}\hskip-4.2679pt\widetilde{\eta}(0,x,v)f_{0}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v)-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}\hskip-4.2679pt\Big(-\int_{0}^{t_{0}}\phi(t,0)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\Big)f_{0}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v)=0,

(3.51)

so that
g
is indeed a weak solution in the sense of Definition
5
, which concludes the proof.
∎

We complete the results concerning the weak solutions of (
1.6
) by proving the uniqueness of such weak solutions.

Proposition 3

(Uniqueness of weak solutions to (
1.6
))
.

Let
f_{0}\in\mathcal{P}\big(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\big)
be a probability measure, which is also a non-negative Radon measure.
Then, there exists at most one weak solution to (
1.6
) in the sense of Definition
5
, with initial datum
f_{0}
.

Proof.

By linearity, it is enough to prove that only the zero function is a weak solution with zero initial datum. We consider then a weak solution
f
to (
1.6
), with zero initial datum.

Let
t_{0}>0
be any positive number. We consider a sequence
\big(\chi_{n}\big)_{n}
of smooth functions
\chi_{n}:\mathbb{R}_{+}\rightarrow[0,1]
that converges pointwise towards
\mathds{1}_{[0,t_{0}]}
, supported on
[0,t_{0}]
, and such that
\chi_{n}(t)=1
for any
t\leq t_{0}-1/n
. By assumption, for any compactly supported test function
\widetilde{\eta}
, we have:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t_{0}}\hskip-7.11317pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}\hskip-4.2679pt\partial_{t}\big(\chi_{n}(t)\widetilde{\eta}(t,x,v)\big)f(t,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt=-\int_{0}^{t_{0}}\hskip-7.11317pt\chi_{n}(t)\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}\hskip-4.2679ptv\cdot\nabla_{x}\widetilde{\eta}(t,x,v)f(t,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t-\int_{0}^{t_{0}}\hskip-7.11317pt\chi_{n}(t)\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}\mathcal{L}^{*}\big[\widetilde{\eta}\big](t,x,v)f(t,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t.

(3.52)

We have:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t_{0}}\hskip-7.11317pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}\hskip-4.2679pt\partial_{t}\chi_{n}(t)\widetilde{\eta}(t,x,v)f(t,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\Big(\int_{t_{0}-1/n}^{t_{0}}\hskip-17.07164pt\partial_{t}\chi_{n}(t)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\Big)\Big(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}\hskip-2.84526pt\widetilde{\eta}(t_{0},x,v)f(t_{0},\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v)\Big)+o(1)

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}\hskip-2.84526pt\widetilde{\eta}(t_{0},x,v)f(t_{0},\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v)+o(1)

(3.53)

as
n\rightarrow+\infty
, using in particular that
\widetilde{\eta}
and
f
are both continuous. Therefore, by dominated convergence theorem, we have:

\displaystyle-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}\hskip-2.84526pt\widetilde{\eta}(t_{0},x,v)f(t_{0},\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v)+\int_{0}^{t_{0}}\hskip-7.11317pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}\hskip-4.2679pt\partial_{t}\widetilde{\eta}(t,x,v)f(t,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt=-\int_{0}^{t_{0}}\hskip-7.11317pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}\hskip-4.2679ptv\cdot\nabla_{x}\widetilde{\eta}(t,x,v)f(t,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t-\int_{0}^{t_{0}}\hskip-7.11317pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}\mathcal{L}^{*}\big[\widetilde{\eta}\big](t,x,v)f(t,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t.

(3.54)

For any Borel set
A\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we consider now a sequence
\big(\widetilde{\eta}_{m}\big)_{m}
of smooth functions smooth such that
\widetilde{\eta}_{m}(t_{0},\cdot,\cdot)
approximates
\mathds{1}_{A}
and such that
\widetilde{\eta}_{m}(-t)
solves the adjoint equation (
2.31
). According to Proposition
1
,
\widetilde{\eta}_{m}\chi_{n}
is indeed a smooth, compactly supported function, so that (
3.4
) holds for
\widetilde{\eta}_{m}
. In the limit
m\rightarrow+\infty
, we find:

\displaystyle f(t_{0},A)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}\mathds{1}_{A}(x,v)f(t_{0},\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v)=0,

(3.55)

which shows that
f(t_{0})
is the zero measure, concluding the proof of Proposition
3
.
∎

3.5
Some considerations on the kinetic energy

To complete this section on the inelastic linear Boltzmann equation, we conclude with a discussion on the cooling of the tagged particle as it evolves and collides with the scatterers. More precisely, we provide here an upper bound on the decay of the kinetic energy, following classical arguments in inelastic kinetic theory.

Proposition 4
.

Let
f
be a non-negative and regular solution of (
1.6
) with initial datum
f_{0}
, such that its mass is equal to
1
, and such that its second and third moments are always finite. Then we have:

\displaystyle\int_{x}\int_{v}|v|^{2}f(t,x,v)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\leq\left(\left(\int_{x}\int_{v}|v|^{2}f_{0}(t,x,v)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\right)^{-1/2}+\frac{(1-r^{2})}{2}\left[\int_{\omega}|e_{1}\cdot\omega|^{3}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega\right]t\right)^{-2}.

(3.56)

Proof.

The result is obtained as a consequence of a Grönwall-type estimate. We have:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t}\int_{x}\int_{v}|v|^{2}f(t,x,v)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{\omega}|e_{1}\cdot\omega|^{3}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega\right)(r^{2}-1)\int_{x}\int_{v}|v|^{3}f(t,x,v)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x.

(3.57)

Applying now the Hölder inequality, and keeping in mind that the mass of
f
is constantly equal to
1
:

\displaystyle\int_{x}\int_{v}|v|^{2}f(v)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\leq\left(\int_{x}\int_{v}|v|^{2p}f(v)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v\right)^{1/p}\left(\int_{x}\int_{v}f(v)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\right)^{1/q}=\left(\int_{x}\int_{v}|v|^{2p}f(v)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v\right)^{1/p},

(3.58)

for any
p,q\in[1,+\infty]
that are Hölder conjugates. In particular, taking
p=3/2
, the result follows by direct integration of the inequality.
∎

Remark 5
.

Let us observe that (
3.57
) enables to recover the conservation of the kinetic energy in the elastic case, that is, when
r=1
. In the general case, the upper bound on the decay of the kinetic energy agrees with the celebrated Haff’s law for granular gases of hard spheres with fixed restitution coefficient
[
15
]
.

4
Rigorous derivation of the linear inelastic Boltzmann equation, via weak convergence

4.1
Technical preliminaries

To perform the derivation of the weak form, we will rely on the following technical results. The purpose of the following lemma is to deduce a condition on the angular parameter
\omega
, providing that the pre-collisional velocity
v
is given, and that the direction of the post-collisional velocity
v^{\prime}=v-(1+r)\big(v\cdot\omega)\omega
is given, up to a small error.

Lemma 1

(Almost colinearity after scattering)
.

Let
r\in\ ]0,1[
. Then, at
v,p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
fixed, the post-collisional velocity
v^{\prime}=\kappa_{\omega}(v)
defined in (
1.3
) is almost colinear to
p
, that is:

\displaystyle\left[1-\left|\frac{v^{\prime}}{|v^{\prime}|}\cdot p\right|\right]\leq\delta

(4.1)

only if the angular parameter
\omega
belongs to a measurable subset
\mathcal{P}_{\text{colin.}}(v,p,\delta)\subset\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
of the unit sphere that has a Lebesgue measure smaller than:

\displaystyle\big|\mathcal{P}_{\text{colin.}}(v,p,\delta)\big|\leq C(d,r)\delta^{1/2},

(4.2)

where
C(d,r)
is a constant that depends only on the dimension
d
and the restitution coefficient
r
. In particular, the estimate (
4.2
) holds uniformly in
v,p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

More precisely, there exist two positive constants
\delta_{0}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{*}
and
C(d,r)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{*}
such that, for any vectors
v,\omega,p\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
v\neq 0
and
\omega,p\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, and any positive number
0<\delta\leq\delta_{0}
, such that if (
4.1
)
holds true, then,
\omega
belongs to a subset subset
\mathcal{P}_{\text{colin.}}(v,p,\delta)\subset\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
which satisfies the condition (
4.2
).

In the core of the proof of Theorem
1
, we will also make use of the following result. More precisely, the following result will be used to estimate the measure of the dynamical tube, that we will introduce in due time.

Lemma 2

(Estimate on the measure of twisted tubes)
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
be a positive number, and let
x_{1}
,
x_{2}
and
x_{3}
be three vectors of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. We consider the following set:

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=\big([x_{1},x_{2}]\cup[x_{2},x_{3}]\big)+\overline{B(0,\varepsilon)}

\displaystyle=\big\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\ /\ \exists\,\lambda\in[0,1],\,z\in\overline{B(0,\varepsilon)}\ \text{such that}\ y=\lambda x_{1}+(1-\lambda)x_{2}+z

\displaystyle\hskip 227.62204pt\text{or}\hskip 5.69054pty=\lambda x_{2}+(1-\lambda)x_{3}+z\big\}.

(4.3)

Then, the Lebesgue measure of
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}
is maximal when
x_{1}
,
x_{2}
and
x_{3}
are aligned, and in this order, that is, when:

\displaystyle\frac{x_{2}-x_{1}}{|x_{2}-x_{1}|}\cdot\frac{x_{3}-x_{2}}{|x_{3}-x_{2}|}=1.

(4.4)

The proofs of Lemmas
1
and
2
are postponed to the appendix.

Now that the technical tools are in place, we can now turn to the main part of the proof of Theorem
1
.

Figure 2
:
Representation of the result of Lemma
2
. The surface of the twisted tube (in blue) is smaller than the surface of the straight tube (in red), even if the distances
|x_{1}-x_{2}|
and
|x_{2}-x_{3}|
are the same for the left and the right tubes. The phenomenon is independent from the dimension.

4.2
Rewriting
\varphi_{\varepsilon}
as a series

The first step to rewrite
\varphi_{\varepsilon}=\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\varepsilon}}\big[\varphi\big(T^{t}_{c,\varepsilon}(x,v)\big)\big]
is to ensure that the flow
T^{t}_{c,\varepsilon}(x,v)
is well-defined. In order to define globally the flow
T_{c,\varepsilon}^{t}
for a given distribution of scatterers
c\in C
, we will assume that
c\in X_{0}\,\cap\,\mathcal{P}^{c}_{\text{patho.}}
, where
X_{0}
is the set of the distributions
c=(c_{i})_{i\in I}
such that none of the
c_{i}
is centered in the ball
\overline{B(x,\varepsilon)}
(so that the initial position
x
of the tagged particle is not inside any of the scatterers), and
\mathcal{P}_{\text{patho.}}
is defined along Proposition
8
in (
5.25
), and contains all the distributions
c
for which the flow
T_{c,\varepsilon}^{t}
may not be globally defined because of the inelastic collapse.

If
c\in X_{0}^{c}\cup\mathcal{P}_{\text{patho.}}
, we extend the definition of the flow
T_{c,\varepsilon}
by introducing
\widetilde{T}_{c,\varepsilon}
as described in Definition
2
. We can then consider the object:

\displaystyle\varphi_{\varepsilon}=\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\varepsilon}}\big[\varphi\big(\widetilde{T}^{t}_{c,\varepsilon}(x,v)\big)\big],

(4.5)

which is properly defined and which is a function of
t
,
x
and
v
. For shortness, we omit the dependency on these variables. Then, we rewrite the quantity
\varphi_{\varepsilon}=\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\varepsilon}}\big[\varphi\big(\widetilde{T}^{t}_{c,\varepsilon}(x,v)\big)\big]
in a way that allows to identify the limiting object towards which
\varphi_{\varepsilon}
is converging in the Boltzmann-Grad limit.

Proposition 5
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
. Let
\varphi
be a
\mathcal{C}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
function. Then, the quantity
\varphi_{\varepsilon}
, defined as (
4.5
), satisfies:

\displaystyle\varphi_{\varepsilon}=\varphi_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}+R_{1},

(4.6)

with

\displaystyle\varphi_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=e^{-\mu_{\varepsilon}|[x,x+tv]+\overline{B(0,\varepsilon)}|}\varphi(x+tv,v)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}\frac{\mu_{\varepsilon}^{k}}{k!}\int_{c_{1}}\dots\int_{c_{k}}e^{-\mu_{\varepsilon}|\overline{B(x,\varepsilon)}\,\cup\,\mathcal{T}^{t}(c^{k})|}\varphi\big(T_{c,\varepsilon}^{t}(x,v)\big)

(4.7)

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt\times\mathds{1}_{X_{0}}(c^{k})\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{P}_{\text{patho.}}^{c}}(c^{k})\mathds{1}_{\{c_{i}\in B_{\varepsilon}\forall 1\leq i\leq k\}}\mathds{1}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{the obstacles}\\
c_{j},\,1\leq j\leq k,\\
\text{are internal}\end{subarray}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}c_{k}\dots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}c_{1},

where
\mathcal{T}^{t}(c^{k})
is the dynamical tube, defined in (
4.20
) below, and
R_{1}
being a remainder term such that:

\displaystyle\big|R_{1}\big|\leq{\left|\kern-1.29167pt\left|\varphi\right|\kern-1.29167pt\right|}_{\infty}\mu_{\varepsilon}|\overline{B(0,\varepsilon)}|.

(4.8)

Observe that, in principle, we have
R_{1}=R_{1}(t,x,v)
, even though the estimate (
4.8
) provides an upper bound that does not depend on the variables
t
,
x
and
v
. In the sequel, we will consider additional remainder terms, that also depend on these variables, but for which we will obtain estimates that depend also on
t
,
x
and
v
(specifically,
R_{2}
and
R_{3}
, see Propositions
6
and
7
).

Proof.

We have:

\displaystyle\varphi_{\varepsilon}=\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\varepsilon}}\big[\varphi\big(\widetilde{T}^{t}_{c,\varepsilon}(x,v)\big)\big]

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{+\infty}\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\varepsilon}}\big[\varphi\big(\widetilde{T}^{t}_{c,\varepsilon}(x,v)\big)\mathds{1}_{\#(c\,\cap\overline{B(x,\varepsilon)})=k}\big]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\varepsilon}}\big[\varphi\big(T^{t}_{c,\varepsilon}(x,v)\big)\mathds{1}_{X_{0}}(c)\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{P}_{\text{patho.}}^{c}}(c)\big]+\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\varepsilon}}\big[\varphi\big(\widetilde{T}^{t}_{c,\varepsilon}(x,v)\big)\mathds{1}_{X_{0}}(c)\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{P}_{\text{patho.}}}(c)\big]

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\varepsilon}}\big[\varphi\big(\widetilde{T}^{t}_{c,\varepsilon}(x,v)\big)\mathds{1}_{\#(c\,\cap\overline{B(x,\varepsilon)})=k}\big].

(4.9)

Denoting by
R_{1}
the sum of the two quantities:

\displaystyle R_{1}=\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\varepsilon}}\big[\varphi\big(\widetilde{T}^{t}_{c,\varepsilon}(x,v)\big)\mathds{1}_{X_{0}}(c)\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{P}_{\text{patho.}}}(c)\big]+\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\varepsilon}}\big[\varphi\big(\widetilde{T}^{t}_{c,\varepsilon}(x,v)\big)\mathds{1}_{\#(c\,\cap\overline{B(x,\varepsilon)})=k}\big],

(4.10)

we have
\big|\varphi\big(\widetilde{T}^{t}_{c,\varepsilon}(x,v)\big)\mathds{1}_{X_{0}}(c)\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{P}_{\text{patho.}}}(c)\big|\leq{\left|\kern-1.29167pt\left|\varphi\right|\kern-1.29167pt\right|}_{\infty}\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{P}_{\text{patho.}}}(c)
so that

\displaystyle\Big|\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\varepsilon}}\big[\varphi\big(\widetilde{T}^{t}_{c,\varepsilon}(x,v)\big)\mathds{1}_{X_{0}}(c)\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{P}_{\text{patho.}}}(c)\big]\Big|

\displaystyle\leq{\left|\kern-1.29167pt\left|\varphi\right|\kern-1.29167pt\right|}_{\infty}\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\varepsilon}}\big[\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{P}_{\text{patho.}}}\big]={\left|\kern-1.29167pt\left|\varphi\right|\kern-1.29167pt\right|}_{\infty}\mathbb{P}_{\mu_{\varepsilon}}\big(\mathcal{P}_{\text{patho.}}\big)=0.

(4.11)

Besides, using the properties of the Poisson process, we have:

\displaystyle\Big|\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\varepsilon}}\big[\varphi\big(\widetilde{T}^{t}_{c,\varepsilon}(x,v)\big)\mathds{1}_{\#(c\,\cap\overline{B(x,\varepsilon)})=k}\big]\Big|

\displaystyle\leq{\left|\kern-1.29167pt\left|\varphi\right|\kern-1.29167pt\right|}_{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}e^{-\mu_{\varepsilon}|\overline{B(0,\varepsilon)}|}\frac{\mu_{\varepsilon}^{k}}{k!}\big|\overline{B(0,\varepsilon)}\big|^{k}\leq{\left|\kern-1.29167pt\left|\varphi\right|\kern-1.29167pt\right|}_{\infty}\mu_{\varepsilon}|\overline{B(0,\varepsilon)}|,

(4.12)

so that in the end we obtained:

\displaystyle\big|R_{1}\big|\leq{\left|\kern-1.29167pt\left|\varphi\right|\kern-1.29167pt\right|}_{\infty}\mu_{\varepsilon}|\overline{B(0,\varepsilon)}|.

(4.13)

The second step to rewrite
\varphi_{\varepsilon}
consists in identifying a dynamical ball, and in decomposing the cases depending on the number of scatterers contained in the dynamical ball.

More precisely, considering the forward in time dynamics of the tagged particle, given by the flow
T^{t}_{c,\varepsilon}(x,v)
, since the norm of the velocity of the particle is decreasing at any collision, it is clear that the tagged particle cannot exit the ball
B(x,t|v|)
during the time interval
[0,t]
. Therefore, only the obstacles in the ball
B_{\varepsilon}
, that we define as:

\displaystyle B_{\varepsilon}=\overline{B(x,t|v|+\varepsilon)}

(4.14)

can contribute to the dynamics of the tagged particle, that is,
T^{t}_{c,\varepsilon}(x,v)
depends only on the obstacles that are in
B_{\varepsilon}
. We will call the closed ball
B_{\varepsilon}
the
dynamical ball
. We separate then the cases according to the number
n
of obstacles in
B_{\varepsilon}
, that is
n=\#\big(c\cap B_{\varepsilon}\big)
, and introducing the quantity
\varphi_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}
as follows, we obtain:

\displaystyle\varphi_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}=\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\varepsilon}}\big[\varphi\big(T^{t}_{c,\varepsilon}(x,v)\big)\mathds{1}_{X_{0}}(c)\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{P}_{\text{patho.}}^{c}}(c)\big]

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=0}^{+\infty}\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\varepsilon}}\big[\varphi\big(T^{t}_{c,\varepsilon}(x,v)\big)\mathds{1}_{X_{0}}(c)\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{P}_{\text{patho.}}^{c}}(c)\mathds{1}_{\#(c\,\cap B_{\varepsilon})=n}\big].

(4.15)

In the case when
n=0
, there is no obstacle in the dynamical ball
B_{\varepsilon}
, and therefore:

T_{c,\varepsilon}^{t}(x,v)=\big(x+tv,v\big)
. The expected value of
\varphi\big(T^{t}_{c,\varepsilon}(x,v)\big)
is independent from
c
, and by definition of a Poisson process of intensity
\mu_{\varepsilon}
we have:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\varepsilon}}\big[\varphi\big(T^{t}_{c,\varepsilon}(x,v)\big)\mathds{1}_{X_{0}}(c)\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{P}_{\text{patho.}}^{c}}(c)\mathds{1}_{\#(c\,\cap B_{\varepsilon})=0}\big]=e^{-\mu_{\varepsilon}|B_{\varepsilon}|}\varphi(x+tv,v).

(4.16)

In the general case, we find:

\displaystyle\varphi_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=e^{-\mu_{\varepsilon}|B_{\varepsilon}|}\varphi(x+tv,v)+\sum_{n=1}^{+\infty}e^{-\mu_{\varepsilon}|B_{\varepsilon}|}\frac{\mu_{\varepsilon}^{n}}{n!}\int_{c_{1}}\hskip-5.69054pt\dots\hskip-1.42262pt\int_{c_{n}}\varphi\big(T_{c,\varepsilon}^{t}(x,v)\big)\mathds{1}_{X_{0}}(c)\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{P}_{\text{patho.}}^{c}}(c)\mathds{1}_{c_{i}\in B_{\varepsilon}\forall 1\leq i\leq n}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}c_{n}\dots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}c_{1}.

The next step in the rewriting consists in separating the obstacles, between the “internal” and “external” ones. We say that
an obstacle
c_{i}
(
1\leq i\leq n
) is internal
if and only if:

\displaystyle\inf_{s\in[0,t]}\big|x(s)-c_{i}\big|=\varepsilon,

(4.17)

where
x(s)
correspond to the position variables of the inelastic hard sphere flow
T^{s}_{c,\varepsilon}(x,v)
of the tagged particle. In other words,
c_{i}
is internal if this obstacle is touched by the tagged particle during the time interval
[0,t]
.

An obstacle is said to be
external
if it is not internal.

Observe that the infimum in (
4.17
) is a minimum: since
\{T^{s}_{c,\varepsilon}(x,v)\ /\ s\in[0,t]\}
is a compact set, the infimum of the distance between this set and the compact set
\{c_{i}\}
(formed by the single point
c_{i}
) is reached for some
s_{c_{i}}\in[0,t]
. We then perform the following decomposition, separating the particular case
k=0
:

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{+\infty}e^{-\mu_{\varepsilon}|B_{\varepsilon}|}\frac{\mu_{\varepsilon}^{n}}{n!}\int_{c_{1}}\dots\int_{c_{n}}\varphi\big(T_{c,\varepsilon}^{t}(x,v)\big)\mathds{1}_{X_{0}}(c)\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{P}_{\text{patho.}}^{c}}(c)\mathds{1}_{\{c_{i}\in B_{\varepsilon}\forall 1\leq i\leq n\}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}c_{n}\dots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}c_{1}

\displaystyle\hskip-2.84526pt=\sum_{n=1}^{+\infty}e^{-\mu_{\varepsilon}|B_{\varepsilon}|}\frac{\mu_{\varepsilon}^{n}}{n!}\varphi(x+tv,v)\int_{c_{1}}\dots\int_{c_{n}}\mathds{1}_{X_{0}}(c)\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{P}_{\text{patho.}}^{c}}(c)\mathds{1}_{\{c_{i}\in B_{\varepsilon}\forall 1\leq i\leq n\}}\mathds{1}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\,\text{no obstacle}\\
\text{is internal}\end{subarray}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}c_{n}\dots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}c_{1}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\sum_{n=1}^{+\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{n}e^{-\mu_{\varepsilon}|B_{\varepsilon}|}\frac{\mu_{\varepsilon}^{n}}{n!}\frac{n!}{k!(n-k)!}\int_{c_{1}}\dots\int_{c_{n}}\varphi\big(T_{c,\varepsilon}^{t}(x,v)\big)\mathds{1}_{X_{0}}(c)\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{P}_{\text{patho.}}^{c}}(c)

\displaystyle\hskip 199.16928pt\times\mathds{1}_{\{c_{i}\in B_{\varepsilon}\forall 1\leq i\leq n\}}\mathds{1}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{only the }k\\
\text{first obstacles}\\
c_{j},\,1\leq j\leq k,\\
\text{are internal}\end{subarray}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}c_{n}\dots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}c_{1},

(4.18)

which allows in the first term to integrate with respect to obstacles, since all of them have to be outside the tube
\big\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\ /\ \exists s\in[0,t],z\in\overline{B(0,\varepsilon)}\ /\ y=x+sv+z\big\}
. We obtain:

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{+\infty}e^{-\mu_{\varepsilon}|B_{\varepsilon}|}\frac{\mu_{\varepsilon}^{n}}{n!}\int_{c_{1}}\dots\int_{c_{n}}\varphi\big(T_{c,\varepsilon}^{t}(x,v)\big)\mathds{1}_{X_{0}}(c)\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{P}_{\text{patho.}}^{c}}(c)\mathds{1}_{\{c_{i}\in B_{\varepsilon}\forall 1\leq i\leq n\}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}c_{n}\dots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}c_{1}

\displaystyle\hskip-2.84526pt=e^{-\mu_{\varepsilon}|[x,x+tv]+\overline{B(0,\varepsilon)}|}\varphi(x+tv,v)-e^{-\mu_{\varepsilon}|B_{\varepsilon}|}\varphi(x+tv,v)

(4.19)

\displaystyle\hskip-2.84526pt+\sum_{n=1}^{+\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{n}e^{-\mu_{\varepsilon}|B_{\varepsilon}|}\frac{\mu_{\varepsilon}^{n}}{k!(n-k)!}\int_{c_{1}}\dots\int_{c_{n}}\varphi\big(T_{c,\varepsilon}^{t}(x,v)\big)\mathds{1}_{X_{0}}(c)\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{P}_{\text{patho.}}^{c}}(c)\mathds{1}_{\{c_{i}\in B_{\varepsilon}\forall 1\leq i\leq n\}}\mathds{1}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{only the }k\\
\text{first obstacles}\\
c_{j},\,1\leq j\leq k,\\
\text{are internal}\end{subarray}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}c_{n}\dots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}c_{1}

By definition,
T_{c,\varepsilon}^{t}(x,v)
does not depend on the position of any of the external obstacles. We introduce the definition of the
dynamical tube
, which corresponds to the set of positions that are at a distance smaller or equal than
\varepsilon
from the trajectory of the tagged particle. In other words, we define:

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{t}(x,v;c,\varepsilon)=\big\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\ /\ \inf_{s\in[0,t]}|x_{c,\varepsilon}(s)-y|\leq\varepsilon\big\},\hskip 8.53581pt\text{that we will also denote, in short, by}\hskip 8.53581pt\mathcal{T}^{t}(c)

(4.20)

where we recall that
x_{c,\varepsilon}(s)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
corresponds to the position of the particle, namely the first component of the flow, that is
T_{c,\varepsilon}^{s}(x,v)=\big(x_{c,\varepsilon}(s),v_{c,\varepsilon}(s)\big)
(see Definition
1
). To light the notation we simply denoted the dynamical tube
\mathcal{T}^{t}(c)=\mathcal{T}^{t}(x,v;c,\varepsilon)
omitting the dependence on
\varepsilon,x,v
.

By definition, the internal obstacles belong to the (boundary of the) dynamical tube
\mathcal{T}^{t}(c)
, while the external obstacles are outside. Denoting by
c^{k}
the first
k
obstacles, that is
c^{k}=(c_{1},\dots,c_{k})
, by
c^{k+1}
the last
n-k
obstacles, that is
c^{k+1}=(c_{k+1},\dots,c_{n})
, and observing that:

\displaystyle\mathds{1}_{X_{0}}(c)

\displaystyle\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{P}^{c}_{\text{patho.}}}(c)\mathds{1}_{c_{i}\,\text{is internal }\forall\,1\leq i\leq k}\mathds{1}_{c_{j}\,\text{is external }\forall\,(k+1)\leq j\leq n}

\displaystyle=\mathds{1}_{X_{0}}(c^{k})\mathds{1}_{X_{0}}(c^{k+1})\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{P}^{c}_{\text{patho.}}}(c^{k})\mathds{1}_{c_{i}\,\text{is internal }\forall\,1\leq i\leq k}\mathds{1}_{c_{i}\in\big(\mathcal{T}^{t}(c^{k})\big)^{c}\ \forall\,(k+1)\leq i\leq n},

(4.21)

we can integrate with respect to the positions of the last scatterers, which provides:

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{+\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{n}e^{-\mu_{\varepsilon}|B_{\varepsilon}|}\frac{\mu_{\varepsilon}^{n}}{k!(n-k)!}\int_{c_{1}}\dots\int_{c_{n}}\varphi\big(T_{c,\varepsilon}^{t}(x,v)\big)\mathds{1}_{X_{0}}(c)\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{P}_{\text{patho.}}^{c}}(c)\mathds{1}_{\{c_{i}\in B_{\varepsilon}\forall 1\leq i\leq n\}}\mathds{1}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{only the }k\\
\text{first obstacles}\\
c_{j},\,1\leq j\leq k,\\
\text{are internal}\end{subarray}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}c_{n}\dots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}c_{1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{+\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{n}e^{-\mu_{\varepsilon}|B_{\varepsilon}|}\frac{\mu_{\varepsilon}^{n}}{k!(n-k)!}\int_{c_{1}}\dots\int_{c_{k}}\varphi\big(T_{c,\varepsilon}^{t}(x,v)\big)\mathds{1}_{X_{0}}(c^{k})\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{P}_{\text{patho.}}^{c}}(c^{k})\mathds{1}_{\{c_{i}\in B_{\varepsilon}\forall 1\leq i\leq k\}}\mathds{1}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{the obstacles}\\
c_{j},\,1\leq j\leq k,\\
\text{are internal}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\times\Bigg[\int_{c_{k+1}}\hskip-5.69054pt\dots\int_{c_{n}}\mathds{1}_{X_{0}}(c^{k+1})\mathds{1}_{\{c_{i}\in B_{\varepsilon}\forall(k+1)\leq i\leq n\}}\mathds{1}_{c_{j}\in\big(\mathcal{T}^{t}(c^{k})\big)^{c}\ \forall\,(k+1)\leq j\leq n}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}c_{n}\dots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}c_{k+1}\Bigg]\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}c_{k}\dots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}c_{1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{+\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{n}e^{-\mu_{\varepsilon}|B_{\varepsilon}|}\frac{\mu_{\varepsilon}^{n}}{k!(n-k)!}\int_{c_{1}}\dots\int_{c_{k}}\varphi\big(T_{c,\varepsilon}^{t}(x,v)\big)\mathds{1}_{X_{0}}(c^{k})\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{P}_{\text{patho.}}^{c}}(c^{k})\mathds{1}_{\{c_{i}\in B_{\varepsilon}\forall 1\leq i\leq k\}}\mathds{1}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{the obstacles}\\
c_{j},\,1\leq j\leq k,\\
\text{are internal}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\hskip 227.62204pt\times\Big|B_{\varepsilon}\backslash\big(\overline{B(x,\varepsilon)}\cup\mathcal{T}^{t}(c^{k})\big)\Big|^{n-k}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}c_{k}\dots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}c_{1}.

(4.22)

Inverting the two sums, and performing the change of variable
m=n-k
we find:

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{+\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{n}e^{-\mu_{\varepsilon}|B_{\varepsilon}|}\frac{\mu_{\varepsilon}^{n}}{k!(n-k)!}\int_{c_{1}}\dots\int_{c_{n}}\varphi\big(T_{c,\varepsilon}^{t}(x,v)\big)\mathds{1}_{X_{0}}(c)\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{P}_{\text{patho.}}^{c}}(c)\mathds{1}_{\{c_{i}\in B_{\varepsilon}\forall 1\leq i\leq n\}}\mathds{1}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{only the }k\\
\text{first obstacles}\\
c_{j},\,1\leq j\leq k,\\
\text{are internal}\end{subarray}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}c_{n}\dots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}c_{1}

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt=\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}\sum_{m=0}^{+\infty}e^{-\mu_{\varepsilon}|B_{\varepsilon}|}\frac{\mu_{\varepsilon}^{k}\mu_{\varepsilon}^{m}}{k!\,m!}\int_{c_{1}}\dots\int_{c_{k}}\varphi\big(T_{c,\varepsilon}^{t}(x,v)\big)\mathds{1}_{X_{0}}(c^{k})\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{P}_{\text{patho.}}^{c}}(c^{k})\mathds{1}_{\{c_{i}\in B_{\varepsilon}\forall 1\leq i\leq k\}}\mathds{1}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{the obstacles}\\
c_{j},\,1\leq j\leq k,\\
\text{are internal}\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\hskip 241.84842pt\times\Big|B_{\varepsilon}\backslash\big(\overline{B(x,\varepsilon)}\cup\mathcal{T}^{t}(c^{k})\big)\Big|^{m}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}c_{k}\dots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}c_{1}.

(4.23)

Regrouping the terms that depend on the index
m
in order to identify an exponential series, we obtain:

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{+\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{n}e^{-\mu_{\varepsilon}|B_{\varepsilon}|}\frac{\mu_{\varepsilon}^{n}}{k!(n-k)!}\int_{c_{1}}\dots\int_{c_{n}}\varphi\big(T_{c,\varepsilon}^{t}(x,v)\big)\mathds{1}_{X_{0}}(c)\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{P}_{\text{patho.}}^{c}}(c)\mathds{1}_{\{c_{i}\in B_{\varepsilon}\forall 1\leq i\leq n\}}\mathds{1}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{only the }k\\
\text{first obstacles}\\
c_{j},\,1\leq j\leq k,\\
\text{are internal}\end{subarray}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}c_{n}\dots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}c_{1}

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt=\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}\frac{\mu_{\varepsilon}^{k}}{k!}\int_{c_{1}}\dots\int_{c_{k}}e^{-\mu_{\varepsilon}|\overline{B(x,\varepsilon)}\,\cup\,\mathcal{T}^{t}(c^{k})|}\varphi\big(T_{c,\varepsilon}^{t}(x,v)\big)\mathds{1}_{X_{0}}(c^{k})

\displaystyle\hskip 199.16928pt\times\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{P}_{\text{patho.}}^{c}}(c^{k})\mathds{1}_{\{c_{i}\in B_{\varepsilon}\forall 1\leq i\leq k\}}\mathds{1}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{the obstacles}\\
c_{j},\,1\leq j\leq k,\\
\text{are internal}\end{subarray}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}c_{k}\dots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}c_{1}.

(4.24)

\varphi_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}
being the sum of the term
e^{-\mu_{\varepsilon}|B_{\varepsilon}|}\varphi(x+tv,v)
and of the series rewritten in (
4.2
), this concludes the proof of Proposition
5
.
∎

4.3
Comparing the series representation of
\varphi_{\varepsilon}
with the solution of the adjoint equation

In order to compare solutions to the adjoint equation with the expression (
5
) of
\varphi_{\varepsilon}=\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\varepsilon}}\big[\varphi\big(\widetilde{T}_{c,\varepsilon}^{t}(x,v)\big)\big]
given by Proposition
5
, we rely on the series description of the solutions to the adjoint equation (
2.31
) provided by Proposition
1
.

To this aim, we consider (
5
) (see Proposition
5
) and (
1
) (see Proposition
1
) and estimate the difference between these two expressions. We will now compare the two quantities:

\displaystyle T_{c,\varepsilon}^{t}(x,v)\hskip 14.22636pt\text{and}\hskip 14.22636pt\big(x+\sum_{m=1}^{k}\big(t_{m-1}-t_{m}\big)v^{(m-1)}+t_{k}v^{(k)},v^{(k)}\big),

(4.25)

that are the respective variables of the initial datum
\varphi
in (
5
) and (
1
).

There are two main differences between these two trajectories. On the one hand, the trajectory
T_{c,\varepsilon}^{t}(x,v)
of the tagged particle, evolving among obstacles with a positive size, might experience
recollisions
. In other words, some obstacles can be collided several times, inducing correlations. The microscopic dynamics is therefore non-Markovian.

On the other hand, we consider the trajectory
x+\sum_{m=1}^{k}\big(t_{m-1}-t_{m}\big)v^{(m-1)}+t_{k}v^{(k)}
associated to the adjoint Boltzmann equation, which is the limit stochastic trajectory of the microscopic Lorentz process. We will call such a limit trajectory a
pseudo-trajectory
. We first observe that no recollision can take place in such pseudo-trajectories, in the sense that the parameters
t_{m}
and
\omega_{m}
are always chosen independently from each other. This exhibits the memoryless nature of the process which generates such trajectories. In addition, we observe that the position of an “obstacle”, for a trajectory associated to the adjoint equation, can be exactly on a part of the trajectory that connects two other obstacles which have been collided before. In other words, we may have:

\displaystyle x(n)\in[x(p),x(p+1)]\hskip 14.22636pt\text{for}\hskip 14.22636ptn>p+1,

(4.26)

where:

\displaystyle x(n)=x+\sum_{m=1}^{n}\big(t_{m-1}-t_{m}\big)v^{(m-1)}.

(4.27)

Such a phenomenon is called an
interference
. It is important to observe that such a phenomenon cannot take place at the level of the particle system, with obstacles of a positive size. Indeed, a third obstacle cannot lie on the segment between two consecutive obstacles that are collided: the tagged particle would have collided with this third obstacle, violating the definition of the two consecutively collided obstacles.

In summary, the parametrizations of the pseudo-trajectories and the inelastic hard sphere flow present significant difference. The parametrization of the pseudo-trajectories allows to consider dynamics that cannot be achieved by a particle evolving according to the hard sphere flow, for which no interference might take place. Conversely, the parametrization of the hard sphere flow encodes also trajectories that present recollisions, which is not the case for the parametrization of the pseudo-trajectories.

Nevertheless, both interferences and recollisions correspond to extremely rare situtations in the low density limit. The purpose of Proposition
6
that follows is to estimate precisely the subsets of the domains of the integrals in (
5
) leading either to an interference or to a recollision, so that
\varphi_{\varepsilon}=\mathbb{E}_{\mu_{\varepsilon}}\big[\varphi\big(\widetilde{T}_{c,\varepsilon}^{t}(x,v)\big)\big]
and
\widetilde{\varphi}
, solution of the adjoint equation, can be easily compared on the complement of such pathological sets.

Proposition 6

(Elimination of the recollisions)
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
. Let
c
be a
d
-dimensional Poisson process of intensity
\mu_{\varepsilon}>0
such that the
Boltzmann-Grad
scaling (
2.16
) holds. Then, there exists a universal constant
\varepsilon_{0}>0
and two positive constants
\widetilde{C}_{1}=\widetilde{C}_{1}(d,r)
and
\widetilde{C}_{2}=\widetilde{C}_{2}(d,r)
that depend only on the dimension
d
and the restitution coefficient
r
such that, for any
0<\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}
, and any
\mathcal{C}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
function
\varphi
, the quantity
\varphi_{\varepsilon}
, defined as (
4.5
), satisfies:

\displaystyle\varphi_{\varepsilon}=\varphi_{\varepsilon}^{(2)}+R_{1}+R_{2}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{with}\hskip 14.22636pt\varphi_{\varepsilon}^{(2)}=\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}\varphi_{\varepsilon,k}^{(2)}(k)

(4.28)

and with
\varphi_{\varepsilon,k}^{(2)}(k)
defined as

\displaystyle\varphi_{\varepsilon,k}^{(2)}(k)

\displaystyle=e^{-\mu_{\varepsilon}|[x,x+tv]+\overline{B(0,\varepsilon)}|}\varphi(x+tv,v)

\displaystyle\hskip 8.53581pt+\int_{t_{1}=0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\omega_{1}}}\mathds{1}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{1}}^{c}}\int_{t_{2}=0}^{t_{1}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\omega_{2}}}\mathds{1}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{2}}^{c}}\dots\int_{t_{k}=0}^{t_{k-1}}\int_{\mathbb{S}_{\omega_{k}}^{d-1}}\mathds{1}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{k}}^{c}}e^{-\mu_{\varepsilon}|\overline{B(x,\varepsilon)}\,\cup\,\mathcal{T}^{t}(c^{k})|}\prod_{l=1}^{k}|v^{(l-1)}\cdot\omega_{l}|

\displaystyle\hskip 96.02805pt\times\varphi\big(x+\sum_{m=1}^{k}\big(t_{m-1}-t_{m}\big)v^{(m-1)}+t_{k}v^{(k)},v^{(k)}\big)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{k}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{k}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{1},

(4.29)

R_{1}
as in (
4.8
), and
R_{2}
being a remainder term such that:

\displaystyle\big|R_{2}\big|\leq{\left|\kern-1.29167pt\left|\varphi\right|\kern-1.29167pt\right|}_{\infty}\widetilde{C}_{1}\big(1+\max(1,t)|v|\big)e^{\widetilde{C}_{2}t|v|}\varepsilon^{1/4}.

(4.30)

Proof.

We restart from the main term
\varphi_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}
of
\varphi_{\varepsilon}
given by Proposition
5
. First, we observe that, up to relabel the obstacles, we can always order them, in the following sense:

—

c_{1}
is the first obstacle that is collided by the tagged particle evolving according to the trajectory
T_{c,\varepsilon}^{s}(x,v)
of the inelastic hard sphere flow, that is:

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274ptx(t-t_{1})\in\overline{B(c_{1},\varepsilon)}\hskip 5.69054pt\text{for a certain}\hskip 5.69054pt0<t_{1}<t\hskip 14.22636pt\text{and}\hskip 14.22636ptt-t_{1}=\min\{s\in[0,t]\ /\ T_{c,\varepsilon}^{s}(x,v)\in c+\overline{B(0,\varepsilon)}\},

(4.31)

—

c_{2}
is the next first obstacle to be collided by the tagged particle:

\displaystyle\hskip-22.76219ptx(t-t_{2})\in\overline{B(c_{2},\varepsilon)}\hskip 5.69054pt\text{for a certain}\hskip 5.69054pt0<t_{2}<t_{1}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{and}\hskip 14.22636ptt-t_{2}=\min\{s\in[0,t]\ /\ T_{c,\varepsilon}^{s}(x,v)\in\big(c\backslash\{c_{1}\}\big)+\overline{B(0,\varepsilon)}\}.

(4.32)

—

Similarly, we order the other obstacles:

\displaystyle x(t-t_{j})\in\overline{B(c_{j},\varepsilon)}\hskip 5.69054pt\text{for a certain}\hskip 5.69054pt0<t_{j}<t_{j-1}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\text{and}\hskip 14.22636ptt-t_{j}=\min\{s\in[0,t]\ /\ T_{c,\varepsilon}^{s}(x,v)\in\big(c\backslash\{c_{1},\dots,c_{j-1}\}\big)+\overline{B(0,\varepsilon)}\}.

(4.33)

Observe that, a priori, the obstacles
c_{1},\dots,c_{j}
might be collided more than once by the tagged particle before
t-t_{j+1}
. Nevertheless, it is impossible in the case of
c_{1}
before
t-t_{2}
: indeed, after the first collision with
c_{1}
, the tagged particle is in a post-collisional configuration with
c_{1}
, and the tagged particle cannot collide by definition with any other scatterer before
t-t_{2}
.

Since in the expression (
5
) of
\varphi_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}
the obstacles are not ordered, we obtain
k!
similar terms after the relabelling in the case of
k
internal obstacles. Therefore we have:

\displaystyle\varphi_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=e^{-\mu_{\varepsilon}|[x,x+tv]+\overline{B(0,\varepsilon)}|}\varphi(x+tv,v)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}\mu_{\varepsilon}^{k}\int_{c_{1}}\dots\int_{c_{k}}e^{-\mu_{\varepsilon}|\overline{B(x,\varepsilon)}\,\cup\,\mathcal{T}^{t}(c^{k})|}\varphi\big(T_{c,\varepsilon}^{t}(x,v)\big)\mathds{1}_{X_{0}}(c^{k})\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{P}_{\text{patho.}}^{c}}(c^{k})\mathds{1}_{\{c_{i}\in B_{\varepsilon}\forall\,1\leq i\leq k\}}

\displaystyle\hskip 199.16928pt\times\mathds{1}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{the obstacles}\\
c_{j},\,1\leq j\leq k,\\
\text{are internal}\end{subarray}}(c^{k})\mathds{1}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{the $l$-th collided}\\
\text{obstacle is }c_{l}\\
\forall\,1\leq l\leq k\end{subarray}}(c^{k})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}c_{k}\dots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}c_{1}.

(4.34)

We will denote by
\varphi_{\varepsilon}^{(2)}(k)
(
k\geq 1
) the different terms in the previous series, that is
\varphi_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}=e^{-\mu_{\varepsilon}|[x,x+tv]+\overline{B(0,\varepsilon)}|}

\times\varphi(x+tv,v)+\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}\varphi_{\varepsilon}^{(2)}(k)
with

\displaystyle\varphi_{\varepsilon}^{(2)}(k)

\displaystyle=\mu_{\varepsilon}^{k}\int_{c_{1}}\dots\int_{c_{k}}e^{-\mu_{\varepsilon}|\overline{B(x,\varepsilon)}\,\cup\,\mathcal{T}^{t}(c^{k})|}\varphi\big(T_{c,\varepsilon}^{t}(x,v)\big)\mathds{1}_{X_{0}}(c^{k})\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{P}_{\text{patho.}}^{c}}(c^{k})\mathds{1}_{\{c_{i}\in B_{\varepsilon}\forall\,1\leq i\leq k\}}

\displaystyle\hskip 170.71652pt\times\mathds{1}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{the obstacles}\\
c_{j},\,1\leq j\leq k,\\
\text{are internal}\end{subarray}}(c^{k})\mathds{1}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{the $l$-th collided}\\
\text{obstacle is }c_{l}\\
\forall\,1\leq l\leq k\end{subarray}}(c^{k})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}c_{k}\dots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}c_{1}.

(4.35)

Reparametrization of the positions of the scatterers.

After the relabelling,
c_{1}
is the center of the first obstacle collided by the tagged particle, at time
t-t_{1}
. Therefore, before this time the trajectory of the tagged particle is the free flow:

\displaystyle T_{c,\varepsilon}^{s}(x,v)=\big(x+sv,v\big)\hskip 8.53581pt\forall\ s\in[0,t-t_{1}[.

(4.36)

In addition, the position
c_{1}
can be parametrized as follows:

\displaystyle c_{1}=x+(t-t_{1})v-\varepsilon\omega_{1},

(4.37)

for a certain
\omega_{1}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
such that
\omega_{1}\cdot v\leq 0
(because if the scalar product is positive, there exists a smaller time
s
such that
x(s)=x+sv
belongs to
\overline{B(c_{1},\varepsilon)}
, which is absurd). Provided that
\omega_{1}\cdot v\leq 0
, observe that any choice of the parameters
(t_{1},\omega_{1})\in\ ]0,t[\times\,\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
corresponds to an admissible choice for the position
c_{1}
of the first scatterer. In particular, the condition
c_{1}\notin\overline{B(x,\varepsilon)}
systematically holds true.

Based on (
4.37
), we can perform the change of variables
c_{1}\rightarrow c_{1}(t_{1},\omega_{1})
. The Jacobian determinant of this change of variables is:

\displaystyle\text{J}c_{1}(t_{1},\omega_{1})=\varepsilon^{d-1}\big|v\cdot\omega_{1}\big|.

(4.38)

We find therefore:

\displaystyle\varphi_{\varepsilon}^{(2)}(k)

\displaystyle=\mu_{\varepsilon}^{k}\int_{t_{1}=0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\omega_{1}}}\mathds{1}_{\omega_{1}\cdot v\leq 0}\int_{c_{2}}\dots\int_{c_{k}}e^{-\mu_{\varepsilon}|\overline{B(x,\varepsilon)}\,\cup\,\mathcal{T}^{t}(c^{k})|}\varphi\big(T_{c,\varepsilon}^{t}(x,v)\big)\mathds{1}_{X_{0}}(c^{k})\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{P}_{\text{patho.}}^{c}}(c^{k})\mathds{1}_{\{c_{i}\in B_{\varepsilon}\forall\,2\leq i\leq k\}}

\displaystyle\hskip 119.50157pt\times\mathds{1}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{the obstacles}\\
c_{j},\,2\leq j\leq k,\\
\text{are internal}\end{subarray}}(c^{k})\mathds{1}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{the $l$-th collided}\\
\text{obstacle is }c_{l}\\
\forall\,2\leq l\leq k\end{subarray}}(c^{k})\varepsilon^{d-1}|v\cdot\omega_{1}|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}c_{k}\dots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}c_{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{1}.

(4.39)

The cut-off concerning the integration variable
\omega_{1}
.

In order to estimate more easily the pathological positions
c_{2}
of the second scatterer, we define the following subset of the positions
c_{1}
of the first scatterer:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{1}}=\{\omega_{1}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\ /\ \omega_{1}\cdot v\leq 0\ \text{and}\ 1-\Big|\frac{v^{\prime}}{|v^{\prime}|}\cdot\frac{v}{|v|}\Big|\leq\varepsilon^{\delta_{1}}\},

(4.40)

for a certain constant
\delta_{1}>0
that will be chosen later. We denote the complement of
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{1}}
by
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{1}}^{c}
that is
\{\omega_{1}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}\ /\ \omega_{1}\cdot v\leq 0\}=\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{1}}\cup\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{1}}^{c}
, and decompose then:

\displaystyle\varphi_{\varepsilon}^{(2)}(k)

\displaystyle=\mu_{\varepsilon}^{k}\int_{t_{1}=0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\omega_{1}}}\mathds{1}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{1}}^{c}}\int_{c_{2}}\dots\int_{c_{k}}e^{-\mu_{\varepsilon}|\overline{B(x,\varepsilon)}\,\cup\,\mathcal{T}^{t}(c^{k})|}\varphi\big(T_{c,\varepsilon}^{t}(x,v)\big)\mathds{1}_{X_{0}}(c^{k})\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{P}_{\text{patho.}}^{c}}(c^{k})\mathds{1}_{\{c_{i}\in B_{\varepsilon}\forall\,2\leq i\leq k\}}

\displaystyle\hskip 119.50157pt\times\mathds{1}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{the obstacles}\\
c_{j},\,2\leq j\leq k,\\
\text{are internal}\end{subarray}}(c^{k})\mathds{1}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{the $l$-th collided}\\
\text{obstacle is }c_{l}\\
\forall\,2\leq l\leq k\end{subarray}}(c^{k})\varepsilon^{d-1}|v\cdot\omega_{1}|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}c_{k}\dots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}c_{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{1}

\displaystyle\hskip 8.53581pt+\mu_{\varepsilon}^{k}\int_{t_{1}=0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\omega_{1}}}\mathds{1}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{1}}}\int_{c_{2}}\dots\int_{c_{k}}e^{-\mu_{\varepsilon}|\overline{B(x,\varepsilon)}\,\cup\,\mathcal{T}^{t}(c^{k})|}\varphi\big(T_{c,\varepsilon}^{t}(x,v)\big)\mathds{1}_{X_{0}}(c^{k})\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{P}_{\text{patho.}}^{c}}(c^{k})\mathds{1}_{\{c_{i}\in B_{\varepsilon}\forall\,2\leq i\leq k\}}

\displaystyle\hskip 119.50157pt\times\mathds{1}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{the obstacles}\\
c_{j},\,2\leq j\leq k,\\
\text{are internal}\end{subarray}}(c^{k})\mathds{1}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{the $l$-th collided}\\
\text{obstacle is }c_{l}\\
\forall\,2\leq l\leq k\end{subarray}}(c^{k})\varepsilon^{d-1}|v\cdot\omega_{1}|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}c_{k}\dots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}c_{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{1},

(4.41)

the second term corresponding to a pathological choice of scatterer positions, that we will estimate later. This second term will be denoted by
\varphi_{\varepsilon,c_{1}}^{(2),\text{patho.}}(k)
, while the first term will be denoted by
\varphi_{\varepsilon,1}^{(2)}(k)
, so that
\varphi_{\varepsilon}^{(2)}(k)=\varphi_{\varepsilon,1}^{(2)}(k)+\varphi_{\varepsilon,c_{1}}^{(2),\text{patho.}}(k)
.

Concerning the position of the second obstacle, since no other collision can happen on
[0,t-t_{2}[
except the only collision, at time
t-t_{1}
, between the tagged particle and
c_{1}
, we deduce that
c_{2}
necessarily writes:

\displaystyle c_{2}=x+(t-t_{1})v+(t_{1}-t_{2})v^{\prime}-\varepsilon\omega_{2},

(4.42)

for certain
t_{2}\in\ ]0,t_{1}[
and
\omega_{2}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
such that
\omega_{2}\cdot v^{\prime}\leq 0
. This time, not all the choices of the parameters
(t_{2},\omega_{2})
lead to an admissible position
c_{2}
for the second scatterer. Indeed, one needs to ensure that:

\displaystyle c_{2}\notin\overline{B(x,\varepsilon)},\hskip 14.22636ptd\big(c_{2},T_{c,\varepsilon}^{s}(x,v)\big)>\varepsilon\ \forall s\in[0,t-t_{1}],

(4.43)

the first condition corresponding to the fact that the tagged particle is positionned initially outside the scatterer centered on
c_{2}
, while the second condition describes the absence of interference.

The cut-off concerning the integration variables
t_{2}
and
\omega_{2}
.

Let us denote by
\mathcal{A}_{c_{2}}\subset\ ]0,t_{1}[\times\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
the set of parameters
(t_{2},\omega_{2})
such that the conditions (
4.43
) are satisfied, as well as the other conditions already described by the product of the indicator functions:

\displaystyle\mathds{1}_{X_{0}}(c^{k})\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{P}_{\text{patho.}}^{c}}(c^{k})\mathds{1}_{\{c_{i}\in B_{\varepsilon}\forall\,2\leq i\leq k\}}\mathds{1}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{the obstacles}\\
c_{j},\,2\leq j\leq k,\\
\text{are internal}\end{subarray}}(c^{k})\mathds{1}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{the $l$-th collided}\\
\text{obstacle is }c_{l}\\
\forall\,2\leq l\leq k\end{subarray}}(c^{k}).

In particular, we have
c_{k}\in\mathcal{P}_{\text{patho.}}^{c}
. We perform now the change of variables
c_{2}\rightarrow c_{2}(t_{2},\omega_{2})
, of Jacobian determinant
|v^{\prime}\cdot\omega_{2}|
, and the first term
\varphi_{\varepsilon,1}^{(2)}(k)
of
\varphi_{\varepsilon}^{(2)}(k)
can be written as:

\displaystyle\varphi_{\varepsilon,1}^{(2)}(k)

\displaystyle=\mu_{\varepsilon}^{k}\int_{t_{1}=0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\omega_{1}}}\mathds{1}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{1}}^{c}}\int_{t_{2}=0}^{t_{1}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\omega_{2}}}\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{A}_{c_{2}}}\int_{c_{3}}\dots\int_{c_{k}}e^{-\mu_{\varepsilon}|\overline{B(x,\varepsilon)}\,\cup\,\mathcal{T}^{t}(c^{k})|}\varphi\big(T_{c,\varepsilon}^{t}(x,v)\big)\mathds{1}_{X_{0}}(c^{k})

\displaystyle\hskip 128.0374pt\times\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{P}_{\text{patho.}}^{c}}(c^{k})\mathds{1}_{\{c_{i}\in B_{\varepsilon}\forall\,3\leq i\leq k\}}\mathds{1}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{the obstacles}\\
c_{j},\,3\leq j\leq k,\\
\text{are internal}\end{subarray}}(c^{k})\mathds{1}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{the $l$-th collided}\\
\text{obstacle is }c_{l}\\
\forall\,3\leq l\leq k\end{subarray}}(c^{k})

\displaystyle\hskip 170.71652pt\times\varepsilon^{2(d-1)}|v\cdot\omega_{1}|\cdot|v^{\prime}\cdot\omega_{2}|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}c_{k}\dots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}c_{3}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{1}.

(4.44)

From now on, we will not undertake to describe precisely the pathological parameters leading to recollisions or interferences. We will only estimate them, by introducing larger sets, such that outside those sets, the parameters will lead to well-defined dynamics as a consequence of elementary and direct arguments.

In the case of
c_{2}
, we decompose the set of parameters
\mathcal{A}_{c_{2}}
as follows. We introduce:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{2}}^{1}=\{(t_{2},\omega_{2})\in\mathcal{A}_{c_{2}}\ /\ t_{1}-t_{2}<\frac{\varepsilon^{\delta_{2}}}{r|v|}\}.

(4.45)

\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{2}}^{1}
is the set such that the obstacles
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
are close, with
\delta_{2}>0
a constant to be chosen later. We introduce also:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{2}}^{2}=\{(t_{2},\omega_{2})\in\mathcal{A}_{c_{2}}\ /\ x+(t-t_{1})v+(t_{1}-t_{2})v^{\prime}-\varepsilon\omega_{2}\in\overline{B(x,\varepsilon)}\},

(4.46)

\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{2}}^{2}
is the set such that the tagged particle would be initially situated inside the second scatterer. And finally:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{2}}^{3}=\{(t_{2},\omega_{2})\in\mathcal{A}_{c_{2}}\ /\ x+(t-t_{1})v+(t_{1}-t_{2})v^{\prime}-\varepsilon\omega_{2}\in[x,x+(t-t_{1})v]+\overline{B(0,\varepsilon)}\}.

(4.47)

\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{2}}^{3}
is the set such that the second obstacle lies in between the initial position of the first particle and its position when it collides with the first scatterer. In other words, this set corresponds to an interference.

Observe that no recollision can occur before the collision with the second scatterer
c_{2}
, because the tagged particle is in post-collisional configuration with the first scatterer on the time interval
[t-t_{1},t-t_{2}]
. Nevertheless, recollisions might occur after the collision with the second scatterer. For instance, the tagged particle can collide once again with
c_{1}
after the time
t-t_{2}
. We will remove such a possibility by prescribing a condition on the velocity
v^{(2)}
of the tagged particle after its collision with
c_{2}
. Since the velocity
v^{(2)}
of the tagged particle after its collision with
c_{2}
depends on the angular parameter
\omega_{2}
, we will impose a condition on this parameter, by introducing the following set:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{2}}^{4}=\{(t_{2},\omega_{2})\in\mathcal{A}_{c_{2}}\ /\ 1-\Big|\frac{v^{\prime\prime}}{|v^{\prime\prime}|}\cdot\frac{c_{2}-c_{1}}{|c_{2}-c_{1}|}\Big|\leq\varepsilon^{\delta_{3}}\},

(4.48)

with
\delta_{3}>0
a positive constant to be chosen later.

Finally, we perform a last cut-off to prevent the trajectory to present segments between two consecutive collisions that are almost parallel, which will ensure that the time intervals during which the interfence may take place are small.

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{2}}^{5}=\{(t_{2},\omega_{2})\in\mathcal{A}_{c_{2}}\ /\ 1-\Big|\frac{v^{\prime\prime}}{|v^{\prime\prime}|}\cdot\frac{v}{|v|}\Big|\leq\varepsilon^{\delta_{1}}\hskip 8.53581pt\text{and}\hskip 8.53581pt1-\Big|\frac{v^{\prime\prime}}{|v^{\prime\prime}|}\cdot\frac{v^{\prime}}{|v^{\prime}|}\Big|\leq\varepsilon^{\delta_{1}}\},

(4.49)

In the end, we define:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{2}}=\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{2}}^{1}\cup\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{2}}^{2}\cup\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{2}}^{3}\cup\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{2}}^{4}\cup\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{2}}^{5},\hskip 14.22636pt\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{2}}^{c}=\mathcal{A}_{c_{2}}\backslash\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{2}},

(4.50)

and two integrals
\varphi_{\varepsilon,c_{2}}^{(2),\text{patho.}}(k)
and
\varphi_{\varepsilon,2}^{(2)}(k)
, which differ from each other only by the indicator functions
\mathds{1}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{2}}}
and
\mathds{1}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}^{c}_{c_{2}}}
concerning the integration variables
(t_{2},\omega_{2})
:

\displaystyle\varphi_{\varepsilon,c_{2}}^{(2),\text{patho.}}(k)

\displaystyle=\mu_{\varepsilon}^{k}\int_{t_{1}=0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\omega_{1}}}\mathds{1}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{1}}^{c}}\int_{t_{2}=0}^{t_{1}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\omega_{2}}}\mathds{1}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{2}}}\int_{c_{3}}\dots\int_{c_{k}}e^{-\mu_{\varepsilon}|\overline{B(x,\varepsilon)}\,\cup\,\mathcal{T}^{t}(c^{k})|}\varphi\big(T_{c,\varepsilon}^{t}(x,v)\big)\mathds{1}_{X_{0}}(c^{k})

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt\times\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{P}_{\text{patho.}}^{c}}(c^{k})\mathds{1}_{\{c_{i}\in B_{\varepsilon}\forall\,3\leq i\leq k\}}\mathds{1}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{the obstacles}\\
c_{j},\,3\leq j\leq k,\\
\text{are internal}\end{subarray}}(c^{k})\mathds{1}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{the $l$-th collided}\\
\text{obstacle is }c_{l}\\
\forall\,3\leq l\leq k\end{subarray}}(c^{k})

\displaystyle\hskip 156.49014pt\times\varepsilon^{2(d-1)}|v\cdot\omega_{1}|\cdot|v^{\prime}\cdot\omega_{2}|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}c_{k}\dots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}c_{3}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{1}

(4.51)

and:

\displaystyle\varphi_{\varepsilon,2}^{(2)}(k)

\displaystyle=\mu_{\varepsilon}^{k}\int_{t_{1}=0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\omega_{1}}}\mathds{1}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{1}}^{c}}\int_{t_{2}=0}^{t_{1}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\omega_{2}}}\mathds{1}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{2}}^{c}}\int_{c_{3}}\dots\int_{c_{k}}e^{-\mu_{\varepsilon}|\overline{B(x,\varepsilon)}\,\cup\,\mathcal{T}^{t}(c^{k})|}\varphi\big(T_{c,\varepsilon}^{t}(x,v)\big)\mathds{1}_{X_{0}}(c^{k})

\displaystyle\hskip 128.0374pt\times\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{P}_{\text{patho.}}^{c}}(c^{k})\mathds{1}_{\{c_{i}\in B_{\varepsilon}\forall\,3\leq i\leq k\}}\mathds{1}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{the obstacles}\\
c_{j},\,3\leq j\leq k,\\
\text{are internal}\end{subarray}}(c^{k})\mathds{1}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{the $l$-th collided}\\
\text{obstacle is }c_{l}\\
\forall\,3\leq l\leq k\end{subarray}}(c^{k})

\displaystyle\hskip 170.71652pt\times\varepsilon^{2(d-1)}|v\cdot\omega_{1}|\cdot|v^{\prime}\cdot\omega_{2}|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}c_{k}\dots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}c_{3}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{1}.

(4.52)

We have then:

\displaystyle\varphi_{\varepsilon,1}^{(2)}(k)=\varphi_{\varepsilon,2}^{(2)}(k)+\varphi_{\varepsilon,c_{2}}^{(2),\text{patho.}}(k).

(4.53)

Eliminating the recollisions.

We will apply the same cut-off procedure for all the other scatterers
c_{j}
, with
j\geq 3
. This procedure presents an additional step with respect to the case of
c_{2}
. Let us present in detail the case of the third scatterer
c_{3}
, the case when
j\geq 4
being exactly the same.

In the case of
c_{3}
, the hard sphere transport between
t-t_{2}
(the first time of collision with the second scatterer) and
t-t_{3}
(the first time of collision with the third scatterer) may not be only given by the free transport: the tagged particle may collide several time between the two first scatterers before reaching the third one. In this case, the parametrization of the position
c_{3}
of this third scatterer becomes intricate, and it is a priori not clear how to proceed with the change of variables.

We will now choose the constants
\delta_{1}
and
\delta_{3}
(introduced respectively in (
4.40
), (
4.49
) and (
4.48
)) and the parameter
\varepsilon
such that if
(t_{2},\omega_{2})\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{2}}^{c}
, then no recollision can take place during the time interval
]t-t_{2},t-t_{3}[
. In this case, the position
c_{3}
of the third scatterer writes:

\displaystyle c_{3}=x+(t-t_{1})v+(t_{1}-t_{2})v^{\prime}+(t_{2}-t_{3})v^{\prime\prime}-\varepsilon\omega_{3},

(4.54)

for certain
t_{3}\in\ ]0,t_{2}[
and
\omega_{3}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-2}
such that
\omega_{3}\cdot v^{\prime\prime}\leq 0
. We can always parametrize
c_{3}
as in (
4.54
). Nevertheless, at this step it might be that this piecewise affine parametrization does not correspond to the trajectory of the tagged particle, because of the possible recollisions. By construction the distance between
c_{1}
and
x(t-t_{2})
satisfies:

\displaystyle|c_{2}-c_{1}|

\displaystyle=\big|x+(t-t_{1})v+(t_{1}-t_{2})v^{\prime}-\varepsilon\omega_{2}-x-(t-t_{1})v+\varepsilon\omega_{1}\big|

\displaystyle\geq|(t_{1}-t_{2})v^{\prime}|-2\varepsilon\geq\varepsilon^{\delta_{2}}-2\varepsilon\geq\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon^{\delta_{2}},

(4.55)

for
0<\delta_{2}<1
and
\varepsilon
smaller than a certain
\varepsilon_{1}=\varepsilon_{1}(\delta_{2})
that depends only on
\delta_{2}
. On the other hand, if we assume that a recollision takes place, there exists a time
\tau
such that:

\displaystyle x(t-\tau)=x+(t-t_{1})v+(t_{1}-t_{2})v^{\prime}+(t_{2}-\tau)v^{\prime\prime}=c_{1}+\varepsilon\sigma

(4.56)

for a certain
\sigma\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
. We observe then that, since:

\displaystyle x(t-\tau)-x(t-t_{2})=c_{1}+\varepsilon\sigma-c_{2}-\varepsilon\omega_{2},

(4.57)

we have:

\displaystyle\frac{c_{1}-c_{2}}{|c_{1}-c_{2}|}\cdot\frac{x(t-\tau)-x(t-t_{2})}{|x(t-\tau)-x(t-t_{2})|}

\displaystyle=\frac{|c_{1}-c_{2}|+\varepsilon(\sigma-\omega_{2})\cdot\frac{c_{1}-c_{2}}{|c_{1}-c_{2}|}}{|c_{1}-c_{2}+\varepsilon\sigma-\varepsilon\omega_{2}|}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{|c_{1}-c_{2}|-2\varepsilon}{|c_{1}-c_{2}|+2\varepsilon}\geq 1-4\frac{\varepsilon}{|c_{1}-c_{2}|}\geq 1-8\varepsilon^{1-\delta_{2}},

(4.58)

assuming that
\varepsilon/|c_{1}-c_{2}|<1
, which is the case for
\varepsilon
small enough. As a consequence, since
x(t-\tau)-x(t-t_{2})=(t_{2}-\tau)v^{\prime\prime}
, if the direction of
x(t-\tau)-x(t-t_{2})
is different enough from the direction of
c_{1}-c_{2}
, in the sense that if:

\displaystyle 1-\Big|\frac{v^{\prime\prime}}{|v^{\prime\prime}|}\cdot\frac{c_{2}-c_{1}}{|c_{2}-c_{1}|}\Big|\leq\varepsilon^{\delta_{3}}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{and}\hskip 14.22636pt8\varepsilon^{1-\delta_{2}}\leq\varepsilon^{\delta_{3}},

(4.59)

then no recollision can take place. As a consequence, choosing:

\displaystyle\delta_{3}<1-\delta_{2}

(4.60)

so that
8\varepsilon^{1-\delta_{2}}\leq\varepsilon^{\delta_{3}}
for any
\varepsilon
small enough (that is, smaller than a certain
\varepsilon_{2}=\varepsilon_{2}(\delta_{2},\delta_{3})
that depends only on
\delta_{2}
and
\delta_{3}
), we see that if
(t_{2},\omega_{2})\in\big(\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{2}}^{1}\big)^{c}\cap\big(\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{2}}^{4}\big)^{c}
, then no recollision can take place on the time interval
[t-t_{2},t-t_{3}]
.

We can therefore proceed to the change of variables, and then introduce the same pathological sets
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{3}}^{i}
for the parameters
(t_{3},\omega_{3})
,
1\leq i\leq 5
, as introduced for the second obstacle. Proceeding recursively, we can decompose after
k
steps the term
\varphi_{1}(k)
as follows:

\displaystyle\varphi_{\varepsilon}^{(2)}(k)=\varphi_{\varepsilon,k}^{(2)}(k)+\sum_{j=1}^{k}\varphi_{\varepsilon,c_{j}}^{(2),\text{patho.}}(k),

(4.61)

with:

\displaystyle\varphi_{\varepsilon,k}^{(2)}(k)

\displaystyle=\mu_{\varepsilon}^{k}\int_{t_{1}=0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\omega_{1}}}\mathds{1}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{1}}^{c}}\int_{t_{2}=0}^{t_{1}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\omega_{2}}}\mathds{1}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{2}}^{c}}\dots\int_{t_{k}=0}^{t_{k-1}}\int_{\mathbb{S}_{\omega_{k}}^{d-1}}\mathds{1}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{k}}^{c}}e^{-\mu_{\varepsilon}|\overline{B(x,\varepsilon)}\,\cup\,\mathcal{T}^{t}(c^{k})|}\prod_{l=1}^{k}|v^{(l-1)}\cdot\omega_{l}|

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\times\varphi\big(x+\sum_{m=1}^{k}\big(t_{m-1}-t_{m}\big)v^{(m-1)}+t_{k}v^{(k)},v^{(k)}\big)\varepsilon^{k(d-1)}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{k}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{k}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{1},

(4.62)

\displaystyle\varphi_{\varepsilon,c_{j}}^{(2),\text{patho.}}(k)

\displaystyle=\mu_{\varepsilon}^{k}\int_{t_{1}=0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\omega_{1}}}\mathds{1}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{1}}^{c}}\dots\int_{t_{j-1}=0}^{t_{j-2}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\omega_{j-1}}}\mathds{1}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{j-1}}^{c}}\int_{t_{j}=0}^{t_{j-1}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\omega_{j}}}\mathds{1}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{j}}}\int_{c_{j+1}}\dots\int_{c_{k}}e^{-\mu_{\varepsilon}|\overline{B(x,\varepsilon)}\,\cup\,\mathcal{T}^{t}(c^{k})|}

\displaystyle\hskip 34.1433pt\times\varphi\big(T_{c,\varepsilon}^{t}(x,v)\big)\mathds{1}_{X_{0}}(c^{k})\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{P}_{\text{patho.}}^{c}}(c^{k})\mathds{1}_{\{c_{i}\in B_{\varepsilon}\forall\,j+1\leq i\leq k\}}\mathds{1}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{the obstacles}\\
c_{l},\,j+1\leq l\leq k,\\
\text{are internal}\end{subarray}}(c^{k})\mathds{1}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{the $m$-th collided}\\
\text{obstacle is }c_{m}\\
\forall\,j+1\leq m\leq k\end{subarray}}(c^{k})

\displaystyle\hskip 128.0374pt\times\prod_{l=1}^{j}|v^{(l-1)}\cdot\omega_{l}|\varepsilon^{j(d-1)}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}c_{k}\dots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}c_{j+1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{j}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{j}\dots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{1}

(4.63)

and finally

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{j}}=\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{j}}^{1}\cup\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{j}}^{2}\cup\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{j}}^{3}\cup\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{j}}^{4}\cup\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{j}}^{5},\hskip 14.22636pt\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{j}}^{c}=\mathcal{A}_{c_{j}}\backslash\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{j}},

(4.64)

with

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{j}}^{1}=\Big\{(t_{j},\omega_{j})\in\mathcal{A}_{c_{j}}\ /\ t_{j-1}-t_{j}<\max\Big(\frac{\varepsilon^{\delta_{2}}}{r^{j-1}|v|},\varepsilon\Big)\Big\},

(4.65)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{j}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\Big\{(t_{j},\omega_{j})\in\mathcal{A}_{c_{j}}\ /\ x+\sum_{m=1}^{j}\big(t_{m-1}-t_{m}\big)v^{(m-1)}-\varepsilon\omega_{j}\in\overline{B(x,\varepsilon)}\Big\},

(4.66)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{j}}^{3}

\displaystyle=\Big\{(t_{j},\omega_{j})\in\mathcal{A}_{c_{j}}\ /\

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912ptx+\sum_{m=1}^{j}\big(t_{m-1}-t_{m}\big)v^{(m-1)}-\varepsilon\omega_{j}\in[x(t-t_{n-1}),x(t-t_{n})]+\overline{B(0,\varepsilon)}\hskip 5.69054pt\text{for some }n<j\Big\},

(4.67)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{j}}^{4}=\Big\{(t_{j},\omega_{j})\in\mathcal{A}_{c_{j}}\ /\ 1-\Big|\frac{v^{(j)}}{|v^{(j)}|}\cdot\frac{c_{j}-c_{n}}{|c_{j}-c_{n}|}\Big|\leq\varepsilon^{\delta_{3}}\hskip 5.69054pt\text{for some }n<j\Big\},

(4.68)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{j}}^{5}=\Big\{(t_{j},\omega_{j})\in\mathcal{A}_{c_{j}}\ /\ 1-\Big|\frac{v^{(j)}}{|v^{(j)}|}\cdot\frac{v^{(n)}}{|v^{(n)}|}\Big|\leq\varepsilon^{\delta_{1}}\hskip 5.69054pt\text{for some }n<j\Big\}.

(4.69)

Estimating the remainder terms.

We turn now to the estimates of the pathological terms
\varphi_{\varepsilon,c_{j}}^{(2),\text{patho.}}(k)
. To do so, we will decompose:

\displaystyle\varphi_{\varepsilon,c_{j}}^{(2),\text{patho.}}(k)=\sum_{l=1}^{5}\varphi_{\varepsilon,c_{j},l}^{(2),\text{patho.}}(k),

(4.70)

where
\varphi_{\varepsilon,c_{j},l}^{(2),\text{patho.}}(k)
is defined by replacing the pathological set
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{j}}
in the expression of
\varphi_{\varepsilon,c_{j}}^{(2),\text{patho.}}(k)
by the subset
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{j}}^{l}
, defined in (
4.65
)-(
4.69
), that is:

\displaystyle\varphi_{\varepsilon,c_{j},l}^{(2),\text{patho.}}(k)

\displaystyle=\mu_{\varepsilon}^{k}\int_{t_{1}=0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\omega_{1}}}\mathds{1}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{1}}^{c}}\dots\int_{t_{j-1}=0}^{t_{j-2}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\omega_{j-1}}}\mathds{1}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{j-1}}^{c}}\int_{t_{j}=0}^{t_{j-1}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\omega_{j}}}\mathds{1}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{j}}}^{l}\int_{c_{j+1}}\hskip-5.69054pt\dots\int_{c_{k}}e^{-\mu_{\varepsilon}|\overline{B(x,\varepsilon)}\,\cup\,\mathcal{T}^{t}(c^{k})|}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\times\varphi\big(T_{c,\varepsilon}^{t}(x,v)\big)\mathds{1}_{X_{0}}(c^{k})\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{P}_{\text{patho.}}^{c}}(c^{k})\mathds{1}_{\{c_{i}\in B_{\varepsilon}\forall\,j+1\leq i\leq k\}}\mathds{1}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{the obstacles}\\
c_{l},\,j+1\leq l\leq k,\\
\text{are internal}\end{subarray}}(c^{k})\mathds{1}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{the $m$-th collided}\\
\text{obstacle is }c_{m}\\
\forall\,j+1\leq m\leq k\end{subarray}}(c^{k})

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt\times\prod_{l=1}^{j}|v^{(l-1)}\cdot\omega_{l}|\varepsilon^{j(d-1)}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}c_{k}\dots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}c_{j+1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{j}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{j}\dots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{1}

(4.71)

for any
1\leq l\leq 5
. We will estimate the terms
\varphi_{\varepsilon,c_{j},l}^{(2),\text{patho.}}(k)
separately. As for
\varphi_{\varepsilon,c_{j},1}^{(2),\text{patho.}}(k)
(that is, for
l=1
), we will use the following inequality on the subset
S_{j,[a,b]}
of the
k
-simplex, with
j\leq k
and
0\leq a<b\leq t_{j-1}
, defined as:

\displaystyle S_{j,[a,b]}=\int_{0}^{t}\dots\int_{t_{j-1}=0}^{t_{j-2}}\int_{t_{j}\in[a,b]}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{j}\dots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{1}.

(4.72)

We have:

\displaystyle S_{j,[a,b]}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\dots\int_{t_{j-1}=0}^{t_{j-2}}\big[b-a\big]\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{j-1}\dots t_{1}=\frac{t^{j-1}}{(j-1)!}\big[b-a\big].

(4.73)

In addition, by definition for
j+1\leq l\leq k
the obstacle
c_{l}
belongs to the dynamical tube obtained for
t\in[t-t_{l-1},t-t_{l}]
. By construction the configuration of scatterers
c^{k}
is chosen such that it belongs to
\mathcal{P}_{\text{patho.}}^{c}
, and the obstacles are ordered. In particular, the hard sphere flow
T_{c,\varepsilon}^{s}
is well-defined for all time
s\in[0,t]
, and the trajectory presents only a finite number of collisions with the scatterers. So, there exists a sequence of times
0<t_{k}<\dots<t_{j+1}<t_{j}
such that the time
t-t_{l}
corresponds to the first time of collision between the tagged particle and the obstacle
c_{l}
.

Besides, assuming that the position of all the obstacles are known, except the last obstacle
c_{k}
, we deduce that
c_{k}
belongs to a portion of the dynamical tube of length equal to
t_{k-1}|v^{(k-1)}|
(where
t-t_{k-1}
is the time of the first collision with the penultimate obstacle
c_{k-1}
), corresponding to the portion of the dynamical tube obtained in the case when only the first
k-1
obstacles exist and are internal, for the time interval
[t-t_{k-1},t]
. On this time interval, only a finite number of collisions take place. As a consequence, by recursion, and relying on Lemma
2
, we can estimate the measure of the following portion of the dynamical tube
\mathcal{T}^{t}(c^{k})
as:

\displaystyle\Big|\big\{

\displaystyle y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\ /\ \exists\,s\in[t-t_{k-1},t],z\in\partial B(0,\varepsilon)\ \text{such that}\ y=\big(T_{c,\varepsilon}^{s}(x,v)\big)_{x}+z\big\}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\Big|\big[x(t-t_{k-1}),x(t-t_{k-1})+t_{k-1}|v^{(k-1)}|\big]+\overline{B(0,\varepsilon)}\Big|,

(4.74)

with

\displaystyle x(t-t_{k-1})=\big(T_{c,\varepsilon}^{t-t_{k-1}}(x,v)\big)_{x}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{and}\hskip 14.22636ptv^{(k-1)}=\big(T_{c,\varepsilon}^{t-t_{k-1}}(x,v)\big)_{v},

(4.75)

where the subset of which the measure is taken as an upper bound corresponds to the rectified trajectory
T_{c,\varepsilon}^{s}(x,v)
plus the closed ball
\overline{B(0,\varepsilon)}
, in the case when there is no collision on the time interval
]t-t_{l-1},t-t_{l}[
. Since in addition the last obstacle
c_{k}
cannot belong to the ball

\displaystyle\overline{B\big(\big(T_{c,\varepsilon}^{t-t_{k-1}}(x,v)\big)_{x},\varepsilon\big)},

(4.76)

we deduce that
c_{k}
belongs to the following subset
C_{k}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
:

\displaystyle C_{k}=\Big\{

\displaystyle y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\ /\ \exists\,s\in[t-t_{l-1},t-t_{l}],z\in\partial B(0,\varepsilon)\ \text{such that}\ y=\big(T_{c,\varepsilon}^{s}(x,v)\big)_{x}+z\Big\}\,\backslash\,\overline{B\big(\big(T_{c,\varepsilon}^{t-t_{k-1}}(x,v)\big)_{x},\varepsilon\big)}

(4.77)

and the volume of
C_{k}
can be estimated as follows:

\displaystyle|C_{k}|\leq C(d-1)\varepsilon^{d-1}t_{k-1}|v^{(k-1)}|,

(4.78)

where
C(d-1)
is the constant that appears in the formula of the volume of the
(d-1)
-dimensional ball:
|B_{\mathbb{R}^{d-1}}(0,\varepsilon)|=C(d-1)\varepsilon^{d-1}
.
In the end, we can estimate the following integral as:

\displaystyle\int_{c_{j+1}}\dots\int_{c_{k}}\mathds{1}_{X_{0}}(c^{k})\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{P}_{\text{patho.}}^{c}}(c^{k})\mathds{1}_{\{c_{i}\in B_{\varepsilon}\forall\,j+1\leq i\leq k\}}\mathds{1}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{the obstacles}\\
c_{l},\,j+1\leq l\leq k,\\
\text{are internal}\end{subarray}}(c^{k})\mathds{1}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{the $m$-th collided}\\
\text{obstacle is }c_{m}\\
\forall\,j+1\leq m\leq k\end{subarray}}(c^{k})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}c_{k}\dots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}c_{j+1}

\displaystyle\hskip 11.38109pt\leq\int_{c_{j+1}}\dots\int_{c_{k-1}}\mathds{1}_{X_{0}}(c^{k-1})\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{P}_{\text{patho.}}^{c}}(c^{k-1})\mathds{1}_{\{c_{i}\in B_{\varepsilon}\forall\,j+1\leq i\leq k-1\}}\mathds{1}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{the obstacles}\\
c_{l},\,j+1\leq l\leq k-1,\\
\text{are internal}\end{subarray}}(c^{k-1})\mathds{1}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{the $m$-th collided}\\
\text{obstacle is }c_{m}\\
\forall\,j+1\leq m\leq k-1\end{subarray}}(c^{k-1})
