\displaystyle\hskip 213.39566pt\times C(d-1)\varepsilon^{d-1}t_{k-1}(c^{k-1})|v^{(k-1)}|\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}c_{k-1}\dots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}c_{j+1},

(4.79)

relying on the result of Lemma
2
. Then, for any integer
n
, we decompose the integral on
c_{k-1}
as:

\displaystyle\int_{c_{k-1}}t_{k-1}(c^{k-1})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}c_{k-1}=\int_{c_{k-1}}\sum_{l=1}^{n}\mathds{1}_{t_{k-1}\in[\frac{(l-1)}{n}t_{k-2},\frac{l}{n}t_{k-2}]}t_{k-1}(c^{k-1})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}c_{k-1},

(4.80)

which provides the upper bound:

\displaystyle\int_{c_{k-1}}t_{k-1}(c^{k-1})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}c_{k-1}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{l=1}^{n}\frac{l}{n}t_{k-2}(c^{k-2})C(d-1)\varepsilon^{d-1}\frac{t_{k-2}(c^{k-2})}{n}|v^{(k-2)}|

\displaystyle\leq C(d-1)\varepsilon^{d-1}|v|t_{k-2}^{2}(c^{k-2})\frac{n(n+1)}{2n^{2}}\cdotp

(4.81)

In the limit
n\rightarrow+\infty
, we find in particular:

\displaystyle\int_{c_{k-1}}t_{k-1}(c^{k-1})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}c_{k-1}\leq C(d-1)\varepsilon^{d-1}|v|\frac{t_{k-2}^{2}(c^{k-2})}{2}\cdotp

(4.82)

For the next step, in the general case, the same decomposition of the integral provides a series of the form:

\displaystyle\frac{t_{k-m}^{m}}{n^{m}}\sum_{l=1}^{n}l^{m-1}=t_{k-m}^{m}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{l=1}^{n}\Big(\frac{l}{n}\Big)^{m-1},

(4.83)

which is a Riemann sum, and converges as
n
goes to infinity towards
\frac{t_{k-m}^{m}}{m}
. As a consequence, we find for the pathological term
\varphi_{\varepsilon,c_{j},l}^{(1),\text{patho.}}(k)
obtained in the case
l=1
:

\displaystyle\big|\varphi_{\varepsilon,c_{j},1}^{(2),\text{patho.}}(k)\big|

\displaystyle\leq\mu_{\varepsilon}^{k}{\left|\kern-1.29167pt\left|\varphi\right|\kern-1.29167pt\right|}_{\infty}\int_{t_{1}=0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\omega_{1}}}\dots\int_{t_{j-1}=0}^{t_{j-2}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\omega_{j-1}}}\int_{t_{j}=0}^{t_{j-1}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\omega_{j}}}\mathds{1}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{j}}^{1}}\int_{c_{j+1}}\dots\int_{c_{k}}e^{-\mu_{\varepsilon}|\overline{B(x,\varepsilon)}\,\cup\,\mathcal{T}^{t}(c^{k})|}

\displaystyle\hskip 71.13188pt\times\mathds{1}_{X_{0}}(c^{k})\mathds{1}_{\mathcal{P}_{\text{patho.}}^{c}}(c^{k})\mathds{1}_{\{c_{i}\in B_{\varepsilon}\forall\,j+1\leq i\leq k\}}\mathds{1}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{the obstacles}\\
c_{l},\,j+1\leq l\leq k,\\
\text{are internal}\end{subarray}}(c^{k})\mathds{1}_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{the $m$-th collided}\\
\text{obstacle is }c_{m}\\
\forall\,j+1\leq m\leq k\end{subarray}}(c^{k})

\displaystyle\hskip 99.58464pt\times\prod_{l=1}^{j}|v^{(l-1)}\cdot\omega_{l}|\varepsilon^{j(d-1)}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}c_{k}\dots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}c_{j+1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{j}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{j}\dots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\mu_{\varepsilon}^{k}{\left|\kern-1.29167pt\left|\varphi\right|\kern-1.29167pt\right|}_{\infty}|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|^{j}|v|^{j}\varepsilon^{j(d-1)}\frac{\big[C(d-1)\varepsilon^{d-1}t|v|\big]^{k-j}}{(k-j)!}\frac{t^{j-1}}{(j-1)!}\Big(\frac{\varepsilon^{\delta_{2}}}{r^{j-1}|v|}+\varepsilon\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\big(|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|t|v|r^{-1}\big)^{j-1}}{(j-1)!}\frac{\big[C(d-1)t|v|\big]^{k-j}}{(k-j)!}|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|{\left|\kern-1.29167pt\left|\varphi\right|\kern-1.29167pt\right|}_{\infty}\big(\varepsilon^{\delta_{2}}+|v|\varepsilon\big),

(4.84)

using in particular that in the Boltzmann-Grad limit we have
\mu_{\varepsilon}^{k}\varepsilon^{j(d-1)}\varepsilon^{(k-j)(d-1)}=1
.

In the case of
\varphi_{\varepsilon,c_{j},2}^{(2),\text{patho.}}(k)
(
l=2
) we use a similar argument. For
\omega_{j}
fixed, the set of
t_{j}
such that
(t_{j},\omega_{j})\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{j}}^{2}
is contained in a certain interval , with
I_{\text{path.}}
that depends on the
x
,
t_{m}
and
v^{(m-1)}
for
1\leq m\leq j-1
, and such that:

\displaystyle\big|I_{\text{path.}}\big|\leq\frac{2}{|v^{(j)}|}\varepsilon\leq\frac{2}{r^{j}|v|}\varepsilon.

(4.85)

We find therefore:

\displaystyle\big|\varphi_{\varepsilon,c_{j},2}^{(2),\text{patho.}}(k)\big|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\big(|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|t|v|r^{-1}\big)^{j-1}}{(j-1)!}\frac{\big[C(d-1)t|v|\big]^{k-j}}{(k-j)!}\frac{|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|{\left|\kern-1.29167pt\left|\varphi_{0}\right|\kern-1.29167pt\right|}_{\infty}}{r}\varepsilon.

(4.86)

As for
\varphi_{\varepsilon,c_{j},3}^{(2),\text{patho.}}(k)
(
l=3
), we use the fact that
c^{k}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{j-1}}^{c}
, so that in particular
c^{k}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{j-1}}^{5}
, and so the direction of
v^{(j-1)}
is far enough from the direction of
v^{(n)}
, that is, the direction of
x(t-t_{n-1})-x(t-t_{n})
, for any
n<j-1
. Therefore, for any index
n<j-1
, only a small subset of time parameters
t_{j}
are such that
T_{c,\varepsilon}^{s}(x,v)
belongs to the cylinder
[x(t-t_{n-1}),x(t-t_{n})]+\overline{B(0,\varepsilon)}
. More precisely, for
\theta
the angle between
v^{(j-1)}
and
v^{(n)}
, we have that
|\cos\theta|
is bounded from above by
1-\varepsilon^{\delta_{1}}
, so that the maximal length of a trajectory contained in the cylinder is bounded from above by:

\displaystyle 2\frac{\varepsilon}{|v^{(j-1)}||\sin\theta|}

\displaystyle\leq 2\frac{\varepsilon}{r^{j-1}|v|\sqrt{1-\big(1-\varepsilon^{\delta_{1}}\big)^{2}}}\leq 2\frac{\varepsilon}{r^{j-1}|v|\sqrt{\varepsilon^{\delta_{1}}}}\leq\frac{2}{r^{j-1}|v|}\varepsilon^{1-\delta_{1}/2},

(4.87)

for any
\varepsilon
smaller than a certain
\varepsilon_{3}=\varepsilon_{3}(\delta_{1})
that depends only on the positive constant
\delta_{1}
. We find then:

\displaystyle\big|\varphi_{\varepsilon,c_{j},3}^{(2),\text{patho.}}(k)\big|

\displaystyle\leq\mu_{\varepsilon}^{k}{\left|\kern-1.29167pt\left|\varphi\right|\kern-1.29167pt\right|}_{\infty}|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|^{j}|v|^{j}\varepsilon^{j(d-1)}\frac{\big[C(d-1)\varepsilon^{d-1}t|v|\big]^{k-j}}{(k-j)!}\frac{t^{j-1}}{(j-1)!}\frac{2(j-2)\varepsilon^{1-\delta_{1}/2}}{r^{j-1}|v|}

\displaystyle\leq 2\frac{\big(|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|t|v|r^{-1}\big)^{j-2}}{(j-2)!}\frac{\big[C(d-1)t|v|\big]^{k-j}}{(k-j)!}t|v|r^{-1}|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|^{2}{\left|\kern-1.29167pt\left|\varphi\right|\kern-1.29167pt\right|}_{\infty}\varepsilon^{1-\delta_{1}/2}.

(4.88)

Finally, we will rely on Lemma
1
to estimate the size of the pathological terms
\varphi_{\varepsilon,c_{j},4}^{(2),\text{patho.}}(k)
and
\varphi_{\varepsilon,c_{j},5}^{(2),\text{patho.}}(k)
. Each of the sets
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{j}}^{4}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{j}}^{5}
corresponds to a set of
j-1
pathological directions, that are obtained by taking the angular parameter
\omega_{j}
in
j-1
pathological subsets, of respective measure estimated by
C(d,r)\varepsilon^{\delta_{3}/2}
and
C(d,r)\varepsilon^{\delta_{1}/2}
. In the end, we find in the case
l=4
:

\displaystyle\big|\varphi_{\varepsilon,c_{j},4}^{(2),\text{patho.}}(k)\big|

\displaystyle\leq\mu_{\varepsilon}^{k}{\left|\kern-1.29167pt\left|\varphi\right|\kern-1.29167pt\right|}_{\infty}|v|^{j}\varepsilon^{j(d-1)}|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|^{j-1}\frac{\big[C(d-1)\varepsilon^{d-1}t|v|\big]^{k-j}}{(k-j)!}\frac{t^{j}}{j!}(j-1)C(d,r)\varepsilon^{\delta_{3}/2}

\displaystyle\leq C(d,r)\frac{\big(|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|t|v|\big)^{j-1}}{(j-1)!}\frac{\big[C(d-1)t|v|\big]^{k-j}}{(k-j)!}t|v|{\left|\kern-1.29167pt\left|\varphi\right|\kern-1.29167pt\right|}_{\infty}\varepsilon^{\delta_{3}/2},

(4.89)

and similarly, for
\varphi_{\varepsilon,c_{j},5}^{(2),\text{patho.}}(k)
(
l=5
):

\displaystyle\big|\varphi_{\varepsilon,c_{j},5}^{(2),\text{patho.}}(k)\big|

\displaystyle\leq C(d,r)\frac{\big(|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|t|v|\big)^{j-1}}{(j-1)!}\frac{\big[C(d-1)t|v|\big]^{k-j}}{(k-j)!}t|v|{\left|\kern-1.29167pt\left|\varphi\right|\kern-1.29167pt\right|}_{\infty}\varepsilon^{\delta_{1}/2}.

(4.90)

Gathering the estimates, we denote by
R_{2}
the collection of the remainder terms:

\displaystyle R_{2}=\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}\sum_{j=1}^{k}\sum_{l=1}^{5}\varphi_{\varepsilon,c_{j},l}^{(2),\text{patho.}}(k),

(4.91)

so that:

\displaystyle\varphi_{\varepsilon}^{(1)}=\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}\varphi_{\varepsilon,k}^{(2)}(k)+\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}\sum_{j=1}^{k}\sum_{l=1}^{5}\varphi_{\varepsilon,c_{j},l}^{(2),\text{patho.}}(k)=\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}\varphi_{\varepsilon,k}^{(2)}(k)+R_{2}.

(4.92)

Denoting by:

\displaystyle C_{1}=|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|t|v|r^{-1}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{and}\hskip 14.22636ptC_{2}=C(d-1)t|v|

(4.93)

and relying on (
4.3
)-(
4.90
), we find:

\displaystyle\big|R_{2}\big|\leq{\left|\kern-1.29167pt\left|\varphi\right|\kern-1.29167pt\right|}_{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}\sum_{j=1}^{k}\frac{C_{1}^{j-1}}{(j-1)!}\frac{C_{2}^{k-j}}{(k-j)!}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\times\Big[|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|\big(\varepsilon^{\delta_{2}}+|v|\varepsilon\big)+\frac{|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|}{r}\varepsilon+2t|v|\frac{|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|^{2}}{r}\varepsilon^{1-\delta_{1}/2}+C(d,r)t|v|\varepsilon^{\delta_{3}/2}+C(d,r)t|v|\varepsilon^{\delta_{1}/2}\Big].

(4.94)

Inverting the sums:

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}\sum_{j=1}^{k}\frac{C_{1}^{j-1}}{(j-1)!}\frac{C_{2}^{k-j}}{(k-j)!}=\sum_{j=1}^{+\infty}\sum_{k=j}^{+\infty}\frac{C_{1}^{j-1}}{(j-1)!}\frac{C_{2}^{k-j}}{(k-j)!}=e^{C_{1}+C_{2}}.

(4.95)

In the end, choosing:

\displaystyle\delta_{1}=\frac{1}{2},\hskip 14.22636pt\delta_{2}=\frac{1}{4}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{and}\hskip 14.22636pt\delta_{3}=\frac{1}{2},

(4.96)

we have obtained that there exist two positive constants
\widetilde{C}_{1}=\widetilde{C}_{1}(d,r)
and
\widetilde{C}_{2}=\widetilde{C}_{1}=2(d,r)
that depend only on the dimension
d
and the restitution coefficient
r
such that:

\displaystyle\big|R_{2}\big|\leq{\left|\kern-1.29167pt\left|\varphi\right|\kern-1.29167pt\right|}_{\infty}\widetilde{C}_{1}\big(1+\max(1,t)|v|\big)e^{\widetilde{C}_{2}t|v|}\varepsilon^{1/4}.

(4.97)

The proof of Proposition
6
is complete.
∎

Proposition
6
completed the important step which consists in comparing
\varphi_{\varepsilon}
with the solution
\widetilde{\varphi}
of the adjoint equation (
2.31
). To do so, we established that for most of the configurations
c
of the scatterers, the flow
T_{c,\varepsilon}^{t}(x,v)
of the tagged particle corresponds to
\big(x+\sum_{m=1}^{k}\big(t_{m-1}-t_{m}\big)v^{(m-1)}+t_{k}v^{(k)},v^{(k)}\big)
, the position and velocity at which the intial datum
\varphi_{0}
is evaluated in the series representation (
1
) of the solution
\widetilde{\varphi}
.

Proposition 7

(Final comparison between
\varphi_{\varepsilon}
and
\varphi
)
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
. Let
c
be a
d
-dimensional Poisson process of intensity
\mu_{\varepsilon}>0
such that the Boltzmann-Grad scaling (
2.16
) holds. Then, there exists a universal constant
\varepsilon_{0}>0
and two positive constants
\widetilde{C}_{3}=\widetilde{C}_{3}(d,r)
and
\widetilde{C}_{4}=\widetilde{C}_{4}(d,r)
that depend only on the dimension
d
and the restitution coefficient
r
such that, for any
0<\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}
, and any function
\varphi\in\mathcal{C}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
, the quantity
\varphi_{\varepsilon}
, defined as (
4.5
), and the series
\psi
defined in (
1
) with initial datum
\varphi
satisfy:

\displaystyle\varphi_{\varepsilon}-\psi=R_{1}+R_{2}+R_{3},

(4.98)

where
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
satisfy respectively (
4.8
) and (
4.30
), and
R_{3}
is such that:

\displaystyle\big|R_{3}\big|\leq{\left|\kern-1.29167pt\left|\varphi\right|\kern-1.29167pt\right|}_{\infty}\widetilde{C}_{3}\max\big(1,t|v|^{2},t^{2}|v|^{2}\big)e^{\widetilde{C}_{4}t|v|}\varepsilon^{1/4}.

(4.99)

Proof.

We observe that the series representation (
1
) of
\widetilde{\varphi}
is well defined assuming only that
\varphi
is continuous and vanishing at infinity. To compare
\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}\varphi_{\varepsilon,k}^{(2)}(k)
with the series representation, we proceed in two steps: firstly, we determine lower and upper bounds for the measure of the dynamical tube, and secondly, we will estimate the error term coming from the truncations
\mathds{1}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{j}}}
in the domain of integration.

As first step, we consider the collisionless tube, whose measure is given by:

\displaystyle\mu_{\varepsilon}\big|[x,x+tv]+\overline{B(0,\varepsilon)}\big|=\mu_{\varepsilon}\big[|\overline{B(0,\varepsilon)}|+C(d-1)\varepsilon^{d-1}t|v|\big],

(4.100)

so that

\displaystyle\Big|\mu_{\varepsilon}\big|[x,x+tv]+\overline{B(0,\varepsilon)}\big|-C(d-1)t|v|\Big|\leq\mu_{\varepsilon}|\overline{B(0,\varepsilon)}|=C(d)\varepsilon.

(4.101)

Besides, considering the dynamical tube
\mathcal{T}^{t}(c^{k})
(that is, in the case when collisions can occur) and applying Lemma
2
, we find:

\displaystyle\mu_{\varepsilon}\big|\mathcal{T}^{t}(c^{k})\big|

\displaystyle\leq\mu_{\varepsilon}|\overline{B(0,\varepsilon)}|+\mu_{\varepsilon}C(d-1)\varepsilon^{d-1}\Big[\sum_{m=1}^{k}(t_{m-1}-t_{m})|v^{(m-1)}|+t_{k}|v^{(k)}|\Big]

\displaystyle\leq C(d)\varepsilon+C(d-1)\Big[\sum_{m=1}^{k}(t_{m-1}-t_{m})|v^{(m-1)}|+t_{k}|v^{(k)}|\Big].

(4.102)

To obtain a lower bound on the previous quantity, we have
t_{m}-t_{m+1}\geq\varepsilon
for any
1\leq m\leq k-1
thanks to the cut-off
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{j}}^{1}
. Therefore:

\displaystyle\mu_{\varepsilon}\big|\mathcal{T}^{t}(c^{k})\big|

\displaystyle\geq\mu_{\varepsilon}C(d-1)\varepsilon^{d-1}\Big[(t-t_{1})|v|+(t_{1}-t_{2}-2\varepsilon)|v^{\prime}|+\dots+(t_{k-1}-t_{k}-2\varepsilon)|v^{(k-1)}|+t_{k}|v^{(k)}|\Big]

(4.103)

which implies:

\displaystyle\Big|\big(\mu_{\varepsilon}\big|\overline{B(x,\varepsilon)}\cup\mathcal{T}^{t}(c^{k})\big|\big)-\big(\sum_{j=1}^{k}C_{d}(t_{j-1}-t_{j})|v^{(j-1)}|+C_{d}t_{k}|v^{(k)}|\big)\Big|\leq\max\big(2C(d)\varepsilon,2C(d-1)k|v|\varepsilon\big),

(4.104)

observing that the constants
C_{d}
and
C(d-1)
match. As a consequence, comparing
\varphi_{\varepsilon,k}^{(2)}(k)
with
\varphi_{\varepsilon,k}^{(3)}(k)
defined as (the exponential term below the integrals is replaced by the corresponding term of
\psi
):

\displaystyle\varphi_{\varepsilon,k}^{(3)}(k)

\displaystyle=\int_{t_{1}=0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\omega_{1}}}\mathds{1}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{1}}^{c}}\int_{t_{2}=0}^{t_{1}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\omega_{2}}}\mathds{1}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{2}}^{c}}\dots\int_{t_{k}=0}^{t_{k-1}}\int_{\mathbb{S}_{\omega_{k}}^{d-1}}\mathds{1}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{k}}^{c}}e^{-\sum_{j=1}^{k}C_{d}(t_{j-1}-t_{j})|v^{(j-1)}|-C_{d}t_{k}|v^{(k)}|}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\times\prod_{l=1}^{k}|v^{(l-1)}\cdot\omega_{l}|\varphi\big(x+\sum_{m=1}^{k}\big(t_{m-1}-t_{m}\big)v^{(m-1)}+t_{k}v^{(k)},v^{(k)}\big)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{k}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{k}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{1}

(4.105)

we have:

\displaystyle\big|\varphi_{\varepsilon,k}^{(2)}(k)-\varphi_{\varepsilon,k}^{(3)}(k)\big|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{t_{1}=0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\omega_{1}}}\mathds{1}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{1}}^{c}}\int_{t_{2}=0}^{t_{1}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\omega_{2}}}\mathds{1}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{2}}^{c}}\dots\int_{t_{k}=0}^{t_{k-1}}\int_{\mathbb{S}_{\omega_{k}}^{d-1}}2\max\big[C(d),C(d-1)k|v|\big]\varepsilon

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\times\prod_{l=1}^{k}|v^{(l-1)}\cdot\omega_{l}|\varphi\big(x+\sum_{m=1}^{k}\big(t_{m-1}-t_{m}\big)v^{(m-1)}+t_{k}v^{(k)},v^{(k)}\big)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{k}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{k}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{1}

\displaystyle\leq 2\max\big[C(d),C(d-1)k|v|\big]\frac{|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|^{k}t^{k}|v|^{k}}{k!}{\left|\kern-1.29167pt\left|\varphi\right|\kern-1.29167pt\right|}_{\infty}\varepsilon,

(4.106)

so that

\displaystyle\Big|\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}\varphi_{\varepsilon,k}^{(2)}(k)-\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}\varphi_{\varepsilon,k}^{(3)}(k)\Big|\leq 2\max\Big[C(d)e^{|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|t|v|}{\left|\kern-1.29167pt\left|\varphi\right|\kern-1.29167pt\right|}_{\infty},C(d-1)|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|t|v|^{2}e^{|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|t|v|}\Big]\varepsilon.

(4.107)

As for the second step, we compare finally
\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}\varphi_{\varepsilon,k}^{(3)}(k)
with the series representation (
1
) of
\psi
. We have:

\displaystyle\big|\psi-\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}\varphi_{\varepsilon,k}^{(3)}(k)\big|

\displaystyle=\Big|\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}\sum_{j=1}^{k}\int_{t_{1}=0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\omega_{1}}}\mathds{1}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{1}}^{c}}\int_{t_{2}=0}^{t_{1}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\omega_{2}}}\mathds{1}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{2}}}\dots\int_{t_{j-1}=0}^{t_{j-2}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\omega_{j-1}}}\mathds{1}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{j-1}}^{c}}\int_{t_{j}=0}^{t_{j-1}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\omega_{j}}}\mathds{1}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}_{c_{j}}}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\times\int_{t_{j+1}=0}^{t_{j}}\int_{\mathbb{S}_{\omega_{j+1}}^{d-1}}\dots\int_{t_{k}=0}^{t_{k-1}}\int_{\mathbb{S}_{\omega_{k}}^{d-1}}e^{-\sum_{j=1}^{k}C_{d}(t_{j-1}-t_{j})|v^{(j-1)}|-C_{d}t_{k}|v^{(k)}|}\prod_{l=1}^{k}|v^{(l-1)}\cdot\omega_{l}|

\displaystyle\hskip 71.13188pt\times\varphi\big(x+\sum_{m=1}^{k}\big(t_{m-1}-t_{m}\big)v^{(m-1)}+t_{k}v^{(k)},v^{(k)}\big)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{k}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{k}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{1}\Big|

so that

\displaystyle\big|\psi-\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}\varphi_{\varepsilon,k}^{(3)}(k)\big|

\displaystyle\leq{\left|\kern-1.29167pt\left|\varphi\right|\kern-1.29167pt\right|}_{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}\sum_{j=1}^{k}|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|^{k-1}\frac{t^{j-1}}{(j-1)!}\frac{t^{k-j}}{(k-j)!}|v|^{k}\Bigg[\frac{\varepsilon^{\delta_{2}}}{r|v|}|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|+\frac{2\varepsilon}{r^{j}|v|}|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|

\displaystyle\hskip 49.79231pt+\frac{2(j-2)\varepsilon^{1-\delta_{1}/2}}{r^{j-1}|v|}|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|+t(j-1)C(d,r)\varepsilon^{\delta_{3}/2}+t(j-1)C(d,r)\varepsilon^{\delta_{1}/2}\Bigg].

(4.108)

Therefore, choosing as before
\delta_{1}=1/2
,
\delta_{2}=1/4
and
\delta_{3}=1/2
, there exists a constant
\widetilde{C}=\widetilde{C}(d,r)
that depends on the dimension
d
and the restitution coefficient
r
such that:

\displaystyle\big|\psi-\sum_{k=1}^{+\infty}\varphi_{\varepsilon,k}^{(3)}(k)\big|\leq\widetilde{C}\max\big(1,t^{2}|v|^{2}\big)e^{\big(1+\frac{1}{r}\big)|\mathbb{S}^{d-1}|t|v|}\varepsilon^{1/4}.

(4.109)

The proof of Proposition
7
is now complete.
∎

With the result of Proposition
7
, we are now in position to prove Theorem
1
.

Proof of Theorem
1
.

Let
f
be the unique weak solution to the inelastic linear Boltzmann equation (
1.6
) in the sense of Definition
5
, with initial datum
f_{0}
. We consider a function
\varphi
of
\mathcal{C}_{0}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and a positive real number
\varepsilon>0
. For any
t_{0}>0
fixed and for any
\varepsilon>0
small enough, we will prove that we have for any
t\in[0,t_{0}]
:

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}\varphi(x,v)f_{\varepsilon}(t,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v)-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}\varphi(x,v)f(t,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v)\Big|\leq\varepsilon.

(4.110)

To do so, we prove instead the weak
-*
convergence of
f_{\varepsilon}
towards the weak solution
g
introduced in Proposition
2
, with initial datum
f_{0}
. By definition, we have:

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}\varphi(x,v)f_{\varepsilon}(t,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}\varphi_{\varepsilon}(t,x,v)f_{0}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v)

(4.111)

where
\varphi_{\varepsilon}
is defined as in (
4.5
), and

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}\varphi(x,v)g(t,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}\psi(t,x,v)f_{0}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v),

(4.112)

where
\psi
is defined by the series (
1
), with initial datum
\varphi
. We consider now
\varepsilon>0
smaller than the universal constant
\varepsilon_{0}
given by Propositions
6
and
7
, so that gathering the results of Propositions
5
,
6
and
7
, we have:

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}\varphi(x,v)f_{\varepsilon}(t,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v)

\displaystyle-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}\varphi(x,v)f(t,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v)\Big|\leq\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}\big[R_{1}+R_{2}+R_{3}\big](t,x,v)f_{0}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v)\Big|,

(4.113)

where
R_{1}
,
R_{2}
and
R_{3}
satisfy respectively (
4.8
), (
4.30
) and (
4.99
).

Without loss of generality, we can assume that:

\displaystyle\max\Big[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}f_{0}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v),\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}e^{|v|^{p}}f_{0}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v)\Big]=1.

(4.114)

Since the intensity
\mu_{\varepsilon}
of the Poisson process satisfies the Boltzmann-Grad scaling (
2.16
), we have:

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}|R_{1}|f_{0}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v)\leq{\left|\kern-1.29167pt\left|\varphi\right|\kern-1.29167pt\right|}_{\infty}C(d)\varepsilon.

(4.115)

Since by (
4.30
) and (
4.99
) we have:

\displaystyle|R_{2}|\leq{\left|\kern-1.29167pt\left|\varphi\right|\kern-1.29167pt\right|}_{\infty}\widetilde{C}_{1}\big(1+\max(1,t)|v|\big)e^{\widetilde{C}_{2}t|v|}\varepsilon^{1/4}\hskip 5.69054pt\text{and}\hskip 5.69054pt|R_{3}|\leq{\left|\kern-1.29167pt\left|\varphi\right|\kern-1.29167pt\right|}_{\infty}\widetilde{C}_{3}\max\big(1,t|v|^{2},t^{2}|v|^{2}\big)e^{\widetilde{C}_{4}t|v|}\varepsilon^{1/4},

(4.116)

and since
t_{0}>0
is fixed, there exists a constant
M=M(d,r,p,t_{0},{\left|\kern-1.29167pt\left|\varphi\right|\kern-1.29167pt\right|}_{\infty})
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}t\in[0,t_{0}]\\
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\hskip-2.84526pt,\ v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\end{subarray}}\big[|R_{2}|+|R_{3}|\big]e^{-|v|^{p}}\leq M\varepsilon^{1/4}.

(4.117)

We find therefore:

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}R_{2}(t,x,v)f_{0}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v)\Big|\leq M\varepsilon^{1/4}\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}e^{|v|^{p}}f_{0}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v)\Big|\leq M\varepsilon^{1/4},

(4.118)

and similarly:

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}R_{3}(t,x,v)f_{0}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v)\Big|\leq M\varepsilon^{1/4}.

(4.119)

Gathering (
4.115
), (
4.118
) and (
4.119
), we obtain that there exists a constant
C_{\text{final}}=C_{\text{final}}(d,r,p,t_{0},{\left|\kern-1.29167pt\left|\varphi\right|\kern-1.29167pt\right|}_{\infty})
such that for any
\varepsilon
smaller than the universal constant
\min(\varepsilon_{0},1)>0
, we have:

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}}\hskip-2.84526pt\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}_{v}}\big[\varphi_{\varepsilon}(t,x,v)-\psi(t,x,v)\big]f_{0}(\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}v)\Big|\leq C_{\text{final}}\varepsilon^{1/4},

(4.120)

which concludes the proof of the weak
-*
convergence of
f_{\varepsilon}(t,\cdot,\cdot)
towards the measure
g
. Since
g
is a weak solution to (
1.6
) in the sense of Definition
5
according to Proposition
2
, and since such a weak solution is unique according to Proposition
3
, the proof of Theorem
1
is complete.
∎

5
Well-posedness of the dynamics of the particle system

In this section, we will address the question of the well-posedness of the dynamics of the particle system. More precisely, we will prove that the forward dynamics of a tagged particle colliding inelastically with inelastic, fixed scatterers distributed according to a Poisson process is globally well-posed, except for a set of distributions of scatterers realized with a zero probability.

This question is fundamental: there is no hope to establish a rigorous derivation of a kinetic equation from a particle system without proving before that the dynamics of the particle system is indeed well-posed. We also emphasize that, to the best of our knowledge, such a result has not been established in the case of inelastic scatterers.

It is usually argued that the elastic case can be addressed using the result of Burago-Ferleger-Kononenko
[
16
]
, which establishes that there exists a bound on the maximal number of collisions that a system of
N
elastic hard spheres can experience, globally in time. Remarkably, the result depends on the number of hard spheres
N
, but is uniform on the initial configurations of the particle system, and holds also when the hard spheres evolve in a domain with boundary, covering in particular the case of one single elastic hard sphere evolving among fixed scatterers.

In this section, we present a self-contained result, which applies of course to the inelastic case, but also to the elastic case
r=1
. It can also be easily extended to any type of tagged particle dynamics such that the particle evolves according to the free flow between two collisions with scatterers, and such that the norm of the velocity of the tagged particle does not increase at any collision. The proof relies on a direct adaptation of the original proof due to Alexander (
[
1
]
,
[
2
]
, revisited in
[
35
]
), concerning the global well-posedness of the system of
N
elastic hard spheres.

Remark 6
.

The proof of Alexander does not rule out the possibility that trajectories mght present infinitely many collisions. To this regard, the results of Burago-Ferleger-Kononenko
[
16
]
and Alexander
[
1
]
-
[
2
]
are complementary.

From Definition
1
, it is possible to identify the potential difficulties that may arise when defining the dynamics. The first one is the fact that is not clear how to define the particle dynamics when a collision involving the tagged particle and multiple scatterers occurs at a specific time. Additionally, it is also unclear whether the dynamics can be constructed globally over any arbitrary time interval
[0,t]
, as an infinite number of collisions, i.e. a collapse, may occur strictly before time
t
. We prove the following result.

Proposition 8

(Global well-posedness of the forward flow)
.

Let
r\in\ ]0,1[
,
\varepsilon>0
and
\mu>0
be three positive real numbers. Let
x,v\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
be two vectors. We consider a Poisson point process
C
of intensity
\mu
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Then, there exists a subset
\mathcal{P}_{\text{patho.}}
of scatterers, contained in:

\displaystyle X_{0}=\big\{c\in C\ /\ c\cap\overline{B(0,\varepsilon)}=\emptyset\big\}

(5.1)

such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mu}\big(\mathcal{P}_{\text{patho.}}\big)=0

(5.2)

and such that if
c\in\mathcal{P}_{\text{patho.}}^{c}
, then the forward inelastic hard sphere flow, introduced in Definition
1
, of the tagged particle among the distribution of scatterers
c
of radius
\varepsilon
is well-posed on
[0,t]
for any
t>0
. In other words, the mapping
s\in[0,t]\mapsto T^{s}_{c,\varepsilon}(x,v)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
introduced in
1
is globally well-defined for almost every distribution of scatterers
c
. In addition, the mapping
s\in[0,t]\mapsto T^{s}_{c,\varepsilon}(x,v)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is piecewise affine, right continuous and with a limit from the left at all point, and it satisfies (
2.5
)-(
2.6
).

Proof.

Fixing a small time interval
I
, the idea is to determine a set
\mathcal{P}
of distributions of scatterers for which the consecutive collisions of the tagged particles cannot take place in the same time interval
I
, and to prove that the probability of choosing a distribution in the complement of the set
\mathcal{P}
is small. As a consequence, the dynamics of a tagged particle evolving among scatterers distributed according to an element of
\mathcal{P}
is well-posed on such a time interval
I
. Finally, decomposing any arbitrary time interval
[0,t]
as
[0,t]=\bigcup_{k}I_{k}
, the objective is to show that
\sum_{k}\mathcal{P}_{k}^{c}=0
, where
\mathcal{P}_{k}^{c}
is the probability to choose a distribution of scatterers for which the dynamics is not well-defined on
I_{k}
.

Without loss of generality, we assume that the initial velocity
v
of the tagged particle is such that
|v|=1
, and that the initial position of the tagged particle is
0
. Let
t>0
be a positive real number. Let
\delta>0
. We introduce the following sets:

\displaystyle X_{0}=\Big\{c\ /\ c\cap\overline{B(0,\varepsilon)}=\emptyset\Big\},

(5.3)

\displaystyle X_{1}=X_{1}(t)=\Big\{c\ /\ c\cap\left([0,tv]+\overline{B(0,\varepsilon)}\right)\neq\emptyset\Big\},

(5.4)

where
[0,tv]+\overline{B(0,\varepsilon)}
denotes:

\displaystyle[0,tv]+\overline{B(0,\varepsilon)}=\big\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\ /\ \exists s\in[0,t],y\in\overline{B(0,\varepsilon)}\ /\ x=sv+y\big\}.

(5.5)

In addition, if the distribution
c
of scatterers belongs to
X_{1}
, we define the point
x_{1}
as:

\displaystyle x_{1}=t_{1}v\hskip 14.22636pt\text{where}\hskip 14.22636ptt_{1}=\min\{s\geq 0\ /\ sv\in c+\overline{B(0,\varepsilon)}\},

(5.6)

and the set:

\displaystyle Y_{1}=Y_{1}(t,\delta)=\{c\in X_{1}\ /\ \#\{c\cap\big(x_{1}+\overline{B(0,\varepsilon+\delta|v|}\big)\}=1\}.

(5.7)

Let us observe first that if
c\notin X_{0}
, then the tagged particle, initially at the position
x=0
, lies outside any scatterer. If in addition
c\notin X_{1}
, then there is no scatterer that intersects the segment
[0,tv]
. In this case, we define the dynamics of the tagged particle on the time interval
[0,t]
as:

\displaystyle\left(x(s),v(s)\right)=\left(sv,v\right)\hskip 8.53581pt\forall s\in[0,t].

(5.8)

If on the contrary
c\in X_{0}\cap X_{1}
, at least one scatterer is intersecting the segment
[0,tv]
. In this case, we have to distinguish between two situations.

If
c\in X_{0}\cap X_{1}\cap Y_{1}
, in particular a single scatterer intersects the point
x_{1}
with probability
1
(because the center of such a scatterer has to be at distance exactly
\varepsilon
from
x_{1}
), so that we can define in a unique way
v^{\prime}
, using the the reflection law (
2.7
), and choosing the angular parameter
\omega
as
(c_{i_{1}}-x_{1})/\varepsilon
, where
i_{1}
is the index of the scatterer that intersects
x_{1}
. In addition, we know that no scatterer will intersect the segment
[x_{1},x_{1}+\delta v^{\prime}]
, and since
|v^{\prime}|\leq|v|
, we define the dynamics of the tagged particle as:

\displaystyle\left(x(s),v(s)\right)=\left(sv,v\right)\hskip 8.53581pt\forall s\in[0,t_{1}[,

(5.9)

\displaystyle\left(x(s),v(s)\right)=\left(t_{1}v+(s-t_{1})v^{\prime},v^{\prime}\right)=\left(x_{1}+(s-t_{1})v^{\prime},v^{\prime}\right)\hskip 8.53581pt\forall s\in[t_{1},t_{1}+\delta].

(5.10)

so that in particular we know that the tagged particle will undergo a single collision on the time interval
[0,\delta]
.

If now
c\in X_{0}\cap X_{1}\cap Y_{1}^{c}
, then in particular:

\displaystyle\#\{c\cap\big(x_{1}+\overline{B(0,\varepsilon+\delta)}\big)\}\geq 2.

(5.11)

In addition, by definition of
x_{1}
, we have
\#\{c\cap B(x_{1},\varepsilon)\}=0
, so that if
c\in X_{0}\cap X_{\cap}Y_{1}^{c}
we have:

\displaystyle\#\{c\cap\big(\overline{B(x_{1},\varepsilon+\delta)}\backslash B(x_{1},\varepsilon)\big)\}\geq 2.

(5.12)

Since the measure of
\overline{B(x_{1},\varepsilon+\delta)}\backslash B(x_{1},\varepsilon)
is
C(d,\varepsilon)\delta
, where
C(d,\varepsilon)
is a number that depends only on the dimension
d
and the size
\varepsilon
of the scatterers, the probability that the distribution of scatterers
c
belongs to
X_{0}\cap X_{1}\cap Y_{1}^{c}
is given by:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(c\in X_{0}\cap X_{1}\cap Y_{1}^{c})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k\geq 2}e^{-\mu\left|\overline{B(x_{1},\varepsilon+\delta)}\backslash B(x_{1},\varepsilon)\right|}\mu^{k}\frac{\left|\overline{B(x_{1},\varepsilon+\delta)}\backslash B(x_{1},\varepsilon)\right|^{k}}{k!}\leq C(d,\varepsilon)\mu^{2}\delta^{2}.

(5.13)

We proceed recursively: we assume now that we define the dynamics of the particle globally on
[0,t]
if
c\in\left(X_{0}\cap X_{1}^{c}\right)\cup\left(X_{0}\cap X_{1}\cap Y_{1}\cap X_{2}^{c}\right)\cup\dots\cup\left(X_{0}\cap X_{1}\cap Y_{1}\cap\dots\cap X_{k-2}\cap Y_{k-2}\cap X_{k-1}^{c}\right)
, and on the time interval
[0,t_{k}+\delta]
if
c\in X_{0}\cap X_{1}\cap Y_{1}\cap\dots\cap X_{k-1}\cap Y_{k-1}
, as:

\displaystyle\left(x(s),v(s)\right)=\big(sv,v\big)\hskip 8.53581pt\forall\,s\in[0,t_{1}[,

(5.14)

\displaystyle\left(x(s),v(s)\right)=\big(t_{1}v+(s-t_{1})v^{\prime},v^{\prime}\big)=\big(x_{1}+(s-t_{1})v^{\prime},v^{\prime}\big)\hskip 8.53581pt\forall s\in[t_{1},t_{2}[,

(5.15)

\displaystyle\dots

\displaystyle\left(x(s),v(s)\right)=\Big(\sum_{j=1}^{k-1}t_{j}v^{(j-1)}+(s-t_{k-1})v^{(k-1)},v^{(k-1)}\Big)

\displaystyle\hskip 137.99586pt=\big(x_{k-1}+(s-t_{k-1})v^{(k-1)},v^{(k-1)}\big)\hskip 8.53581pt\forall\,s\in[t_{k-1},t_{k-1}+\delta].

(5.16)

Introducing then the set:

\displaystyle X_{k}=\big\{c\in X_{0}\cap X_{1}\cap Y_{1}\cap\dots\cap X_{k-1}\cap Y_{k-1}\ /\ c\cap\Big(\big[x_{k-1},x_{k-1}+t\frac{v^{(k-1)}}{|v^{(k-1)}|}\big]+\overline{B(0,\varepsilon)}\Big)\neq 0\big\},

(5.17)

we observe that if
c\in X_{0}\cap X_{1}\cap Y_{1}\cap\dots\cap X_{k}^{c}
, we can define globally on
[0,t]
the dynamics of the tagged particle, by completing the definition (
5.14
)-(
5.16
) as:

\displaystyle\left(x(t),v(t)\right)=\big(x_{k-1}+(s-t_{k-1})v^{(k-1)},v^{(k-1)}\big)\hskip 8.53581pt\forall\,s\in[t_{k-1},t].

(5.18)

If now
c\in X_{0}\cap X_{1}\cap Y_{1}\cap\dots\cap X_{k}
, we introduce the position
x_{k}
and the set
Y_{k}
, defined as:

\displaystyle x_{k}=x_{k-1}+(t_{k}-t_{k-1})v^{(k-1)}\hskip 8.53581pt\text{where}\hskip 8.53581ptt_{k}=\min\{s\geq t_{k-1}\ /\ x_{k-1}+(s-t_{k-1})v^{(k-1)}\in c+\overline{B(0,\varepsilon)}\}

(5.19)

and

\displaystyle Y_{k}=\{c\in X_{1}\cap Y_{1}\cap\dots\cap X_{k}\ /\ \#\{c\cap\big(x_{k}+\overline{B(0,\varepsilon+\delta}\big)\}=1\}.

(5.20)

As before, we have:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mu}(c\in X_{0}\cap X_{1}\cap Y_{1}\cap\dots\cap X_{k}\cap Y_{k}^{c})\leq C(d,\varepsilon)\mu^{2}\delta^{2},

(5.21)

and if
c\in X_{0}\cap X_{1}\cap Y_{1}\cap\dots\cap X_{k}\cap Y_{k}
,
x_{k}
intersects a single scatterer
i_{k}
with probability
1
, so that we can complete the definition of the dynamics of the tagged particle as:

\displaystyle\big(x(s),v(s)\big)=\big(x_{k-1}+(s-t_{k-1})v^{(k-1)},v^{(k-1)}\big)\hskip 8.53581pt\forall\,s\in[t_{k-1},t_{k}[

(5.22)

\displaystyle\big(x(s),v(s)\big)=\big(x_{k-1}+(t_{k}-t_{k-1})v^{(k-1)}+(s-t_{k})v^{(k)},v^{(k)}\big)\hskip 8.53581pt\forall\,s\in[t_{k},t_{k}+\delta].

(5.23)

In summary, we defined recursively the dynamics of the tagged particle, either on the whole time interval
[0,t]
, or on the time interval
[0,t_{k}+\delta]
for any
k\geq 1
if
c\in(X_{0}\cap X_{1}^{c})\cup(X_{0}\cap X_{1}\cap Y_{1}\cap X_{2}^{c})\cup\dots\cup(X_{0}\cap X_{1}\cap Y_{1}\cap\dots X_{k}\cap Y_{k})
.

On the one hand, since by construction we have
t_{k+1}-t_{k}\geq\delta\hskip 2.84526pt\forall\,k
, we need only
t/\delta
iterations to define the dynamics on the whole time interval
[0,t]
.

On the other hand, the dynamics is not defined if
c\in X_{0}
, or if
c\in(X_{0}\cap X_{1}\cap Y_{1}^{c})\cup(X_{0}\cap X_{1}\cap Y_{1}\dots\cap X_{k}\cap Y_{k}^{c})
. But since we have, for
k_{0}
the smallest integer larger or equal to
t/\delta
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mu}\big((X_{0}\cap X_{1}\cap Y_{1}^{c})\cup\dots\cup(X_{0}\cap X_{1}\cap Y_{1}\dots\cap X_{k_{0}}\cap Y_{k_{0}}^{c})\big)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{k_{0}}\mathbb{P}\big(X_{0}\cap X_{1}\cap Y_{1}\dots\cap X_{k}\cap Y_{k}^{c}\big)

\displaystyle\leq C(d,\varepsilon)\mu^{2}k_{0}\delta^{2}.

(5.24)

Now, for all
\delta>0
such that
t/\delta
is an integer, we consider the intersection:

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\text{patho.}}(t)=\bigcap_{\begin{subarray}{c}\delta>0\\
t/\delta\in\mathbb{N}^{*}\end{subarray}}\Big[(X_{0}\cap X_{1}\cap Y_{1}(\delta)^{c})\cup\dots\cup(X_{0}\cap X_{1}\cap Y_{1}(\delta)\dots\cap X_{k_{0}}(\delta)\cap Y_{k_{0}}(\delta)^{c})\big)\Big].

(5.25)

By (
5
), we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mu}\big(\mathcal{P}_{\text{patho.}}(t)\big)=0,

(5.26)

and also, if
c\in X_{0}\cap\mathcal{P}(t)^{c}
, that the dynamics of the tagged particle is defined on the whole time interval
[0,t]
. In addition, if
c\in X_{0}\cap\mathcal{P}(t)^{c}
, there exists
\delta_{0}>0
such that all the collisions of the trajectory of the tagged particles are separated by a time interval larger than
\delta_{0}
.

Finally, we can repeat the argument for a countable sequence of times
(t_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
which tends to infinity as
n\rightarrow+\infty
, to obtain that the dynamics of the tagged particle is defined on the whole time interval
[0,+\infty[
, with probability
1
. The proof of Proposition
8
is complete.
∎

Remark 7
.

As in the original proof of Alexander for the global well-posedness of the elastic hard sphere system, the key argument is that, except for a pathological set of measure
\delta^{2}
, we can define the dynamics further on a time interval of length
\delta
. In the original proof, the pathological set corresponds to the configurations in the phase space such that at least two pairs of particles are at a distance smaller than
\delta
. In the present case, we rely on the property of a Poisson process: having two scatterers or more in an annulus of radius
\delta
(or, in general dimension
d
, in the difference of two concentric balls with radii that differ by
\delta
) has a probability smaller than
\delta^{2}
.

We observe also that the proof can be reused without any change in the case when the interaction with the scatterers is more general, provided only that the norm of the velocity of the tagged particle is non-increasing along the collisions.

6
Conclusion

In the present paper we presented a rigorous derivation of the inelastic linear Boltzmann equation (
1.1
), from the inelastic Lorentz gas, in the Boltzmann-Grad limit (Theorem
1
). To the best of our knowledge, this result constitutes the first rigorous derivation of an inelastic version of the Boltzmann equation.

To achieve such a derivation, we proved the absence of inelastic collapse in the forward in time dynamics of the inelastic Lorentz gas (Proposition
8
), and the analog of Alexander’s theorem in our setting, ensuring that the dynamics of the particle system is well-posed.

In addition, we relied on the series representation (
1
) of the solutions of the adjoint equation (
2.31
). Assuming that an exponential moment of the initial datum is finite, we showed the convergence of such a series. This result allowed us to proof the existence of weak solutions to the inelastic linear Boltzmann equation (Proposition
2
). Under the same assumption, we also proved the convergence of the series (
3.1
), which provided the existence of explicit strong solutions to the linear inelastic Boltzmann equation (Theorem
2
).

We further remark that we obtained explicit estimates on the measures of the pathological sets preventing the Markovian behaviour of the limit process, i.e. the configurations of scatterers leading to recollisions or interferences, hence allowing us to provide a quantitative derivation of the linear inelastic Boltzmann equation.

Nevertheless, the rigorous derivation we established in this paper holds only in terms of weak convergence of the distribution function (
1.9
) of the microscopic tagged particles towards the associated solution of (
1.1
). In future work, our aim is to perform the derivation in the stronger
L^{p}
topology, in the same spirit of the original proof obtained in the elastic case by Gallavotti
[
37
]
. Another question that it is worth investigating is the analysis of the long-time behaviour of solutions to the linear inelastic Boltzmann equation (
1.1
). In particular, the characterization of the decay of the temperature (Haff’s law) remains to be proved.

Appendix A
Proof of the series representation of the solutions to the adjoint equation (
2.31
)

Proof of Proposition
1
.

For any integer
n\geq 0
, we introduce the function
\varphi_{n}
defined as:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\varphi}_{n}(t,x,v)

\displaystyle=e^{-C_{d}|v|t}\varphi(x+tv,v)+\sum_{k=1}^{n}\int_{t_{1}=0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\omega_{1}}}\hskip-8.53581pt\dots\int_{t_{k}=0}^{t_{k-1}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\omega_{k}}}e^{\big[\sum_{j=1}^{k}C_{d}|v^{(j-1)}|(t_{j}-t_{j-1})-C_{d}|v^{(k)}|t_{k}\big]}

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\times\prod_{l=1}^{k}\big|v^{(l-1)}\cdot\omega_{l}\big|\varphi(x+\sum_{m=1}^{k}\big(t_{m-1}-t_{m}\big)v^{(m-1)}+t_{k}v^{(k)},v^{(k)})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{k}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{k}\dots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{1}.

(A.1)

The function
\widetilde{\varphi}_{n}
is
\mathcal{C}^{1}
, and we have:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\widetilde{\varphi}_{n}(t,x,v)

\displaystyle=v\cdot\nabla_{x}\varphi(x+tv,v)e^{-C_{d}|v|t}-C_{d}|v|\varphi(x+tv,v)e^{-C_{d}|v|t}+I_{1}+I_{2}+I_{3}

(A.2)

with

\displaystyle I_{1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\omega_{1}}}\hskip-8.53581pt\dots\int_{t_{k}=0}^{t_{k-1}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\omega_{k}}}e^{\big[\sum_{j=2}^{k}C_{d}|v^{(j-1)}|(t_{j}-t_{j-1})-C_{d}|v^{(k)}|t_{k}\big]}\prod_{l=1}^{k}\big|v^{(l-1)}\cdot\omega_{l}\big|

\displaystyle\hskip 128.0374pt\times\varphi(x+\sum_{m=2}^{k}\big(t_{m-1}-t_{m}\big)v^{(m-1)}+t_{k}v^{(k)},v^{(k)})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{k}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{k}\dots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{1},

(A.3)

\displaystyle I_{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\int_{t_{1}=0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\omega_{1}}}\hskip-8.53581pt\dots\int_{t_{k}=0}^{t_{k-1}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\omega_{k}}}\big(-C_{d}|v|\big)e^{\big[\sum_{j=1}^{k}C_{d}|v^{(j-1)}|(t_{j}-t_{j-1})-C_{d}|v^{(k)}|t_{k}\big]}\prod_{l=1}^{k}\big|v^{(l-1)}\cdot\omega_{l}\big|

\displaystyle\hskip 128.0374pt\times\varphi(x+\sum_{m=1}^{k}\big(t_{m-1}-t_{m}\big)v^{(m-1)}+t_{k}v^{(k)},v^{(k)})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{k}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{k}\dots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{1}

(A.4)

and

\displaystyle I_{3}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\int_{t_{1}=0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\omega_{1}}}\hskip-8.53581pt\dots\int_{t_{k}=0}^{t_{k-1}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\omega_{k}}}e^{\big[\sum_{j=1}^{k}C_{d}|v^{(j-1)}|(t_{j}-t_{j-1})-C_{d}|v^{(k)}|t_{k}\big]}\prod_{l=1}^{k}\big|v^{(l-1)}\cdot\omega_{l}\big|

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt\times v\cdot\nabla_{x}\varphi(x+\sum_{m=1}^{k}\big(t_{m-1}-t_{m}\big)v^{(m-1)}+t_{k}v^{(k)},v^{(k)})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{k}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{k}\dots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{1}.

(A.5)

Observe that the effect of the time derivative on the sum produces two terms. On the one hand for the first term, the integral in
t_{1}
disappears, and the integrand is evaluated in the variable
t_{1}
at
t
. Therefore, the first term in the sum inside the exponential vanishes, as well as the first term in
\varphi
below the integral. On the other hand, the second term is obtained by differentiating with respect to
t
below the integrals.

Identifying the different terms in the previous computation, we find that
\varphi_{n}
solves the equation:

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\widetilde{\varphi}_{n}=v\cdot\nabla_{x}\widetilde{\varphi}_{n}-C_{d}|v|\widetilde{\varphi}_{n}+\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\omega}}\hskip-8.53581pt|v\cdot\omega|\widetilde{\varphi}_{n-1}(t,x,v^{\prime})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega.

(A.6)

In addition, the sequences
\big(\widetilde{\varphi}_{n}\big)_{n\geq 0}
,
\big(\partial_{t}\widetilde{\varphi}_{n}\big)_{n\geq 0}
and
\big(\nabla_{x}\widetilde{\varphi}_{n}\big)_{n\geq 0}
being Cauchy sequences locally uniformly in
(t,x,v)
, they converge locally uniformly towards respective limits
\widetilde{\psi}
,
\widetilde{g}
and
\widetilde{h}
, that satisfy:

\displaystyle\widetilde{g}(t,x,v)=v\cdot\widetilde{h}(t,x,v)-C_{d}|v|\widetilde{\psi}(t,x,v)+\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\omega}}\hskip-8.53581pt|v\cdot\omega|\widetilde{\psi}(t,x,v^{\prime})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega,

(A.7)

and where
\widetilde{\psi}
is given by the expression (
1
). Since in addition
\widetilde{\varphi}_{n}
and its derivatives are converging locally uniformly, we have
\partial_{t}\widetilde{\psi}=\widetilde{g}
and
\nabla_{x}\widetilde{\psi}=\widetilde{h}
, so that
\widetilde{\psi}
solves the adjoint equation (
2.31
) of the linear inelastic Boltzmann equation (
1.6
), concluding the proof of the first part of Proposition
1
.

Conversely, for a solution
\widetilde{\varphi}
of the adjoint equation (
2.31
) with initial datum
\varphi
, integrating
\widetilde{\varphi}
along the characteristics it is possible to show by recursion that, for all
n\geq 0
:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\varphi}(t,x,v)

\displaystyle=e^{-C_{d}|v|t}\varphi(x+tv,v)+\sum_{k=1}^{n}\int_{t_{1}=0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\omega_{1}}}\hskip-8.53581pt\dots\int_{t_{k}=0}^{t_{k-1}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\omega_{k}}}e^{\big[\sum_{j=1}^{k}C_{d}|v^{(j-1)}|(t_{j}-t_{j-1})-C_{d}|v^{(k)}|t_{k}\big]}\prod_{l=1}^{k}\big|v^{(l-1)}\cdot\omega_{l}\big|

\displaystyle\hskip 128.0374pt\times\varphi(x+\sum_{m=1}^{k}\big(t_{m-1}-t_{m}\big)v^{(m-1)}+t_{k}v^{(k)},v^{(k)})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{k}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{k}\dots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{1}

\displaystyle+\int_{t_{1}=0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\omega_{1}}}\hskip-8.53581pt\dots\int_{t_{n}=0}^{t_{n-1}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\omega_{n}}}\int_{t_{n+1}=0}^{t_{n}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\omega_{n+1}}}e^{\big[\sum_{j=1}^{n+1}C_{d}|v^{(j-1)}|(t_{j}-t_{j-1})\big]}\prod_{l=1}^{n+1}\big|v^{(l-1)}\cdot\omega_{l}\big|

\displaystyle\hskip 99.58464pt\times\widetilde{\varphi}(t_{n+1},x+\sum_{m=1}^{n+1}\big(t_{m-1}-t_{m}\big)v^{(m-1)},v^{(n+1)})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{n+1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{n+1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{n}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{n}\dots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{1}.

(A.8)

The difference between
\widetilde{\varphi}
and
\widetilde{\varphi}_{n}
is exactly equal to the last term in the previous formula (
A
). We call
R
such a term. Since by assumption
\widetilde{\varphi}
is a
\mathcal{C}^{1}
function, it is locally bounded, and we have:

\displaystyle\big|R\big|

\displaystyle\leq{\left|\kern-1.29167pt\left|\mathds{1}_{[0,t]\times B(x,t|v|)\times B(0,|v|)}\widetilde{\varphi}\right|\kern-1.29167pt\right|}_{\infty}\Big|\int_{t_{1}=0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\omega_{1}}}\hskip-8.53581pt\dots\int_{t_{n}=0}^{t_{n-1}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\omega_{n}}}\int_{t_{n+1}=0}^{t_{n}}\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}_{\omega_{n+1}}}|v|^{n+1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{n+1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{n+1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{n}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{n}\dots\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\omega_{1}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t_{1}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq{\left|\kern-1.29167pt\left|\mathds{1}_{[0,t]\times B(x,t|v|)\times B(0,|v|)}\widetilde{\varphi}\right|\kern-1.29167pt\right|}_{\infty}\frac{t^{n+1}|v|^{n+1}|\mathbb{S}|^{n+1}}{(n+1)!},

(A.9)

we deduce that
\widetilde{\varphi}=\widetilde{\psi}
, concluding the proof of the second part of Proposition
1
.
∎

Appendix B
Proofs of the geometrical lemmas
1
and
2

B.1
Estimate coming from the colinearity condition after scattering

Proof of Lemma
1
.

The proof of Lemma
1
is divided into the following steps:

—

we write the scalar product
\frac{v^{\prime}}{|v^{\prime}|}\cdot p
in coordinates, in terms of the three vectors
v
,
\omega
and
\sigma
(
\sigma
being the angular parameter such that the renormalized post-collisional velocity obtained from
v
with angular parameter
\sigma
is
p
),

—

we analyze in detail the particular case when
v
,
\omega
and
\sigma
are coplanar,

—

we finally prove that the case when
v
,
\omega
and
\sigma
are not coplanar can be treated as a consequence of the result obtained in the previous particular case.

The scalar product
\frac{v^{\prime}}{|v^{\prime}|}\cdot p
, that we will denote as
f
in the rest of this proof, can be rewritten as:

\displaystyle f=\frac{v^{\prime}}{|v^{\prime}|}\cdot p

\displaystyle=\left[\frac{v-(1+r)\big(v\cdot\omega\big)\omega}{\big|v-(1+r)\big(v\cdot\omega\big)\omega\big|}\right]\cdot\left[\frac{v-(1+r)\big(v\cdot\sigma\big)\sigma}{\big|v-(1+r)\big(v\cdot\sigma\big)\sigma\big|}\right]

\displaystyle=\left[\frac{\frac{v}{|v|}-(1+r)\big(\frac{v}{|v|}\cdot\omega\big)\omega}{\big|\frac{v}{|v|}-(1+r)\big(\frac{v}{|v|}\cdot\omega\big)\omega\big|}\right]\cdot\left[\frac{\frac{v}{|v|}-(1+r)\big(\frac{v}{|v|}\cdot\sigma\big)\sigma}{\big|\frac{v}{|v|}-(1+r)\big(\frac{v}{|v|}\cdot\sigma\big)\sigma\big|}\right].

(B.1)

The norms in the denominator are given by the following expressions:

\displaystyle\Big|\frac{v}{|v|}-(1+r)\big(\frac{v}{|v|}\cdot\omega\big)\omega\Big|

\displaystyle=\sqrt{1-2(1+r)\Big(\frac{v}{|v|}\cdot\omega\Big)^{2}+(1+r)^{2}\Big(\frac{v}{|v|}\cdot\omega\Big)^{2}}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{1-(1-r^{2})\Big(\frac{v}{|v|}\cdot\omega\Big)^{2}},

(B.2)

and similarly:

\displaystyle\Big|\frac{v}{|v|}-(1+r)\big(\frac{v}{|v|}\cdot\omega\big)\omega\Big|=\sqrt{1-(1-r^{2})\Big(\frac{v}{|v|}\cdot\sigma\Big)^{2}}.

(B.3)

Expanding the scalar product in the numerator of (
B.1
), we find:

\displaystyle f=\frac{1-(1+r)\big(\frac{v}{|v|}\cdot\sigma\big)^{2}-(1+r)\big(\frac{v}{|v|}\cdot\omega\big)^{2}+(1+r)^{2}\big(v\cdot\sigma\big)\big(v\cdot\omega\big)\big(\sigma\cdot\omega\big)}{\sqrt{\Big[1-(1-r^{2})\big(\frac{v}{|v|}\cdot\sigma\big)^{2}\Big]\cdot\Big[1-(1-r^{2})\big(\frac{v}{|v|}\cdot\omega\big)^{2}\Big]}}\cdotp

(B.4)

From this point, it will be convenient to decompose the unit vector
\omega
as its component along the plane spanned by
v
and
\sigma
and its orthogonal, that is we write:

\displaystyle\omega=a\frac{v}{|v|}+b\sigma+u,

(B.5)

with
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
, and
u\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
u\cdot v=u\cdot\sigma=0
.

In what follows, we will denote the following scalar products as:

\displaystyle\frac{v}{|v|}\cdot\sigma=\cos\theta_{0}\hskip 8.53581pt\text{and}\hskip 8.53581pt\frac{v}{|v|}\cdot\omega=\cos\theta,

(B.6)

where
\theta_{0}
is the orientated angle from
v
to
\sigma
, and
\theta
is the orientated angle from
v
to
\omega
(after having chosen an orientation on the planes respectively spanned by
(v,\sigma)
, and
(v,\omega)
).
\omega
being unitary, we have:

\displaystyle 1=|\omega|^{2}=a^{2}+2ab\cos\theta+b^{2}+|u|^{2}.

(B.7)

With these notations, we have:

\displaystyle\frac{v}{|v|}\cdot\omega=\cos\theta=a+b\cos\theta_{0}\hskip 8.53581pt\text{and}\hskip 8.53581pt\sigma\cdot\omega=a\cos\theta_{0}+b.

(B.8)

Finally, considering the scalar product
f
at
v
,
\sigma
and
|u|
fixed, it is possible to express
\sigma\cdot\omega
as a function of
\cos\theta
by writing:

\displaystyle\sigma\cdot\omega=a\cos\theta_{0}+b=\cos\theta_{0}\big(a+b\cos\theta_{0}\big)+b\sin^{2}\theta_{0}=\cos\theta_{0}\cos\theta+\sin\theta_{0}\,\text{sgn}(b)\sqrt{1-|u|^{2}-\cos^{2}\theta},

(B.9)

where we used the identity:

\displaystyle 1=\cos^{2}\theta+b^{2}\sin^{2}\theta_{0}+|u|^{2}.

(B.10)

Observe that the sign of
\sin\theta_{0}
has been taken positive by assumption, which can always been done by choosing appropriately the orientation in the plane spanned by
v
and
\sigma
. Nevertheless, the sign of
b
has to be taken into account.

In the end, the scalar product
f
can be rewritten as:

\displaystyle f=\frac{1-(1+r)\cos^{2}\theta_{0}-(1+r)\cos^{2}\theta+(1+r)^{2}\cos\theta_{0}\cos\theta\big(\cos\theta_{0}\cos\theta+\sin\theta_{0}\,\text{sgn}(b)\sqrt{1-|u|^{2}-\cos^{2}\theta}\big)}{\sqrt{\Big[1-(1-r^{2})\cos^{2}\theta_{0}\Big]\cdot\Big[1-(1-r^{2})\cos^{2}\theta\Big]}}\cdotp

(B.11)

Obtaining the general expression (
B.11
) of the scalar product
f
in coordinates concludes the first step of the proof.

Turning to the second step, we now assume that the three vectors
v
,
\sigma
and
\omega
are coplanar, that is, we assume that
u=0
. We now study
f
under this assumption.

In such a case, the sign of
\sin\theta
is equal to the sign of
b
, and we have:

\displaystyle\cos\theta_{0}\cos\theta+\sin\theta_{0}\,\text{sgn}(b)\sqrt{1-|u|^{2}-\cos^{2}\theta}=\cos\theta_{0}\cos\theta+\sin\theta_{0}\,\text{sgn}\big(\sin\theta\big)\sqrt{1-\cos^{2}\theta}=\cos(\theta-\theta_{0}).

(B.12)

Therefore, the scalar product
f
can be rewritten as:

\displaystyle f=\frac{1-(1+r)\cos^{2}\theta_{0}-(1+r)\cos^{2}\theta+(1+r)^{2}\cos\theta_{0}\cos\theta\cos(\theta-\theta_{0})}{\sqrt{\Big[1-(1-r^{2})\cos^{2}\theta_{0}\Big]\cdot\Big[1-(1-r^{2})\cos^{2}\theta\Big]}}\cdotp

(B.13)

We consider
f
as a function of
\theta
, with
\theta_{0}
fixed. Its derivative with respect to
\theta
writes:

\displaystyle\partial_{\theta}f

\displaystyle=\frac{\Big[2(1+r)\cos\theta\sin\theta-(1+r)^{2}\cos\theta_{0}\sin\theta\cos(\theta-\theta_{0})-(1+r)^{2}\cos\theta_{0}\cos\theta\sin(\theta-\theta_{0})\Big]\big[1-(1-r^{2})\cos^{2}\theta\big]}{\Big[1-(1-r^{2})\cos^{2}\theta_{0}\Big]^{1/2}\cdot\Big[1-(1-r^{2})\cos^{2}\theta\Big]^{3/2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt-\frac{\Big[1-(1+r)\cos^{2}\theta_{0}-(1+r)\cos^{2}\theta+(1+r)^{2}\cos\theta_{0}\cos\theta\cos(\theta-\theta_{0})\Big]\big[(1-r^{2})\cos\theta\sin\theta\big]}{\Big[1-(1-r^{2})\cos^{2}\theta_{0}\Big]^{1/2}\cdot\Big[1-(1-r^{2})\cos^{2}\theta\Big]^{3/2}}\cdotp

(B.14)

Gathering the terms:

\displaystyle 2(1+r)\cos\theta\sin\theta-(1-r^{2})\cos\theta\sin\theta=(1+r)^{2}\cos\theta\sin\theta,

(B.15)

we find:

\displaystyle\partial_{\theta}f

\displaystyle=\frac{(1+r)^{2}\cos\theta\sin\theta-2(1+r)(1-r^{2})\cos^{3}\theta\sin\theta}{\Big[1-(1-r^{2})\cos^{2}\theta_{0}\Big]^{1/2}\cdot\Big[1-(1-r^{2})\cos^{2}\theta\Big]^{3/2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt-\frac{\Big[(1+r)^{2}\cos\theta_{0}\sin\theta\cos(\theta-\theta_{0})+(1+r)^{2}\cos\theta_{0}\cos\theta\sin(\theta-\theta_{0})\Big]\big[1-(1-r^{2})\cos^{2}\theta\big]}{\Big[1-(1-r^{2})\cos^{2}\theta_{0}\Big]^{1/2}\cdot\Big[1-(1-r^{2})\cos^{2}\theta\Big]^{3/2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\frac{\Big[(1+r)\cos^{2}\theta_{0}+(1+r)\cos^{2}\theta-(1+r)^{2}\cos\theta_{0}\cos\theta\cos(\theta-\theta_{0})\Big]\big[(1-r^{2})\cos\theta\sin\theta\big]}{\Big[1-(1-r^{2})\cos^{2}\theta_{0}\Big]^{1/2}\cdot\Big[1-(1-r^{2})\cos^{2}\theta\Big]^{3/2}}\cdotp

(B.16)

It is now possible to factor by
(1+r)^{2}
in all the terms of the derivative. Denoting now by
g
the quantity:

\displaystyle g=\frac{\Big[1-(1-r^{2})\cos^{2}\theta_{0}\Big]^{1/2}\cdot\Big[1-(1-r^{2})\cos^{2}\theta\Big]^{3/2}\partial_{\theta}f}{(1+r)^{2}},

(B.17)

and observing that the terms
(1+r)^{2}(1-r^{2})\cos\theta_{0}\cos^{2}\theta\sin\theta\cos(\theta-\theta_{0})
in the second and third lines cancel each other, we obtain:

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle=\cos\theta\sin\theta-2(1-r)\cos^{3}\theta\sin\theta-\big[\cos\theta_{0}\sin\theta\cos(\theta-\theta_{0})+\cos\theta_{0}\cos\theta\sin(\theta-\theta_{0})\big]

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+(1-r^{2})\cos\theta_{0}\cos^{3}\theta\sin(\theta-\theta_{0})+(1-r)\big[\cos^{2}\theta_{0}+\cos^{2}\theta\big]\cos\theta\sin\theta

\displaystyle=\sin(\theta-\theta_{0})\Big[-\cos\theta_{0}\cos\theta+(1-r^{2})\cos\theta_{0}\cos^{3}\theta\Big]

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\sin\theta\Big[\cos\theta-2(1-r)\cos^{3}\theta-\cos\theta_{0}\cos(\theta-\theta_{0})+(1-r)\big[\cos^{2}\theta_{0}+\cos^{2}\theta\big]\cos\theta\Big]

\displaystyle=\sin(\theta-\theta_{0})\Big[(1-r^{2})\cos\theta_{0}\cos^{3}\theta-\cos\theta_{0}\cos\theta\Big]

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\sin\theta\Big[\cos\theta+(1-r)\big[\cos^{2}\theta_{0}-\cos^{2}\theta\big]\cos\theta-\cos\theta_{0}\cos(\theta-\theta_{0})\Big].

(B.18)

In the case when
\theta=\theta_{0}
,
\omega
and
\sigma
are colinear, so
v^{\prime}
and
p
are colinear in this case. We have in particular
f=1
. Since
\frac{v^{\prime}}{|v^{\prime}|}\cdot p
is the scalar product of two unit vectors,
\theta=\theta_{0}
corresponds therefore to a maximum of the function
f
. As a consequence, to obtain a simpler expression of the derivative of
f
, we will factor the expression of
g
by
\sin(\theta-\theta_{0})
, that is a factor of already some terms.

To do so, we use the following identity:

\displaystyle\cos^{2}\theta_{0}-\cos^{2}\theta=\sin^{2}\theta-\sin^{2}\theta_{0}=\sin(\theta+\theta_{0})\sin(\theta-\theta_{0}).

(B.19)

Therefore we have:

\displaystyle\cos\theta

\displaystyle+(1-r)\big[\cos^{2}\theta_{0}-\cos^{2}\theta\big]\cos\theta-\cos\theta_{0}\cos(\theta-\theta_{0})

\displaystyle=\cos\theta+(1-r)\sin(\theta+\theta_{0})\sin(\theta-\theta_{0})\cos\theta-\cos^{2}\theta_{0}\cos\theta-\cos\theta_{0}\sin\theta_{0}\sin\theta

\displaystyle=(1-r)\sin(\theta+\theta_{0})\sin(\theta-\theta_{0})\cos\theta+\sin^{2}\theta_{0}\cos\theta-\cos\theta_{0}\sin\theta_{0}\sin\theta

\displaystyle=\Big[(1-r)\sin(\theta+\theta_{0})\cos\theta-\sin\theta_{0}\Big]\sin(\theta-\theta_{0}).

(B.20)

As a consequence,
g
can be rewritten as:

\displaystyle g

\displaystyle=\sin(\theta-\theta_{0})\Big[(1-r^{2})\cos\theta_{0}\cos^{3}\theta-\cos\theta_{0}\cos\theta+(1-r)\cos\theta\sin\theta\sin(\theta+\theta_{0})-\sin\theta_{0}\sin\theta\Big]

\displaystyle=\sin(\theta-\theta_{0})\Big[\Big((1-r^{2})\cos^{2}\theta-1+(1-r)\sin^{2}\theta\Big)\cos\theta_{0}\cos\theta+\Big((1-r)\cos^{2}\theta-1\Big)\sin\theta_{0}\sin\theta\Big]

\displaystyle=\sin(\theta-\theta_{0})\Big[\Big(-r^{2}\cos^{2}\theta-r\sin^{2}\theta\Big)\cos\theta_{0}\cos\theta+\Big(-r\cos^{2}\theta-\sin^{2}\theta\Big)\sin\theta_{0}\sin\theta\Big].

(B.21)

In the end, we find the following factorization for
g
:

\displaystyle g=\sin(\theta-\theta_{0})\Big(-r\cos^{2}\theta-\sin^{2}\theta\Big)\Big(r\cos\theta_{0}\cos\theta+\sin\theta_{0}\sin\theta\Big),

(B.22)

which allows to deduce that
g
is zero if and only if
\theta=\theta_{0}+k\pi
or
\theta_{1}+k\pi
(
k\in\mathbb{Z}
), where
\theta_{1}
is defined as:

\displaystyle\sin\theta_{1}=-\frac{r\cos\theta_{0}}{\sqrt{r^{2}\cos^{2}\theta_{0}+\sin^{2}\theta_{0}}}\hskip 8.53581pt\text{and}\hskip 8.53581pt\cos\theta_{1}=\frac{\sin\theta_{0}}{\sqrt{r^{2}\cos^{2}\theta_{0}+\sin^{2}\theta_{0}}}\cdotp

(B.23)

We observe now that the function
f
is
\pi
-periodic in
\theta
. Since there is exactly one of each of the angles of the form
\theta_{0}+k\pi
and
\theta_{1}+k\pi
in the interval
[\frac{\pi}{2},\frac{3\pi}{2}[
(this interval being chosen because we consider only pre-collisional configurations, that is such that
v\cdot\omega\leq 0
), decomposing the interval
[\frac{\pi}{2},\frac{3\pi}{2}[
into three intervals delimited respectively by
\frac{\pi}{2}
,
\theta_{0}+k_{1}\pi
,
\theta_{1}+k_{2}\pi
(
k_{1},k_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}
being chosen such that
\theta_{0}+k_{1}\pi,\theta_{1}+k_{2}\pi\in[\frac{\pi}{2},\frac{3\pi}{2}[
) we deduce that the function
f
is monotone on each of these three intervals.

We observed already that
f(\theta_{0})=1
, because:

\displaystyle f(\theta_{0})=\frac{1-2(1+r)\cos^{2}\theta_{0}+(1+r)^{2}\cos^{2}\theta_{0}}{1-(1-r^{2})\cos^{2}\theta_{0}}=1.

(B.24)

In the case when
\theta=\theta_{1}
we have:

\displaystyle f(\theta_{1})

\displaystyle=\frac{1-(1+r)\cos^{2}\theta_{0}-(1+r)\cos^{2}\theta_{1}+(1+r)^{2}\cos\theta_{0}\cos\theta_{1}\Big(\cos\theta_{1}\cos\theta_{0}+\sin\theta_{1}\sin\theta_{0}\Big)}{\sqrt{\Big[1-(1-r^{2})\cos^{2}\theta_{0}\Big]\cdot\Big[1-(1-r^{2})\cos^{2}\theta_{1}\Big]}}

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt=\frac{1-(1+r)\cos^{2}\theta_{0}-(1+r)\displaystyle{\frac{\sin^{2}\theta_{0}}{r^{2}\cos^{2}\theta_{0}+\sin^{2}\theta_{0}}}+(1+r)^{2}\displaystyle{\frac{\cos^{2}\theta_{0}\sin^{2}\theta_{0}-r\cos^{2}\theta_{0}\sin^{2}\theta_{0}}{r^{2}\cos^{2}\theta_{0}+\sin^{2}\theta_{0}}}}{\sqrt{\Big[1-(1-r^{2})\cos^{2}\theta_{0}\Big]\cdot\Big[\displaystyle{\frac{r^{2}\cos^{2}\theta_{0}+\sin^{2}\theta_{0}-(1-r^{2})\sin^{2}\theta_{0}}{r^{2}\cos^{2}\theta_{0}+\sin^{2}\theta_{0}}}}\Big]}

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt=\frac{r^{2}\cos^{2}\theta_{0}+\sin^{2}\theta_{0}-(1+r)r^{2}\cos^{4}\theta_{0}-(1+r)\cos^{2}\theta_{0}\sin^{2}\theta_{0}-(1+r)\sin^{2}\theta_{0}+(1+r)^{2}(1-r)\cos^{2}\theta_{0}\sin^{2}\theta_{0}}{r\Big(r^{2}\cos^{2}\theta_{0}+\sin^{2}\theta_{0}\Big)}

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt=\frac{r\cos^{2}\theta_{0}-(1+r)r\cos^{4}\theta_{0}-(1+r)r\cos^{2}\theta_{0}\sin^{2}\theta_{0}-\sin^{2}\theta_{0}}{r^{2}\cos^{2}\theta_{0}+\sin^{2}\theta_{0}}=\frac{r\cos^{2}\theta_{0}-(1+r)r\cos^{2}\theta_{0}-\sin^{2}\theta_{0}}{r^{2}\cos^{2}\theta_{0}+\sin^{2}\theta_{0}}=-1.

(B.25)

As a consequence, the global extrema of
f
are reached exactly at
\theta=\theta_{0}
, and
\theta=\theta_{1}
, and there are no other local extrema on the whole interval
[\frac{\pi}{2},\frac{3\pi}{2}[
. In between two consecutive global extrema, which are necessarily distinct (the extrema
1
is followed by
-1
and vice versa),
f
is monotone.

We will now assume that:

\displaystyle\big|f(\theta)-1\big|\leq\delta,

(B.26)

for
\delta
small enough, and we will characterize such angles
\theta
. More precisely, we choose a positive real number
\mu_{1}>0
such that:

\displaystyle\big|r\cos\theta_{0}\cos\theta+\sin\theta_{0}\sin\theta\big|\geq\frac{r}{2}\hskip 8.53581pt\forall\,\theta\in[\theta_{0}-\mu_{1},\theta_{0}+\mu_{1}].

(B.27)

We also choose a positive real number
\mu_{2}>0
such that:

\displaystyle\mu_{2}\leq\min(\mu_{1},\frac{\pi}{2})\hskip 22.76219pt\text{and}\hskip 22.76219pt\frac{x}{2}\leq\sin(x)\hskip 8.53581pt\forall\,x\in[0,\mu_{2}],

(B.28)

and we finally define:

\displaystyle\delta_{0}=C(r)\int_{0}^{\mu_{2}}\frac{x}{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\hskip 14.22636pt\text{with}\hskip 14.22636ptC(r)=\frac{(1+r)^{2}}{2r^{2}}.

(B.29)

We consider now a positive number
\delta
smaller than
\delta_{0}
. By continuity, there exist
\theta_{\delta}^{\pm}
such that:

\displaystyle 1-f(\theta_{\delta}^{\pm})=\delta\hskip 8.53581pt\text{and}\hskip 8.53581pt\theta_{\delta}^{-}<\theta_{0}<\theta_{\delta}^{+}.

(B.30)

We denote by
\theta_{\delta}^{\pm}
the closest solutions to
\theta_{0}
of (
B.30
). We now estimate the distance between
\theta_{0}
and
\theta_{\delta}^{\pm}
. We will treat in detail only the case of
\theta_{\delta}^{-}
, since the case of
\theta_{\delta}^{+}
can be obtained with exactly the same arguments. We consider then the angle
\theta_{m}=\theta_{0}-\mu_{2}
, so that we have:

\displaystyle 1-f(\theta_{m})=\int_{\theta_{m}}^{\theta_{0}}\partial_{\theta}f(\theta)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta.

(B.31)

Turning to the derivative
f^{\prime}
, we observe that on the interval
[\theta_{m},\theta_{0}]
we have:

\displaystyle\big|\partial_{\theta}f\big|

\displaystyle=\frac{(1+r)^{2}\big|\sin(\theta-\theta_{0})\big(-r\cos^{2}\theta-\sin^{2}\theta\big)\big(r\cos\theta_{0}\cos\theta+\sin\theta_{0}\sin\theta\big)\big|}{\Big[1-(1-r^{2})\cos^{2}\theta_{0}\Big]^{1/2}\cdot\Big[1-(1-r^{2})\cos^{2}\theta\Big]^{3/2}}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{(1+r)^{2}}{r^{4}}\cdot r\cdot\frac{r}{2}\sin(\theta_{0}-\theta)

(B.32)

because
0\leq\theta_{0}-\theta_{m}\leq\mu_{1}
. In particular, the only zero of the derivative on this interval is at
\theta=\theta_{0}
because
\mu_{2}\leq\frac{\pi}{2}
, and therefore
\partial_{\theta}f
is non-negative on the whole interval
[\theta_{m},\theta_{0}]
, so that:

\displaystyle 1-f(\theta_{m})\geq C(r)\int_{\theta_{m}}^{\theta_{0}}\sin(\theta_{0}-\theta)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta.

(B.33)

Since we have by definition
\theta_{0}-\theta_{m}=\mu_{2}
, we have in particular:

\displaystyle\int_{\theta_{m}}^{\theta_{0}}\sin(\theta_{0}-\theta)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta=\int_{0}^{\mu_{2}}\sin(\theta)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta\geq\int_{0}^{\mu_{2}}\frac{\theta}{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta=\frac{\delta_{0}}{C(r)}\cdotp

(B.34)

Therefore, by continuity, there exists an angle
\theta_{\delta}^{-}\in[\theta_{m},\theta_{0}]
such that:

\displaystyle 1-f(\theta_{\delta}^{-})=\int_{\theta_{\delta}^{-}}^{\theta_{0}}\partial_{\theta}f(\theta)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta=\delta.

(B.35)

In particular, we deduce that:

\displaystyle\delta=\int_{\theta_{\delta}^{-}}^{\theta_{0}}\partial_{\theta}f(\theta)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta\geq C(r)\int_{0}^{\theta_{0}-\theta_{\delta}^{-}}\frac{\theta}{2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\theta=C(r)\frac{\big(\theta_{0}-\theta_{\delta}^{-}\big)^{2}}{4}\cdotp

(B.36)

As a consequence,
f
being monotone between the consecutive global extrema for which
f=\pm 1
, these extrema being reach at points of the form
\theta_{0}+k\pi
when
f(\theta)=1
and of the form
\theta_{1}+k\pi
when
f(\theta)=-1
, we deduce that:

\displaystyle f(\theta)\geq 1-\delta\hskip 14.22636pt\text{if and only if}\hskip 14.22636pt\theta\in[\theta_{\delta}^{-}+k\pi,\theta_{\delta}^{+}+k\pi]\ \text{for a certain }k\in\mathbb{Z},

(B.37)

and we have:

\displaystyle\theta_{0}-\frac{2\delta^{1/2}}{\sqrt{C(r)}}\leq\theta_{\delta}^{-}\leq\theta_{0}\leq\theta_{\delta}^{+}\leq\theta_{0}+\frac{2\delta^{1/2}}{\sqrt{C(r)}}\cdotp

(B.38)

The same approach applied to
\theta=\theta_{1}
leads to the existence of a similar family of intervals in which lies
\theta
if
f(\theta)\leq-1+\delta
, of which the size is estimated exactly in the same way.

Estimate (
B.38
) concludes the study of the scalar product
f
in the case when
|u|=0
.

As for the third and last step of the proof, we consider the general case
|u|\neq 0
. In such a case, the vectors
v
,
\sigma
and
\omega
are not coplanar. In particular, the plane spanned by
v
and
\omega
is not the same plane as the one spanned by
v
and
\sigma
, and the orientations of these two planes are a priori independent, so that the orientation of the plane spanned by
v
and
\omega
remains to be chosen. In order to make a consistent choice with the case when
|u|=0
, we define the orientation of the plane spanned by
v
and
\omega
so that the sign of
b
corresponds to the sign of
\sin\theta
(we recall that the angle
\theta
is the angle orientated from
v
to
\omega
).

With such a choice of orientation, according to the expression (
B.11
) the scalar product
f
writes:

\displaystyle f=\frac{1-(1+r)\cos^{2}\theta_{0}-(1+r)\cos^{2}\theta+(1+r)^{2}\cos\theta_{0}\cos\theta\Big(\cos\theta_{0}\cos\theta+\sin\theta_{0}\,\text{sgn}(\sin\theta)\sqrt{1-|u|^{2}-\cos^{2}\theta}\Big)}{\sqrt{\Big[1-(1-r^{2})\cos^{2}\theta_{0}\Big]\cdot\Big[1-(1-r^{2})\cos^{2}\theta\Big]}}\cdotp

(B.39)

Introducing the following function
g:x\in[0,1]\mapsto\displaystyle{\frac{1-(1+r)x}{\sqrt{1-(1-r^{2})x}}}
,
and computing its two first derivatives, we find:

\displaystyle g^{\prime}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{-2(1+r)\big(1-(1-r^{2})x\big)-\big(1-(1+r)x\big)\big(-(1-r^{2})\big)}{2\big(1-(1-r^{2})x\big)^{3/2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(1+r)\Big(-2+2(1-r^{2})x+(1-r)-(1-r^{2})x\Big)}{2\big(1-(1-r^{2})x\big)^{3/2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(1+r)^{2}\big((1-r)x-1\big)}{2\big(1-(1-r^{2})x\big)^{3/2}}\leq 0

(B.40)

and

\displaystyle g^{\prime\prime}(x)=(1+r)^{2}(1-r)\frac{\big((1-r^{2})x-1-3r\big)}{4\big((r^{2}-1)x+1\big)^{5/2}},

(B.41)

so we deduce then that:

\displaystyle-1=g(1)\leq g(x)\leq g(0)=1\ \forall x\in[0,1],

(B.42)

and that
g
is decreasing and concave.

Collecting the results we established above, we can now conclude the third and last step of the proof of Lemma
1
. We recall that we will conduct the proof under the assumption that
\theta_{0}\in[\frac{\pi}{2},\pi[
, without loss of generality.

First, we observe that the derivative of the scalar product with respect to the real variable
|u|^{2}
is:

\displaystyle\partial_{|u|^{2}}f=-\frac{(1+r)^{2}\cos\theta_{0}\sin\theta_{0}\,\text{sgn}(\sin\theta)\cos\theta}{2\sqrt{\Big[1-(1-r^{2})\cos^{2}\theta_{0}\Big]\cdot\Big[1-(1-r^{2})\cos^{2}\theta\Big]\cdot\big[1-|u|^{2}-\cos^{2}\theta\big]}}\cdotp

(B.43)

In particular, on the interval
[\frac{\pi}{2},\frac{3\pi}{2}[
, this derivative has the sign of
\cos\theta_{0}\sin\theta_{0}
on
[\frac{\pi}{2},\pi]
, and has the opposite sign on
[\pi,\frac{3\pi}{2}[
.

We observe also that in the case when
\cos\theta_{0}\sin\theta_{0}=0
, we have
f(|u|^{2},\theta)=f(0,\theta)
, and this case follows directly from the second step of the proof.

Assuming then
\cos\theta_{0}\sin\theta_{0}\neq 0
, and:

\displaystyle f(|u|^{2},\theta)\geq 1-\delta,

(B.44)

we consider three different cases.

First, let us assume that (
B.44
) holds for a certain
\theta\in[\frac{\pi}{2},\pi[
. Using the sign of the derivative
\partial_{|u|^{2}}f
, which is negative on
[\frac{\pi}{2},\pi[
, we have:

\displaystyle 1-\delta\leq f(|u|^{2},\theta)\leq f(0,\theta),

(B.45)

and using again the second step of the proof, we deduce that
\theta
belongs to an interval of measure smaller than
C(r)\sqrt{\delta}
.

We consider now a second case, when (
B.44
) holds for a certain
\theta\in[\partial\pi,\frac{3\pi}{2}]\subset\,]\pi,\frac{3\pi}{2}[
, and we assume also that:

\displaystyle|\cos\theta_{0}|>\frac{\sqrt{2}}{1+r}\sqrt{\delta}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{and}\hskip 14.22636pt|\sin\theta_{0}|>\frac{2r}{1+r}\sqrt{\delta}

(B.46)

for any
0<\delta
small enough, that is, smaller than
\delta_{0}=1
. Relying on the sign of
\partial_{|u|^{2}}f
, which is this time non-negative because
\theta\in[\pi,\frac{3\pi}{2}]
, we deduce that
f(|u|^{2},\theta)
is bounded from above by the corresponding expression of
f
that we can define choosing
|u|^{2}
maximal, which provides:

\displaystyle f(|u|^{2},\theta)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1-(1+r)\cos^{2}\theta_{0}-(1+r)\cos^{2}\theta+(1+r)^{2}\cos^{2}\theta_{0}\cos^{2}\theta}{\sqrt{\Big[1-(1-r^{2})\cos^{2}\theta_{0}\Big]\cdot\Big[1-(1-r^{2})\cos^{2}\theta\Big]}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1-(1+r)\cos^{2}\theta_{0}}{\sqrt{1-(1-r^{2})\cos^{2}\theta_{0}}}\cdot\frac{1-(1+r)\cos^{2}\theta}{\sqrt{1-(1-r^{2})\cos^{2}\theta}}\cdot

(B.47)

Therefore, since the function
g
takes values in
[-1,1]
, we have:

\displaystyle f(|u|^{2},\theta)=\big|f(|u|^{2},\theta)\big|\leq\left|\frac{1-(1+r)\cos^{2}\theta_{0}}{\sqrt{1-(1-r^{2})\cos^{2}\theta_{0}}}\right|.

(B.48)

In particular, if on the one hand
\cos^{2}\theta_{0}
is such that the right hand side in (
B.48
) is non-negative, using the convexity of the function
g
, we have:

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1-(1+r)\cos^{2}\theta_{0}}{\sqrt{1-(1-r^{2})\cos^{2}\theta_{0}}}\right|=\frac{1-(1+r)\cos^{2}\theta_{0}}{\sqrt{1-(1-r^{2})\cos^{2}\theta_{0}}}

\displaystyle\leq 1-\frac{(1+r)^{2}}{2}\cos^{2}\theta_{0}

\displaystyle<1-\delta

(B.49)

using in the last inequality the assumption (
B.46
) on the cosine of
\theta_{0}
. On the other hand, if
\cos^{2}\theta_{0}
is such that
g(\cos^{2}\theta_{0})
is negative, we have:

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1-(1+r)\cos^{2}\theta_{0}}{\sqrt{1-(1-r^{2})\cos^{2}\theta_{0}}}\right|

\displaystyle=-\frac{1-(1+r)\cos^{2}\theta_{0}}{\sqrt{1-(1-r^{2})\cos^{2}\theta_{0}}}

\displaystyle\leq 1+\frac{(1+r)^{2}}{4r^{2}}\big(\cos^{2}\theta_{0}-1\big)

\displaystyle\leq 1-\frac{(1+r)^{2}}{4r^{2}}\sin^{2}\theta_{0}

\displaystyle<1-\delta,

(B.50)

using in the first inequality that we have:

\displaystyle-g(x)\leq 1+\frac{(1+r)^{2}}{2r}(x-1)

(B.51)

for any
x\in[0,1]
such that
g(x)
is negative (because the line
y=-1+(1-x)\frac{(1+r)^{2}}{2r}
intersects the first axis at
x_{0}=\frac{1+r^{2}}{(1+r)^{2}}
, and we have
g(x_{0})=\frac{1-r}{\sqrt{1+r^{2}}}>0
), and using in the last inequality the assumption (
B.46
) on
\sin\theta_{0}
.
As a consequence, we see that, regardless the sign of
g(\cos^{2}\theta_{0})
, when (
B.46
) holds, there exists no angle
\theta\in[\pi,\frac{3\pi}{2}]
such that (
B.44
) can hold.

Turning now the last of the three cases we consider, we assume that (
B.44
) holds, together with the fact that:

\displaystyle|\cos\theta_{0}|\leq\frac{\sqrt{2}}{1+r}\sqrt{\delta}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{or}\hskip 14.22636pt|\sin\theta_{0}|\leq\frac{2r}{1+r}\sqrt{\delta}

(B.52)

for any
\delta\leq 1
. In this case, we see that if we assume that the angle
\theta
is such that:

\displaystyle\big|\cos\theta\big|>\frac{\sqrt{2}}{1+r}\sqrt{\delta}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{and}\hskip 14.22636pt\big|\sin\theta\big|>\frac{2r}{1+r}\sqrt{\delta},

(B.53)

that is, up to exclude two intervals in
\theta
of measure at most
C(r)\sqrt{\delta}
, we deduce using the same arguments as in the previous case that:

\displaystyle f(|u|^{2},\delta)\leq\left|\frac{1-(1+r)\cos^{2}\theta}{\sqrt{1-(1-r^{2})\cos^{2}\theta}}\right|<1-\delta.

(B.54)

As a consequence, in the case when (
B.52
) holds, (
B.44
) cannot hold, except if
\theta
belongs to one of two intervals of length at most
C(r)\sqrt{\delta}
.

The proof of Lemma
1
can now be concluded, since except if the angle
\theta
defined as
\cos(\theta)=\frac{v}{|v|}\cdot\omega
belongs to a finite family of intervals of length at most
C(r)\sqrt{\delta}
, we have
f(|u|^{2},\theta)<1-\delta
. This condition defines a subset of the unit sphere
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
of measure
C(d)\sqrt{\delta}
. The proof of Lemma
1
is now complete.
∎

It is also possible to describe in more details the behaviour of the function
f
defined by the expression (
B.39
). More precisely, we have the following result.

Proposition 9

(Behaviour of the scalar product (
B.1
) in the general case
|u|\neq 0
)
.

Without loss of generality, let us assume that
\theta_{0}\in[\frac{\pi}{2},\pi[
. If we assume in addition that
\cos\theta_{0}\sin\theta_{0}\neq 0
, then the scalar product (
B.1
), also describe by the expression (
B.39
), has a global minimum on the interval
[\partial\pi,\frac{3\pi}{2}]\subset[\pi,\frac{3\pi}{2}]
, with
\partial\pi
defined as
\cos^{2}(\partial\pi)=1-|u|^{2}
, and the function is decreasing between
\partial\pi
and the abscissa of its global minimum, and increasing between this abscissa and
\frac{3\pi}{2}
.

In addition we have:

\displaystyle f(\partial\pi)=\frac{1-(1+r)\cos^{2}\theta_{0}}{\sqrt{1-(1-r^{2})\cos^{2}\theta_{0}}}\cdot\frac{-r+(1+r)|u|^{2}}{\sqrt{1-(1-r^{2})\big(1-|u|^{2}\big)}}\hskip 14.22636pt\text{and}\hskip 14.22636ptf(\frac{3\pi}{2})=\frac{1-(1+r)\cos^{2}\theta_{0}}{\sqrt{1-(1-r^{2})\cos^{2}\theta_{0}}}\cdotp

(B.55)

Proof.

We start with considering the derivative of
f
with respect to
\theta
in the general case when
|u|\neq 0
. Without generality, we will focus on the particular case when
\theta_{0}\in[\frac{\pi}{2},\pi[
, and when
\theta\in]\pi,\frac{3\pi}{2}[
. In the present case, we have
\text{sgn}(\sin\theta)=-1
. All the other cases can be studied in the same way. A direct but tedious computation enables to obtain that the derivative of the expression (
B.39
) of
f
writes:

\displaystyle\partial_{\theta}f=\frac{(1+r)^{2}\sin\theta\Big(1+(r^{2}-1)\cos^{2}\theta_{0}\Big)}{\sqrt{1-|u|^{2}-\cos^{2}\theta}\Big(\big[1-(1-r^{2})\cos^{2}\theta_{0}\big]\cdot\big[1-(1-r^{2})\cos^{2}\theta\big]\Big)^{3/2}}F(|u|,\theta_{0},\theta)

(B.56)

with

\displaystyle F(|u|,\theta_{0},\theta)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{1-|u|^{2}-\cos^{2}\theta}\Big[\big(1-(1+r)\cos^{2}\theta_{0}\big)\cos\theta+\big(-1+r+(1-r^{2})\cos^{2}\theta_{0}\big)\cos^{3}\theta\Big]

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+(|u|^{2}-1)\cos\theta_{0}\sin\theta_{0}+2\cos\theta_{0}\sin\theta_{0}\cos^{2}\theta+(r^{2}-1)\cos\theta_{0}\sin\theta_{0}\cos^{4}\theta.

(B.57)

The zeros of the derivative
\partial_{\theta}f
correspond exactly to the zero of
F
. Although obtaining a simple expression of such zeros seems impossible, we observe that if
\theta
is a zero of
F
, then
X=\cos^{2}\theta
is a zero of the quartic polynomial
P
defined as:

\displaystyle P(X)=C^{2}\big[1-|u|^{2}-X\big]X\big(1+(r-1)X\big)^{2}-\cos^{2}\theta_{0}\sin^{2}\theta_{0}\Big(1-|u|^{2}-2X+(1-r^{2})X^{2}\Big)^{2},

(B.58)

with
C=1-(1+r)\cos^{2}\theta_{0}
. We will now estimate the maximal number of zeros of
P
in the interval
[0,1]
.

We observe first that the polynomial
P
satisfies the following properties:

\displaystyle P(X)\leq 0\hskip 14.22636pt\forall\,X\leq 0\hskip 14.22636pt\text{and}\hskip 14.22636pt\forall\,X\geq 1.

(B.59)

In addition, except if
|u|=0
, which corresponds to a set of angles
\omega
of zero Lebesgue measure, we have
P(X)<0\ \forall\,X\geq 1
. Similarly, we have always
P(X)<0\ \forall\,X<0
.

If we assume now that there are three distinct zeros or more to the polynomial
P
in
[0,1]
, because of the sign of
P
outside
[0,1]
we deduce that there are at least four distinct zeros, or if the number of zeros is exactly three, then at least one of them has to be a root of multiplicity at least
2
. As a consequence, using Rolle’s theorem, we deduce that there exist at least three zeros (counted with their multiplicity) of the derivative
P^{\prime}
of the polynomial
P
in the interval
[0,1]
.

Then, since:

\displaystyle P^{\prime}(X)

\displaystyle=-C^{2}X\big(1+(r-1)X\big)^{2}+C^{2}\big[1-|u|^{2}-X\big]\big(1-(r-1)X\big)^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+2C^{2}(r-1)\big[1-|u|^{2}-X\big]X\big(1+(r-1)X\big)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt-2\cos^{2}\theta_{0}\sin^{2}\theta_{0}\big(-2+2(1-r^{2})X\big)\Big(1-|u|^{2}-2X+(1-r^{2})X^{2}\Big),

(B.60)

we observe that:

\displaystyle P^{\prime}\big(\frac{1}{1-r}\big)

\displaystyle=4\cos^{2}\theta_{0}\sin^{2}\theta_{0}\Big(1-\frac{1-r^{2}}{1-r}\Big)\Big(1-|u|^{2}-\frac{2}{1-r}+\frac{1-r^{2}}{1-r}\Big)

\displaystyle=-4\cos^{2}\theta_{0}\sin^{2}\theta_{0}r\Big(-|u|^{2}-r\frac{(1+r)}{(1-r)}\Big)\geq 0.

(B.61)

Taking into account the limits of
P
as
X\rightarrow\pm\infty
, we deduce that there exists another root to the derivative
P^{\prime}
outside the interval
[0,1]
.

We obtain therefore a contradiction:
P
being a quartic polynomial,
P^{\prime}
is a cubic polynomial and has therefore at most three real roots counted with their multiplicity. We deduce then that
P
has at most two real roots in the interval
[0,1]
.

Back to the expression of
F
, we observe that when
\theta=\partial\pi
(where
\partial\pi
denotes the angle such that
1-|u|^{2}-\cos^{2}\theta=0
, which delimits the interval of definition of
F
in
[\pi,\frac{3\pi}{2}]
) we have:

\displaystyle F(|u|,\theta_{0},\partial\pi)=\cos\theta_{0}\sin\theta_{0}\big[|u|^{2}-1+2+r^{2}-1\big]\leq 0,

(B.62)

keeping in mind that
\theta_{0}\in[\frac{\pi}{2},\pi[
, so that
\sin\theta_{0}>0
and
\cos\theta_{0}\leq 0
. We have also:

\displaystyle F(|u|,\theta_{0},\frac{3\pi}{2})=\big(|u|^{2}-1\big)\cos\theta_{0}\sin\theta_{0}\geq 0.

(B.63)

In the case when
|u|\neq 1
and
\cos\theta_{0}\sin\theta_{0}\neq 0
we have even:

\displaystyle F(|u|,\theta_{0},\partial\pi)<0\hskip 14.22636pt\text{and}\hskip 14.22636ptF(|u|,\theta_{0},\frac{3\pi}{2})>0.

(B.64)

We prove now that if
F
has a double root, in the sense that
F
and its derivative
\partial_{\theta}F
vanish together, then so does
P
. Indeed, if
F
and
\partial_{\theta}F
vanish at the same value
\widetilde{\theta}
, there exists a differentiable function
G
such that:

\displaystyle F(\theta)=\big(\cos\theta-\cos\widetilde{\theta}\big)G\big(\cos\theta\big)\ \forall\,\theta,\hskip 14.22636pt\text{and}\hskip 14.22636ptG\big(\cos\widetilde{\theta}\big)=0.

(B.65)

F
has the form:

\displaystyle F(\theta)=\cos\theta R\big(\cos^{2}\theta\big)P_{1}\big(\cos^{2}\theta\big)+P_{2}\big(\cos^{2}\theta\big)

(B.66)

where
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
are two polynomials, and
R
is a function such that its square is a polynomial. By definition, the polynomial
P
introduced in (
B.58
) is equal to:

\displaystyle P(X)=XR^{2}(X)P_{1}^{2}(X)-P_{2}^{2}(X).

(B.67)

Therefore, if
F
and
\partial_{\theta}F
both vanish at
\widetilde{\theta}
, we have:

\displaystyle P(\cos^{2}\theta)

\displaystyle=F\big(\cos\theta\big)\Big(\cos\theta R\big(\cos^{2}\theta\big)P_{1}\big(\cos^{2}\theta\big)+P_{2}\big(\cos^{2}\theta\big)\Big)

\displaystyle=\big(\cos\theta-\cos\widetilde{\theta}\big)G\big(\cos\theta\big)\Big(\cos\theta R\big(\cos^{2}\theta\big)P_{1}\big(\cos^{2}\theta\big)+P_{2}\big(\cos^{2}\theta\big)\Big).

(B.68)

This last expression proves that
P
and its derivative also vanish at
\cos^{2}\widetilde{\theta}
, so that
P
would have a double root.

We are now in position to deduce that
F
has exactly one root in the case when
\cos\theta_{0}\sin\theta_{0}\neq 0
