. Let us assume that
F
has at least two distinct roots. First, if
F
has three distinct roots or more, we obtain a contradiction because we would deduce that
P
has three distinct roots or more, which is not possible.

If now we assume that
F
has exactly two distinct roots, necessarily at least one of these roots is a double root, because of the signs of
F
at the boundary of its interval of definition. We deduce then that
P
would have two distinct roots, one having a multiplicity at least two. However, in the case when
P
has at least two distinct roots (hence, exactly two), the respective multiplicities of these roots have to be equal to
1
. Indeed, if the two roots have both a respective multiplicity
k_{1},k_{2}
larger than one, the derivative of
P
would have two roots with respective multiplicity
(k_{1}-1)
and
(k_{2}-1)
. Applying Rolle’s theorem,
P^{\prime}
vanishes also at another point, between the two distinct roots of
P
. Using once again the fact that
P^{\prime}
vanishes at a certain point above
\frac{1}{1-r}
we obtain a contradiction, because the non-trivial cubic polynomial
P^{\prime}
would have at least four roots, counted with their multiplicities. Finally, if one root has multiplicity
1
, while the other has a multiplicity
k>1
, on the one hand we obtain the same contradiction by counting the roots if
k\geq 3
. On the other hand, if the multiplicity
k
of the second root is exactly
2
, we obtain a contradiction by sign considerations: the limits of
P
cannot be both
-\infty
in such a case.

Therefore, we have proved that
F
has exactly one root in the interval
[\pi,\frac{3\pi}{2}[
, provided that
\cos\theta_{0}\sin\theta_{0}\neq 0
. Relying on the signs of
F(|u|,\theta_{0},\partial\theta)
and
F(|u|,\theta_{0},\frac{3\pi}{2})
, we can deduce that there exists an angle
\theta_{\text{min}}
such that:

\displaystyle F(\theta)<0\ \forall\,\theta\in[\partial\pi,\theta_{\text{min}}[\hskip 14.22636pt\text{and}\hskip 14.22636ptF(\theta)>0\ \forall\,\theta\in\ ]\theta_{\text{min}},\frac{3\pi}{2}].

(B.69)

In conclusion, we proved that if
\cos\theta_{0}\sin\theta_{0}\neq 0
and
|u|\neq 0
, then:

\displaystyle f(|u|,\theta_{0},\theta)\leq\max\Big(f(|u|,\theta_{0},\partial\pi),f(|u|,\theta_{0},\frac{3\pi}{2})\Big).

(B.70)

We conclude now the proof of the proposition by evaluating the two boundaries values of the scalar product
f
, at
\theta=\partial\pi
and
\frac{3\pi}{2}
respectively. On the one hand we have, relying on the identity
\cos^{2}(\partial\pi)=1-|u|^{2}
:

\displaystyle f(\partial\pi)

\displaystyle=\frac{1-(1+r)\cos^{2}\theta_{0}-(1+r)\big(1-|u|^{2}\big)+(1+r)^{2}\cos^{2}\theta_{0}\big(1-|u|^{2}\big)}{\sqrt{\Big[1-(1-r^{2})\cos^{2}\theta_{0}\Big]\cdot\Big[1-(1-r^{2})\big(1-|u|^{2}\big)\Big]}}

\displaystyle=\frac{-r+r(1+r)\cos^{2}\theta_{0}+(1+r)\big[1-(1+r)\cos^{2}\theta_{0}\big]|u|^{2}}{\sqrt{\Big[1-(1-r^{2})\cos^{2}\theta_{0}\Big]\cdot\Big[1-(1-r^{2})\big(1-|u|^{2}\big)\Big]}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1-(1+r)\cos^{2}\theta_{0}}{\sqrt{1-(1-r^{2})\cos^{2}\theta_{0}}}\cdot\frac{-r+(1+r)|u|^{2}}{\sqrt{1-(1-r^{2})\big(1-|u|^{2}\big)}}\cdotp

(B.71)

On the other hand, we find:

\displaystyle f(\frac{3\pi}{2})=\frac{1-(1+r)\cos^{2}\theta_{0}}{\sqrt{1-(1-r^{2})\cos^{2}\theta_{0}}}\cdotp

(B.72)

The proof of Proposition
9
is complete.
∎

B.2
Estimate of the measure of the dynamical tube

Proof of Lemma
2
.

To prove Lemma
2
, we will decompose the set
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}
into the three following subsests, that are not disjoint. We define:

\displaystyle T_{1}

\displaystyle=\big([x_{1},x_{2}]+\overline{B(0,\varepsilon)}\big)\cap\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\ /\ (y-x_{2})\cdot(x_{2}-x_{1})\leq 0\}

\displaystyle=\big\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\ /\ \exists\ \lambda\in[0,1],\,z\in\overline{B(0,\varepsilon)}\ \text{such that}\ y=\lambda x_{1}+(1-\lambda)x_{2}+z,\ \text{and}(y-x_{2})\cdot(x_{2}-x_{1})\leq 0\big\}.

(B.73)

T_{1}
is the “first part” of the tube
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}
. It is constituted of a portion of a cylinder, together with a half sphere centered on
x_{1}
. Similarly, we introduce:

\displaystyle T_{2}

\displaystyle=\big([x_{2},x_{3}]+\overline{B(0,\varepsilon)}\big)\cap\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\ /\ (y-x_{2})\cdot(x_{2}-x_{3})\leq 0\}

\displaystyle=\big\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\ /\ \exists\ \lambda\in[0,1],\,z\in\overline{B(0,\varepsilon)}\ \text{such that}\ y=\lambda x_{2}+(1-\lambda)x_{3}+z,\ \text{and}(y-x_{2})\cdot(x_{2}-x_{3})\leq 0\big\},

(B.74)

and we denote finally by
B
the ball:

\displaystyle B=\overline{B(x_{2},\varepsilon)}.

(B.75)

We have
\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}=T_{1}\cup B\cup T_{2}
, where the three subsets
T_{1}
,
B
,
T_{2}
intersect each other pairwise.

Relying the inclusion-exclusion formula, we have:

\displaystyle\big|\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}\big|=|T_{1}|+|T_{2}|+|B|-|T_{1}\cap B|-|T_{2}\cap B|-|T_{1}\cap T_{2}|+|T_{1}\cap T_{2}\cap B|.

(B.76)

The objective is to prove that
\big|\mathcal{T}_{\varepsilon}\big|\leq|T_{1}|+|T_{2}|
, which would conclude the proof of the lemma. Since by the inclusion-exclusion formula we have also:

\displaystyle\big|B\cap(T_{1}\cup T_{2})\big|=\big|\big(B\cap T_{1}\big)\cup\big(B\cap T_{2}\big)\big|=|B\cap T_{1}|+|B\cap T_{2}|-|B\cap T_{1}\cap T_{2}|,

(B.77)

we deduce:

\displaystyle|B|-|T_{1}\cap B|-|T_{2}\cap B|-|T_{1}\cap T_{2}|+|T_{1}\cap T_{2}\cap B|

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt=|B|-\big|B\cap\big(T_{1}\cup T_{2}\big)\big|-|T_{1}\cap T_{2}|.

(B.78)

Finally, decomposing
|B|=\big|B\cap\big(T_{1}\cup T_{2}\big)\big|+\big|B\cap T_{1}^{c}\cap T_{2}^{c}\big|
and observing by symmetry that
\big|B\cap T_{1}^{c}\cap T_{2}^{c}\big|=\big|B\cap T_{1}\cap T_{2}\big|
(in this last step we used the fact
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
both contain a half ball centered on
x_{2}
), we conclude:

\displaystyle|B|-|T_{1}\cap B|-|T_{2}\cap B|-|T_{1}\cap T_{2}|+|T_{1}\cap T_{2}\cap B|\leq 0.

(B.79)

concluding the proof of Lemma
2
.
∎
